THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD, and its utility for the discovery of water sources, mines, hidden treasures, thieves & fugitive murderers. With Principles explaining the most obscure phenomena of Nature, by M. L. L. De Vallemont, Priest & Doctor of Theology. PARIS, at Jean Anisson, Director of the Royal Press, rue Saint-Jacques, at the Sign of the Florentine Fleur-de-lis. 1693. WITH THE PRIVILEGE OF THE KING.

---

DEDICATORY EPISTLE

TO MONSIEUR POLLART, COUNSELOR TO THE KING in the Parliament of Paris.

MONSIEUR, Even if serving you didn't already make it my duty to present this work to you, I would have chosen you as its protector anyway, out of both affection and good sense. For years now you have shown me so much kindness, and I feel so indebted for it, that I want to express my gratitude at every opportunity. Beyond that, I'd like to prepare the public to receive the Occult Physics favorably, and the best way I can do that, MONSIEUR, is to make it known that you take an interest in it. You are recognized as a magistrate whose every judgment rests on truth and justice, so when people see your name at the front of this book, they will look more kindly on the case I argue in it — because they know you can't be swayed by favoritism, and that nothing on earth could make you stray from strict fairness. That reputation, so fine and so well established, has taught the world to receive your opinions like oracles; even the people you rule against can't help joining the crowd that sings your praises. And among everyone paying this well-earned tribute to your virtue and merit, I'll venture to say that some may equal me, but no one outdoes me in the zeal with which I do it — since I am, with deep respect and unshakable attachment, MONSIEUR, your most humble and most obedient servant. P. L. L. De Vallemont, Priest.

---

PREFACE

Since people first prided themselves on doing philosophy, no subject more curious or more important has ever been examined than the one treated in this book. I'll go further: if we could once clearly explain what makes the Divining Rod move over water sources, mines, buried treasure, and the trails of fleeing criminals, nothing in nature would stay hidden for long. If we could understand how the streams of tiny particles given off by underground water, by metals, and by certain human bodies work their way — through the breathing we never notice — into the pores of another person, we would soon understand why contagious and epidemic diseases strike some people and spare others. We would have found the invisible channel by which harmful matter leaves one body through the skin and gets drawn into another through the breath. And once that channel was understood, medicine could easily find ways to protect or cure people from the many diseases that spread through contagious particles in the air. That seems to me a matter of the highest importance. And think how useful the Rod would be for finding water sources — which we can't live without — and for locating the most precious metals, which today hold human society together. The sensation that the story of the Peasant of Dauphiné caused around the world, and how eagerly everyone wanted to hear about it, show better than anything I could say how much the public wants this astonishing corner of physics explained.

I know perfectly well that certain suspicious scholars will dismiss anything good said about the Rod's movement and go on treating it as beneath their attention. Let them think what they like; I can point to other learned men who didn't consider it a waste of their time. The records of the Royal Society of England show that this distinguished body planned to investigate everything about the Divining Rod's use in prospecting for mines. In fact, among the hundred questions M. Boyle drew up on the subject of mines, the eighteenth lays out exactly the research he wanted done on the Rod: "Utrum Virgula Divinatoria adhibetur ad investigationem venarum propositarum fodinarum: & si fa, quo id fiat successu? art. 18." [Whether the Divining Rod is used in the investigation of proposed mineral veins, and if so, with what success? Article 18.] — as reported in the Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of England, November 1666, p. 344.

So there are people who haven't sneered at the subject. More honest than the scholars I just described, they admit the phenomena of the Divining Rod are remarkable and well worth the attention of the wisest minds. But some of them, giving in to irrational fears, imagine the Rod has no movement except what the devil gives it. They can't believe anything in nature could be beyond their understanding: whatever they can't grasp can't be natural. That's exactly the attitude that filled the world with so many crude and ridiculous fables about sorcerers. A few hundred years ago, anyone who knew a little Greek or Hebrew was taken for a magician. Many times, ignorant people have mistaken mathematical diagrams for magical symbols. Jean Schiphower, of the order of Hermits of Saint Augustine of the Convent of Osnabrück in the County of Edinbourg, writing about printing around the year 1440, says that in its earliest days the superstitious and ignorant treated it as an art that might involve the most criminal kind of magic. There isn't a stage magician whose tricks don't pass for sorcery with much of the public. The same mentality is at work today when people accuse the Rod's operations of being magic, simply because the cause isn't known. Van Helmont rightly observed that you can't overstate the damage these prejudices do to the sciences, and especially to physics. Is there anything more astonishing and depressing, he asks, than watching the lowly mechanical trades improve every day while physics stays stuck in nearly the same place? Nothing slows the progress of natural science like the shouting and unjust censure of the ignorant — it frightens, stops, and even drives back the very people whose open minds and long study could have helped perfect the field. "Quod dolendum summopere atque admirandum magi artes mechanicas proficere quotidie, solum vero naturalium studium censuris iniquis terreri, & retroire." [Van Helmont, De cura Magnet. Vulner. n. 36: It is most to be lamented and wondered at that the mechanical arts advance daily, while the study of natural things alone is frightened and driven back by unjust censure.]

I declare that I haven't been scared off by that bogeyman. We live in an enlightened age, and we can expect more fairness from it than from times when ignorance and barbarism cast such thick darkness. My main goal has been to show that beyond the practical uses of the Rod, these new phenomena can shed real light on physics and medicine. The public will judge whether my efforts amount to anything. Readers will find that this subject, obscure as it is in itself, is brought to life by beautiful and curious experiments, which I've put within everyone's reach, and which are perfectly suited to getting the mind used to the idea that nature uses invisible agents when it performs its greatest marvels. That is what I call the Occult Physics — to distinguish it from what nature does in the open, by causes we can see.

To explain the occult physics of the Divining Rod, I decided I should prefer the Philosophy of Corpuscles — the philosophy of tiny particles — over every other system. Not only is it the only one capable of usefully untangling nature's secrets; it's actually older than any other system whose record has come down to us. Before Leucippus, the teacher of Democritus — whom Minucius Felix names as the first to use atoms in philosophy — a certain Moschus, a native of Phoenicia, was already explaining the phenomena of nature by corpuscles, that is, by particles, by imperceptibly small parts of matter. Strabo, who records this, adds that Moschus lived before the Trojan War, and so several centuries before any Greek philosopher appeared in the world. That is the ancient pedigree of the corpuscular philosophy; and since it's Phoenician, there's every reason to think it was the philosophy of the Hebrews, from whom it passed to the Greeks. No one in modern times has worked the corpuscular philosophy as well as M. Boyle, as the many fine passages from his observations cited throughout this treatise will show.

A physicist who philosophizes without doing experiments, Father Kircher the Jesuit used to say, is like a blind man foolish enough to argue about colors. "In physicis rebus sine experimento philosophari, idem est, ac si caecum de colore judicium ferre insipienter praesumeret." [Mund. Subterr. Bk. 10, ch. 1: To philosophize about physical things without experiment is no different from a blind man foolishly presuming to pass judgment on color.] Something would always have been missing from my work if I hadn't observed Jaques Aymar in person — I could have been accused of reasoning only from accounts that not everyone finds credible. At last this famous man came to Paris on January 3, 1693, at the command of a great Prince. I saw him two or three hours a day for nearly a month, and you may be sure I examined him as thoroughly as I should have. It is certain that the Divining Rod turns in his hands over water, over metals, and over the trails of thieves and fleeing murderers. He doesn't know why. If he knew the physical cause, and had the wit to reason about it, I can assure you he would never fail in any attempt. But a peasant who can neither read nor write hardly knows what is meant by an atmosphere, a volume, streams of particles spread through the air. Still less does he understand how those particles can be disturbed so that they stop producing the Rod's movement. Nor can he appreciate how much his success depends on his own body being in the right state of sensitivity — so that the particles given off by whatever the Rod bends toward can actually register on him. It takes almost nothing to upset the chain of natural causes and ruin an experiment.

So however simple and honest a man Jaques Aymar may be, he can take on tasks he won't always carry off — because he doesn't know that he has to be in a particular condition of sensitivity at that moment for the particles in the air to produce any sensation in him, and that this rare condition can be wrecked by a jolt of fear or any other sudden, violent emotion. He can't sort all this out, but he does recognize that he can be mistaken, and that he can't know for certain, every time his Rod turns, whether it's water, metal, or a corpse underneath — because it moves over anything that gives off matter abundantly. When he asserts that it's a murderer he's following, it's because he recognizes that the sensation he felt at the crime scene is the same one that continues along the road and keeps agitating him. That's his test.

So if Jaques Aymar sometimes attempts things that fail, no one should be surprised — provided they have an accurate idea of how nature works and have studied physics through experiment. They'll know that nature's machinery demands such precise proportions in the arrangement, the force, and the motion of causes that the slightest obstacle wrecks the result. Don't even the best hunting dogs sometimes lose the scent? Why demand that Aymar always be equally sensitive to impressions in the air? For anyone who insists he should succeed every time, the cure is to look at the dip of the magnetized iron needle, which I use to explain the dip of the Divining Rod. They'll find that the procedure for measuring this dip demands such painstaking precision that ordinarily, out of twenty trials, you won't find four that come out exactly alike. So common sense says the failed trials shouldn't count against the consistent ones.

I don't deny there are frauds who exploit people's credulity and stretch the Rod's use into far too many things — like those quacks who actually have one good specific remedy and then ruin its reputation themselves by trying to pass it off as a cure-all. I'll add that there are people whose sharper, more delicate sensitivity gives them the ability to find springs, mines, hidden treasures, thieves, and fleeing murderers even more abundantly than Aymar. Word already comes from Lyon of an eighteen-year-old who considerably outdoes Jaques Aymar in this gift. And anyone in Paris can see, at the home of M. Geoffroy, former Alderman of that city, a young man who finds gold hidden in the ground by the violent emotion he feels the moment he walks over it.

Chap. I. There is such a thing as a Divining Rod: what it is, and how it is used — History of the golden tooth — Several doubt the Divining Rod — Four rules for telling truth from falsehood in matters of fact — Various names given to the Rod — Varro & Cicero spoke of the Rod — First manner of holding the Rod — Second manner — Third manner — Fourth manner.

Chap. II. The surprising story of a peasant who, guided by the Divining Rod, tracked a murderer more than 45 leagues over land and more than 30 leagues by sea — Aymar goes to Beaucaire, where he finds a murderer — The Hunchback confesses his crime — Letters written to M. l'Abbé Bignon — Aymar returns to Beaucaire & goes by sea within sight of Genoa — Example of the Royal Society of England regarding belief in matters of fact.

Chap. III. Nature has only one mechanism in all its operations, & the Philosophy of Corpuscles is the only one that can explain the marvels of sympathy & the movement of the Divining Rod — Nature acts by the simplest means — Occult qualities of the schools — Mathematical contact — Physical contact — Sympathy — Why the wounds of a murdered man reopen in the presence of the murderer — Why the rooster crows at dawn — Why the heliotrope follows the sun — Difference between magnetic bodies & electric bodies — One must sometimes refresh the air in a sick person's room.

Chap. IV. We know enough about the nature of corpuscles to use them to explain the phenomena of the Divining Rod — Exhalations, vapors — Properties of streams of subtle matter — Experiments — Usefulness of the microscope — Touch sometimes discovers what the eyes cannot — Different kinds of corpuscles — Emanations sometimes produce the same effect as the body they come from.

Chap. V. A system of the movement and dip of the Divining Rod over springs, mines, treasures, and the trail of thieves and fleeing murderers — The dip of the Rod is the same as the dip of the compass needle — Dip of the magnet — Experiment — Subtle matter is nature's active agent.

Chap. VI. Vapors rise over underground watercourses and make the Divining Rod dip — Several different opinions on the origin of springs — Aristotle's view — M. Mariotte's — Another opinion — Father Cassini's view — Vapors rise over underground waters — How vapors enter the Rod and make it dip.

Chap. VII. Exhalations or fumes rise over every kind of mine & over treasures hidden in the earth, making the Divining Rod dip — Underground fires — The central fire — Underground fires push fumes & exhalations into the air — The sign soldiers follow to find hidden treasure — Why the Rod dips so forcefully over metals.

Chap. VIII. Many particles escape through the unnoticed perspiration of the bodies of thieves & fleeing murderers; they linger on their trail & make the Rod dip — Bodies are porous — Metals have pores — Plants have pores — Animals perspire — Sanctorius's aphorisms on perspiration — M. Cufac applies Sanctorius's aphorisms to medicine — The cloud of particles a criminal's body gives off — The rosemary branch that grew in a dead man's hands.

Chap. IX. The particles given off by the Lyon murderers' unnoticed perspiration, spread through the air, could easily have entered the man with the Rod the same way — How this observation could improve medicine — Magnetic cures — Bodies breathe without our noticing — Physicians should promote sweating rather than bleeding — Effects of contagion on Jaques Aymar — The sense of touch is as delicate as the other senses — On the Transplantation of diseases — On the Powder of Sympathy — The weapon-salve, Unguentum armarium.

Chap. X. The particles of vapors, exhalations, & unnoticed perspiration are fine enough to work their way into the Rod — The astonishing divisibility of matter shown by cochineal — The subtlety of vapors demonstrated by quicksilver — Sympathetic ink — Venomous animals showing how strangely fine emanations can be — Experiments demonstrating the fineness of corpuscles.

Chap. XI. The particles of vapors, exhalations, & unnoticed perspiration have enough force to move the Divining Rod & to produce in Jaques Aymar the symptoms described — The powerful action of vapors — Hygrometers — The barometer — Observations on the barometer — The weather-man, or Physical Prophet, who announces changes in the weather — The thermometer — Fulminating gold — Fulminating powder — The force of seepage — The mechanics of animals.

Chap. XII. The particles of vapors, exhalations, & unnoticed perspiration that move the Divining Rod do not mix easily in the air — A vial representing the elementary world — Wine filtering through water — Experiment with tobacco smoke — The magic lantern — Experiments with rays of light — The camera obscura — A burning mirror made from a block of ice.

Chap. XIII. Why the Divining Rod doesn't turn in everyone's hands — What good is the Rod if the power comes from the person holding it — What temperament you need to have Jaques Aymar's ability — Aymar's agitation — Why the Rod sometimes fails in the hands of someone who has often used it successfully — Some people see & hear farther than others — What the Rod actually does.

Chap. XIV. Of all the ways of finding underground watercourses, the Divining Rod is the best.

Chap. XV. Of all the methods of finding mines, the Divining Rod is the best — France has many very rich mines — Different Rods for different metals — Three fine experiments in favor of the Rod — Methods of prospecting for mines — The greatness of France — Catalogue of more than one hundred and fifty mines in France — Seven Rods for the seven metals — Three experiments.

The dipping of the Divining Rod over water, metals, & the footsteps of criminals does not come from the devil — This divination has no connection with Rhabdomancy — The Germans used the Rod before 1630 — Some impious people have corrupted the legitimate use of the Rod — M. Van Dale refuted — Sorcerers do exist.

========================================


THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER ONE

There is such a thing as a Divining Rod: what it is, and how it is used.

For more than two hundred years, mining men have used a hazelwood rod to find gold and silver deposits, and for a century, well-finders have used it to locate water — yet no one had noticed it might have other uses. Now we've just learned that a peasant from Dauphiné uses it to follow the trail of thieves, and to test whether nature can really perform certain supposed miracles that time later proves to be fables. Such fables have badly discredited natural science and convinced people that it does nothing but chase visions and fantasies. So you have to verify the fact before you work at explaining it — at least if you want to do philosophy properly.

I. People argued for ages about how the remora fish could stop a ship dead, even under full sail; today we say the story is a fable, and that the fish found stuck by chance to the prow of the stalled ship wasn't the cause at all — more likely it was caverns in the seafloor into which the water plunges, briefly holding the ships passing overhead. The naturalists agonized over why the plant called Lunaria major supposedly pulls the shoe off a horse that steps on it, as Dioscorides reports. Today that's treated as a story told for fun. Suppose the plant's leaves did stick firmly to a horseshoe: all that could happen is that the nails holding the shoe, being stronger than the plant's stem, would snap the plant or tear it out of the ground. Pliny, and several gullible writers who copied him, tell so many tales like this that ancient physics is now in universal disrepute — even among those who claim to prefer the old errors to newly discovered truths.

But nothing on this subject is more entertaining than what happened at the end of the last century, when a boy toured several towns showing off a golden tooth he claimed had grown in his mouth. In 1595, around Easter, the rumor spread that in the village of Weildorf in Silesia, in Bohemia, there was a seven-year-old whose teeth had fallen out, and that in place of his last molar a gold one had grown in. No story ever made more noise. The scholars got involved. Physicians and philosophers immediately took the field to examine the case and rule on it, as a matter within their expertise. One of the first to distinguish himself was Jacobus Horstius, professor of medicine at the University of Helmstadt. In a printed pamphlet, this physician argued that the golden tooth was partly a work of nature and partly a prodigy, and that however you looked at it, it was clearly a consolation heaven was sending the Christians of Bohemia, on whom the Turks were then inflicting the worst cruelties. At the same time Martinus Rulandus also published an account of the golden tooth; two years later Johannes Ingolstetterus refuted it, and Rulandus, undaunted, defended his work that same year, 1597, against the attack. Andreas Libavius entered the fray and published a book summarizing everything said for and against the golden tooth — which was generating real quarrels, and which turned out to be nothing but a rather crude hoax. The child was taken to Breslau, where everyone rushed to see this marvelous novelty. He was produced before an assembly of doctors, all very keen to examine the famous tooth — among them Christophorus Rhumbaumius, professor of medicine, a man who wanted to see before believing. First a goldsmith, wanting to confirm it was gold, rubbed his touchstone against it; to the eye, the streak on the stone looked like genuine gold, but when aqua fortis was applied, the streak vanished — exposing part of the fraud. Rhumbaumius, a sharp and skillful man, examined the tooth still more closely and spotted a small hole at the top. Inserting an iron probe, he found it was a sheet of copper, perhaps gilded; and he would easily have pried the sheet off if the con man who was parading the child from town to town hadn't stopped him — loudly protesting the wrong being done him, by ruining his chance to fleece the curious and the gullible. The con man and the child vanished, and to this day nobody really knows what became of them.

But just because scholars have sometimes been duped doesn't make it reasonable to doubt everything forever. It would be plainly unjust to believe no one merely because you know some people enjoy peddling fables. So even though the golden-tooth story is false, you shouldn't reject — out of sheer contrariness — the story of the hazel rod, which has become so famous since the events at Lyon last July. Believing improbable things too easily is a blameworthy credulity: it puts you in danger of swallowing falsehood as readily as truth, of acting at random rather than like a thinking person. But refusing to believe what carries every mark of evidence is a hypochondriac's skepticism, a degree of folly not far from that of the man who couldn't be persuaded he had a head — and who was only cured of it by the weight of a lead cap, whose discomfort soon brought him back to his senses.

The Divining Rod has long been used to find springs, mines, and hidden treasure; recently it has been used to track murderers; and all this is a matter of public record carrying every mark of evidence. Yet plenty of people still call it into doubt. Some of them are even counted among the learned, among the interpreters of nature's deepest secrets — and yet they set themselves, in a civilized state, under laws that forbid such rebellion, against the reports of magistrates, against the official records of judges, against the explanations of curious and learned men, and finally against the testimony of countless eyewitnesses of excellent sense and exacting judgment. Isn't it also rather conceited to deny a fact just because you don't believe it possible? How, asked Van Helmont on a similar occasion, will these people excuse their pride and arrogance — measuring God's omnipotence by the reach of their own minds, and denying any fact they can't conceive? Who appointed them to judge everyone else by themselves, and to rule that whatever they don't understand, nobody will? "Omnium animos ex suo aestimat, qui putat fieri non posse, quod intelligere non potest." [De Curat. Magnet. Vulner.: He measures all minds by his own, who thinks that what he cannot understand cannot be done.]

To these freethinkers who hide their ignorance and pride behind their skepticism, one can repeat what Father Schott the Jesuit answered certain people who denied that the hazel rod indicated water and metals. There's no quibbling about it: this rod certainly does turn over metal veins, over springs, and over treasure buried in the earth. The fact is settled. The only difficulty is to know whether the effect is natural, or whether it works with the devil's help: "Dubium ergo nullum est, quin dicta virgula effectum praestet in venis metallicis detegendis, & in pecuniis ac thesauris reperiendis. Controversia solum est..." [Thaumaturg. Physic. bk. 4, ch. 1, p. 422: There is therefore no doubt that the said rod produces its effect in detecting metallic veins, and in finding money and treasures. The only controversy is...]

Still, you can't demand that a person believe without knowing why. In fact, you should approve of someone bringing extra scrutiny and caution to a case this surprising and apparently brand-new. But that person should also use the rules we have for handling such situations. The late M. de Launoy, Doctor of Navarre, famous for his critical works on several points of church history, gives four rules for telling truth from falsehood in matters of fact. 1. Believe contemporary authors, when they are honest and not contradicted by witnesses of their own time. 2. Defer to those who were closest to where the thing happened. 3. The fact must not offend reason — but reason that is well informed. 4. Distrust a fact that is reported in conflicting versions, where the witnesses disagree on several points. Apply these four excellent tests to the story of the peasant with the rod, and you'll see why it can't be doubted — if you mean to be guided by reason, which teaches us that water is purest the closer to the spring you draw it, in the words of the poet: "Purius ex ipso fonte bibuntur aquae." [Waters are drunk more purely from the spring itself.]

After all, you would have to be quite a stranger in France, and in books too, never to have heard of the Divining Rod. I can truthfully say that purely by chance, in Paris and in various provinces of the kingdom, I have known more than fifty people who used this simple instrument to find water, mines, and hidden treasure, and in whose hands it genuinely turned. "It is more reasonable," says Father Malebranche, "to believe one man who says: I have seen, than a million others who talk into the air." [Recherche de la vérité, bk. 2, ch. 3.]

II. This Divining Rod has been given many names. It has been called the Caduceus, the Divine Wand, the Divine Rod, the Divining Rod, the Rod of Aaron, the Staff of Jacob. And those most struck by the usefulness of this admirable invention haven't failed to dress it up with other ringing titles — the Shining Rod, the Burning Rod, the Leaping Rod, the Transcendent Rod, the Trembling Rod, the Falling Rod, the Superior Rod — names given it by the Italians who work the mines of Trent and Tyrol; Basil Valentine wrote a kind of commentary on these seven names, in seven chapters of the second book of his Testament. Others have celebrated it with grand comparisons. One says it is the rod Moses used to bring water out of the rock. Others compare it to the scepter of Ahasuerus, King of the Persians and Medes, whose tip Esther had no sooner kissed than she obtained everything she asked. Some even apply to it the words of Psalm 22: "Thy rod and thy staff have comforted me." That's human nature: people know no restraint once they're enamored of something. We naturally condemn these extravagant flourishes and these profane applications of the Word of God — so visibly a criminal abuse of it. In holy Scripture, we should meditate on what doesn't surpass our understanding and adore what we don't understand; that's the use the holy Fathers prescribe. But no one will much object to comparing this rod to the wand of Pallas, which according to Homer let the goddess make Ulysses young again and then strip away the youth she had given him [Odyss. 13 & 16]. Or call it, if you like, the Caduceus of Mercury, which according to Virgil opens and shuts the underworld, commands the winds, and served him as a scepter [Aeneid bk. 4]. We won't fight all these grand metaphors, which smack a little of the temperament of the Chymists, for whom no language is too strong to express the excellence of their Philosopher's Stone.

It would be hard to pin down exactly when people began using the Divining Rod. I've found nothing about it in authors before the middle of the fifteenth century — it's discussed at length in the Testament of Basil Valentine, a Benedictine monk active around 1490. He does, though, speak of it in a way that suggests the practice was known before his time. Could we dare claim the Divine Rod was known and used nearly two thousand years ago? I have a conjecture on this point that has not seemed trivial to people who know how to weigh evidence. What likelihood is there of dismissing what Cicero says at the end of the first book of his Offices? Urging his son Marcus to take up all the duties of society, he warns him against the illusion of people who say you should deal with the public only as much as you need it — and that if a man had found, as the saying goes, by the Divine Rod the means of feeding and clothing himself, he should withdraw from public affairs and give all his time to study: "Quod si omnia nobis, quae ad victum, cultumque pertinent, quasi Virgula Divina, ut aiunt, suppeditarentur..." [But if everything pertaining to our sustenance and comfort were supplied to us, as they say, as if by the Divine Rod...] What makes me think Cicero is alluding to the divine rod of hazel is that he's talking about a fortune made all at once, with hardly any labor — like the fortune of a man who has found a treasure. Add that the commentators and translators of this passage of Cicero go silent right there; and it's hard to believe so great a writer would have used a flat proverb with no salt, no point, no flavor at all — which is what Erasmus and Cicero's other interpreters attribute to him. Varro, the most learned of the Romans, wrote a satire titled Virgula Divina, as Vetranius Maurus tells us in his catalogue of Varro's works; and we find this satire frequently cited by Nonius Marcellus in his book De Proprietate sermonum. But what would clinch my belief that Cicero had the hazel rod in mind, and that it was known in his day, is that at the end of his first book on Divination he quotes verses of Ennius, in which the poet mocks certain people who claimed to teach where treasures lie — for the price of a drachma — and tells them: I'll give it gladly, but you can take it out of the treasures we find by your help. "Quibus divitias pollicentur, ab iis Drachmam ipsi petunt. De his divitiis deducam Drachmam, reddam cetera." [To those to whom they promise riches, they themselves ask a drachma. From these riches I shall deduct the drachma, and return the rest.] That's the portrait of the swindlers who still run the same game today — roaming the country châteaux with their Divining Rod, and on the strength of the treasures they promise to find in the cellars, always collecting good room and board in advance. It's an excellent retort, and you'd be right to use it on those pushy charcoal-burners who promise mountains of gold to anyone they can talk into trading real money for the ashes at the bottom of crucibles where the Philosopher's Stone has been hunted for so long.

III. Before describing the different ways of using the Divining Rod, note that any kind of wood will do, though porous, lighter wood works much better. Jaques Aymar — the peasant of Saint-Véran near Saint-Marcellin in Dauphiné, who became famous when he used this rod to expose a notorious murderer he then followed for more than 45 leagues, guided by this simple instrument — uses whatever wood comes to hand, for water, for metals, and for hidden things. Some use wild pine for lead; and to find gold, iron points have to be fixed to the end of the rod. Some insist it be cut at the full moon, but honestly that's a pointless observance — like the ones mentioned by Georgius Agricola, who says the Germans first enchant the rod with verses they recite; like the other absurd ceremonies recorded by Jean Belot, Curé of Milmonts, a man besotted with cabalistic superstitions and childishness if ever there was one. In fact, someone has just handed me the so-called blessing of the rod: supposedly it must be cut with a single stroke on a Wednesday, at the planetary hour of Mercury, then inscribed with certain symbols, while a little prayer is recited — prayers are never lacking in piety on these occasions: "Virga avellana debet uno ictu incidi, die mercurii, hora..." etc. Leave such practices, unworthy of a sensible person, to people without learning and without religion. I have no doubt that frauds and charlatans for whom the rod turned have sometimes wrapped this natural gift in extravagant ceremonies, to hide their secret and raise its price — just as I described in my Treatise on the Magnet, regarding those who pierce a chicken's head without killing it. There I showed the trick is the simplest thing in the world: you push a probe through the middle of the chicken's head at a point where, the cerebellum being unharmed, the bird stays pinned to a table and doesn't die afterward, provided it's not held there more than a quarter of an hour. I also noted that the mountebanks, to make the trick look harder, accompany it with barbarous words that serve no purpose whatsoever.

FIRST MANNER OF HOLDING THE DIVINING ROD

The most common way to use the Divining Rod is to take a forked branch of hazel — also called the filbert tree — a foot and a half long, about as thick as a finger, and no more than a year's growth if possible. You hold the two branches A and B in your two hands, without gripping hard, with the backs of your hands turned toward the ground, the point C of the rod facing forward, and the rod parallel to the horizon. Then you walk slowly through the places where you suspect there is water, ore, or hidden silver. Don't move abruptly: you would break up the column of vapors and exhalations rising from the spot where these things lie — the very vapors that soak into the rod and make it dip.

SECOND MANNER OF HOLDING THE DIVINING ROD

Some hold the rod differently. The sieur Royer's method is to carry it balanced on the back of the hand. Here is how he describes it: "To find water, take a forked branch — hazel, alder, oak, or apple — about a foot long and as thick as a finger, so the wind can't easily stir it. Set it on one hand in equilibrium, balanced as evenly as possible; then walk slowly; and when you pass over a watercourse, it will turn."

THIRD MANNER OF HOLDING THE DIVINING ROD

Father Kircher the Jesuit says he saw this divination practiced in Germany in a completely different way. You take a hazel shoot, perfectly straight and free of knots, and cut it into two halves of nearly equal length. You hollow the end of one into a little cup and cut the end of the other to a point, so the tip of one stick fits into the tip of the other. You carry this assembly in front of you, held between your two index fingers, as the figure shows. When you pass over watercourses or metal veins, the two sticks move and dip.

FOURTH MANNER OF HOLDING THE DIVINING ROD

There is one more way, which I've seen used only by a few people who hunt for water professionally. They take a long shoot of hazel or any other wood, smooth and straight like an ordinary cane; they hold its two ends in their hands and bend it into a slight arc; they carry it parallel to the horizon; and the moment they pass over a spring, the stick turns and the arc bends toward the earth.

Not only is it certain that not everyone has this gift of making the Divining Rod dip over water, metals, hidden things, and criminals — the gift itself suffers fainting fits, so to speak. I've seen from experience that the same person for whom it had turned several times lost the ability entirely. This has been noticed before, as you can see in Father Schott the Jesuit: "Non omnes cum virgula loqui possunt, nec eidem personae semper percutit." [Magia Sympath. bk. 4, part 4, Syntag. 4, ch. 10, p. 426: Not all can speak with the rod, nor does it always strike for the same person.] It's also certain that the effect comes entirely from the person. If it came from the rod, then nothing is surer than this: suspend it on a pivot like a compass needle and it should still dip over water or metal. Yet that never happens, as I've verified by experiment, following Father Schott [p. 426 of Mag. Sympath.]. I conclude the effect doesn't come from any power residing in the rod.

In all likelihood, the Divining Rod wasn't originally used for everything it's used for today. Nor does anyone know who first hit on this admirable invention. Some believe it was Paracelsus — convinced, as Aldrovandus says [bk. 1, §, ratio metall. inven. p. 20], that metals have a sympathy with certain trees — who introduced the practice among mine workers. But judging from Paracelsus's own works, far from promoting or crediting the Divining Rod, he speaks of it in several places as something he never recommends and in fact consistently condemns, because, he says, there is nothing reliable in its use: "Virgula Divinatoria fallax est, saepius etiam in nummulum unum perditum intendens." [Paracels. de Philosophia occult. p. 480: The Divining Rod is deceptive, often pointing even at a single lost coin.] In that same passage Paracelsus says that after digging where the rod had turned, often no treasure was found — though it sometimes dips over a small coin — and that this might be the work of the Sylphs and Gnomes, taking possession of the treasures and moving them so they won't be taken away. After which this patriarch of the Chymists says, in great ignorance, that in such cases one should perform exorcisms — and on that advice, impious men have often been driven to profane the holiest things in order to find their imaginary treasures and drive out the evil spirit they thought had seized them. Paracelsus is certainly wrong. His blunder lies in believing the Divining Rod turned only over metals. The rod wasn't deceiving anyone: it dips just as readily over water, over dead bodies, over pits dug in the earth — in short, over anything that gives off vapors, exhalations, and fumes. Far from Paracelsus having invented the rod's use for metals, we find that Basil Valentine, active thirty years earlier, devoted seven entire chapters of his Testament to explaining the hazel rod's usefulness in prospecting for minerals.

But Paracelsus's blunder tells us that in his day — around 1530, when he wrote his works — no one had yet noticed the rod's ability to indicate springs and the corpses of murdered people. I imagine that after being misled by it several times, people finally realized it turned just as well over these other things. Chance has always had the biggest share in nearly every discovery. Nature's secrets haven't always been found by looking for them. The Chymists, who rarely find what they search for with so much study and patience, pick up very curious knowledge along the way — pure chance revealing to them mysteries of nature they might never have reached by aiming straight at them. Apelles, unable to paint the foam of a horse, hurled the sponge he used to wipe his colors at the canvas in despair — and by chance produced the very foam his art couldn't render. They say a glazier, cutting his glass, happened to look through a small lens that had broken off and noticed it magnified objects monstrously — and so discovered that marvelous little instrument, the microscope, which he hadn't been thinking about at all.

It was likewise pure chance that taught the peasant of Saint-Marcellin that the rod turned over the bodies of murder victims. One day, searching for water near his home, his rod dipped so sharply over one spot that he declared water must be close. But he was wrong — wrong the way we said Paracelsus was wrong. Instead of water, there was found, in a barrel, the body of a woman who still had around her neck the cord used to strangle her. It was immediately judged that this could only be a woman who had disappeared four months earlier. The peasant went to her house — people had been worried about her for some time — and presented his rod to every member of the household. It stayed motionless until he brought it to the husband, over whom it turned violently. The wretch fled at once, and the peasant concluded that the Divining Rod turns over corpses hidden in the earth, and even over criminals, as well as over springs and metals.

[End of Chapter One]

========================================

CHAPTER TWO

The surprising story of a peasant who, guided by the Divining Rod, tracked a murderer more than 45 leagues over land and more than 30 leagues by sea.

Several accounts of the murderer discovered by means of the Divining Rod have appeared in Paris, in print and in manuscript. They agree completely on the facts; the authors only disagree about how to explain the effect — the most surprising and extraordinary one there has ever been. So I could simply have used the first account that came to hand. I decided instead to give preference to the ones based on the official record drawn up by M. de Vanini, the King's Prosecutor at Lyon, a magistrate of exceptional merit, who investigated this whole important affair with his usual diligence and skill. To this account I add details drawn from several letters written to M. l'Abbé Bignon to brief him on the entire episode — something everyone who cares about the advancement of the sciences should know thoroughly, so that even if we can't fully penetrate the immediate cause of such singular effects, we can at least be sure of the facts. Readers won't mind seeing some passages from these learned letters here: they were read with pleasure at Court, and they come from a person of solid merit, the man we have to thank for the care taken in verifying the facts about the Rod's power. The letters matter all the more for my subject because they show some of the trouble that several people of standing — sharp, exacting minds — took to find out whether there is anything real and reliable in the use of this Rod.

ACCOUNT OF WHAT JAQUES AYMAR DID FOR THE DISCOVERY OF THE MURDERER OF LYON

On July 5, 1692, at about ten in the evening, a wine merchant and his wife were murdered in a cellar in Lyon, for the money they kept in a small shop nearby that doubled as their bedroom. It was all done with such determination and silence that at first no one noticed the murder — which gave the killers time to escape. A neighbor, deeply shaken by the enormity of the crime, remembered that he knew a certain Jaques Aymar, a well-off peasant who occupied himself with tracking thieves and murderers by their trail. He had Aymar brought to Lyon and presented him to the King's Prosecutor, and the villager gave his word that if he were taken to the scene of the murder to pick up its impression, he would certainly get on the killers' track, follow them, and identify them wherever they were. He added that to do this he would use a rod made of any kind of wood at all, cut without any ceremony at any time whatsoever — the same one he used to find springs, metals, and hidden treasure.

So the Criminal Lieutenant and the King's Prosecutor sent him into the cellar where the murder had been committed. There he became agitated; his pulse shot up as in a violent fever; and the forked rod in his hands turned rapidly over the two spots where the bodies of the wine merchant and his wife had been found. Having picked up his impression there, as he wanted, he followed his rod through all the streets the killers had fled along. He entered the courtyard of the Archbishop's palace and came to the Rhône gate, which was shut — this trial was being run at night. The next day he left the city by the Rhône bridge and, still led by his rod, turned right along the river. The three people escorting him witnessed that he sometimes sensed the trail of three accomplices and sometimes counted only two. In this uncertainty his rod led him to the house of a gardener, where the question of how many villains there were was settled. Once there, he insisted absolutely that they had sat around a table, and that of three bottles in the room they had touched one — over which his rod turned unmistakably. And in fact two children of nine or ten, who at first denied it for fear of being punished by their father for leaving the door open against his orders, later admitted that three men, whom they described, had slipped into the house and drunk the wine from the very bottle the peasant pointed out.

With the children's statement in hand, no one hesitated to follow the peasant down to the bank of the Rhône, half a league below the bridge: there, printed in the sand along the shore, were the villains' tracks — from which it was judged they had taken to the river. The villager followed them as precisely by water as by land, steering his boat through side channels and under an arch of the Bridge of Vienne that no one ever uses; from which it was concluded that, since the fugitives strayed so far from the true channel, they certainly had no boatman. During the voyage the villager put in at every landing where the fugitives had gone ashore, walked straight to their lodging, and identified — to the astonishment of the hosts and the onlookers — the beds they had slept in, the tables they had eaten at, and the jugs and glasses they had touched. He reached the camp of Sablon, where he felt much more strongly agitated; he believed he could spot the murderers in that crowd of soldiers — in fact he was sure they were there. But he didn't dare put his rod to work to confirm it, afraid the soldiers would insult and rough him up. So he went back to Lyon, and was sent back to the camp of Sablon by boat with letters of recommendation. The criminals were no longer there. He set out after them anyway, and tracked them all the way to the fair of Beaucaire in Languedoc, identifying along the road the beds, the tables, and the seats where they had rested.

At Beaucaire, searching through the streets, his rod led him to the door of a prison, where he flatly declared that one of the villains was inside. The door was opened for him; fourteen or fifteen prisoners were brought out; he applied his rod to all of them, and it turned only over a hunchback who had been locked up an hour earlier for a petty theft. The peasant didn't hesitate to say this was certainly one of the accomplices in the murder. He then went looking for the others and found they had taken a path leading to the Nîmes road. Nothing more was done on that occasion. The hunchback was transferred to Lyon, insisting to the peasant the whole way that his rod was lying, swearing he knew nothing whatever about the murder and had never even been to Lyon. But as he was led back along the very road he had taken in his flight, and found himself recognized everywhere by the innkeepers he had lodged with, he confessed, at Bagnols, that he had passed through that very house coming down the Rhône with two grown men — just as the gardener's children near Lyon had described them. He added that they were two Provençals who had taken him on as a servant and dragged him into the affair — that he had neither killed nor stolen — and that the Provençals had done the butchery and taken the money, of which they had given him only six and a half écus. This confession rather pleased the peasant, because it proved he hadn't been wrong.

One odd detail: the villager could not walk behind the hunchback on the road, because it gave him terrible heart-sickness. To avoid it, he had to walk in front. It's no doubt for the same reason that he can never be at a murder scene without being noticeably ill with the same heart-sickness, and shaken as if in the fit of a violent fever — which he feels far less when pursuing murderers on a river, and not at all when looking for water or hidden money.

In his first interrogation on reaching Lyon, the hunchback confessed that on the day of the murder, two men who spoke Provençal had taken him to a merchant's shop, where they bought — or stole — two pruning-bills [serpes]; that at about six in the evening all three went together to the wine merchant's, and got him to come down to the cellar with his wife on the pretext of filling a large straw-covered bottle; that the two Provençals went down into the cellar without him, with those good people; that there they killed them with blows of the bill, went back up to the shop, opened a chest, and stole 130 écus, 8 louis d'or, and a silver belt. He confessed further that they immediately hid in a large courtyard; that the next day they left Lyon by the Rhône gate; that they drank at a gardener's house in front of two children; that they untied a boat from the bank; that they went to the camp of Sablon, and from there to Beaucaire. He added, finally, that along the road they had lodged with the very same innkeepers the peasant had made him pass on the way back, so they could identify him.

The hunchback's confession explained many things that couldn't be untangled before. For there was indeed found in the shop that served as a bedroom a woodcutter's billhook, new and covered in blood, along with a large bottle nearly full of wine.

When news of the hunchback's capture spread through Lyon, everyone had his own theory about the man with the rod, who had followed and identified this wretch across the more than 45 French leagues between Lyon and Beaucaire. The whole city was abuzz over an adventure so surprising and so unprecedented that all antiquity offers nothing like it. The man was sent for; he was confronted, listened to, examined; everything he did was watched — and the thing honestly deserved it. The learned ran the same experiments themselves, in front of people of distinction. He went through the cellar, and the turning of his rod marked the two spots where the husband and wife had fallen dying; he was drenched in sweat there, his pulse rose, and he stayed in that state for more than an hour. The experiments were pushed further. The bloody bill was taken along with two others from the same maker; they were laid out a pace apart; the villager set his foot on each one, and the rod dipped only over the bloodstained one. Someone suggested the peasant might be skillfully imparting the movement to the rod himself; so the bills were buried, and his eyes were bound with a cloth — and still the rod turned, unfailingly, over the bloodstained bill. All this happened before the eyes of people who were not only of high standing, but of a turn of mind not easily dazzled.

I think the reader will judge the same once he has read what the first letter to Monsieur l'Abbé Bignon says about it. Here is how it runs:

"Monsieur de Bérulle came to my house some time ago at nine in the evening, with the Criminal Lieutenant, the King's Prosecutor, and the Comte de Varax. He presented a peasant to me, telling me he was from Saint-Marcellin in Dauphiné — the same man he had already mentioned to me, who had the power of discovering water as well as murderers and thieves. He then showed me three large pruning-bills, carried by one of the Prosecutor's footmen, all alike and from the same maker; one had a little blood on it, and he told me it was the one used in a murder committed here a few days before. He added that the peasant's rod turned over that one and not over the others; that he had already witnessed it himself, and asked whether I wanted to witness it too. I accepted. M. de Bérulle and I took the bills, and after placing them on the grass in my garden in different spots — it was already dark — M. de Bérulle took the further precaution of blindfolding the peasant with a cloth himself, and leading him by the hand over the bills. The rod did not turn over the first or the third; but it turned over the second, which by candlelight we recognized as the murder weapon. I wasn't yet satisfied with this first trial; thinking chance might have had a hand in it, I had my servants fetch three kitchen aprons, wrapped one bill in each, and we led the peasant over them again. He had heard they were being wrapped in linen, and told us he didn't think his rod would turn over them — and indeed it did not. I asked the peasant whether it would turn over the bills if they were covered with earth; he said yes. So M. de Bérulle and I buried them in my garden in separate places, completely out of sight, and led the peasant over. The rod did not turn over the first or the second, but it turned over the third, which by candlelight we recognized as the murder weapon. That, Monsieur, is all I have seen of the peasant.

"But here is what happened to me yesterday evening. The King's Prosecutor of this place — who, let it be said in passing, is one of the rarest and ablest men of this region — called for me at about six and took me to the house where the murder was done. There we found M. Grimaut, Director of Customs, whom I know to be a thoroughly honest man, and a young prosecutor named Besson, whom I didn't know, and who, the King's Prosecutor told me, had the power of the rod as well as M. Grimaut. We all went down into the cellar where the murder had been committed; and every time M. Grimaut and this prosecutor passed over the spot where the killing had been done, where there was still blood, the rods in their hands never failed to turn. ...We spent an hour there and ran many experiments on the murder weapon, which the King's Prosecutor had brought along, and every one of them came out exactly. I observed extraordinary things in the prosecutor. The rod turned much more strongly for him than for M. Grimaut; and when I put one of my fingers in each of his hands while the rod was turning, I felt absolutely extraordinary pounding of the arteries in his hands. ...His pulse was up as in a high fever. He sweated in great drops. From time to time he had to go out to the courtyard for air. ...You will easily judge, Monsieur, that after this I cannot help believing in the rod. Let people mock all they like. I expect to be vindicated soon: the experiments that are bound to be run in Paris on thefts and murders, by those who have the gift of finding water to an eminent degree, will soon show that we were right here to believe what we saw. For I have no doubt the power of detecting murders is found in most of those who detect water. I say most — I have already verified here that it is not found in all."

That is testimony detailed enough to leave nothing wanting on that score. But to pick up the thread of our story: two days after the peasant arrived in Lyon, he was sent back with archers to the path leading to Nîmes, where he had broken off following the two other villains, to pick up their trail again. The rod brought him back, by long detours, into Beaucaire — to the door of the same prison where the hunchback had been found. There he declared that yet another of them was inside. But the jailer set him straight: a man matching the description of one of the two villains had recently come to ask after the hunchback. The villager set out again on their track; he went as far as Toulon, to an inn where they had dined the day before. He pursued them onto the sea — they had embarked to take refuge at Genoa. He found that they put ashore from time to time on our coasts, and had slept there under olive trees; and despite the storms and the high wind that rose, he followed them, without being able to overtake them, to the farthest limits of the kingdom.

Meanwhile the hunchback's trial was being conducted at Lyon with the utmost rigor; and by the time the peasant returned, this criminal — who gave his age as only nineteen — had been condemned to be broken alive on the wheel in the Place des Terreaux, and to pass, on his way to execution, before the wine merchant's door, where the sentence was read out. The condemned man had scarcely reached that house when, of his own accord, he asked pardon of those poor people, declaring that he had caused their death by suggesting the robbery and keeping the door while they were being killed. Here is how the letter to Monsieur l'Abbé Bignon puts it: "One of the accomplices in the murder that gave rise to the affair of the Rod, who had been followed as far as Beaucaire by means of that Rod, was broken alive two days ago. He confessed everything; and his confession matched everything the Rod had indicated, and fifty other proofs and circumstances obtained elsewhere, so closely that no affair of this kind was ever better cleared up."

Here is a fact I hold to be thoroughly established — however inclined I am to enjoy the full rights of philosophical liberty, which doesn't readily rest on the credit and authority of witnesses. The gentlemen of the Royal Society of London, who take so many precautions, even scrupulous ones, before pronouncing on matters of fact, nevertheless decide to believe a phenomenon when sixty or a hundred people attest it. It is on this principle that their historian would have no one doubt the truth and certainty of their experiments. Since I ask no more than that for the story I have just told, I'll apply to the experiment of the Divining Rod what he says about the experiments of the Royal Society of England. I'll point out, then, that what is now proved and practiced in the world has no more certainty and evidence than what the Society puts forward — excepting the holy mysteries of our religion. In every matter of belief, opinion, and science, the certainty people ordinarily settle for doesn't equal that of the facts reported to the public. I dare appeal to all sensible people: in every country governed by laws, the testimony of only two or three witnesses is required in cases that decide life and property — so isn't it treating people fairly, in a question of physics, to give them the testimony and agreement of sixty or a hundred? [History of the Royal Society of London, part 2, sect. 17, p. 115.]

I couldn't resist adding this reflection at the end of the account, though what I said at the start of the first chapter ought to be enough to convince the doubters. But since some people act only by imitation, I'm glad to set before them the example of the gentlemen of the Royal Society of London. There you see the sensible limits they place on their own skepticism. After all — will people never remember that you need reasons for doubting as well as for believing?

Since Monsieur Bourdelot, Physician to the King, has done me the favor of sending me a letter he just received from M. Chauvin, physician of Lyon, answering certain objections put to him about the man with the rod, and particularly about the facts, I'll quote some of it here — all the more gladly because it bears on firmly establishing the truth of a fact that many people seem to have made it a policy to doubt.

Here is what M. Chauvin writes to M. Bourdelot: "I cannot conceive how there are still very good philosophers who deny the fact is possible, or who attribute it to some pact with the devil. I'm assured this latter view is Father Malebranche's: do me the kindness of telling me the truth of it — whether it can really be that so great a metaphysician resorts to such a cause to explain a phenomenon of physics, and what his reasons might be. Still, I'm somewhat less surprised at the good Father's position, given that the illustrious Monsieur Chirac, Professor of Medicine at Montpellier, could put this objection to me: ...He doesn't believe anyone in the world is endowed with a power like the one we suppose in our villager — not even for finding springs." To which Monsieur Chauvin adds: "The gift of finding springs is a matter of public record in our villager, and in several other people; it is confirmed every day by countless experiments. So there are men whose bodily makeup suits them for discovering springs; and since I think the disposition for following a killer is better matched to the human mechanism than the one for finding springs, I have no doubt some man may have that gift."

M. Chauvin then declares that the gift doesn't extend as far as M. Panthot stretches it in his letter to the First Physician. But he says that if you stick to the terms of the account he has given — the basis of his dissertation, and itself only a summary of the proceedings on which thirty most vigilant and enlightened judges condemned one of the accomplices to be broken alive, a man who confessed his crime on the scaffold — it appears the most skeptical person in the world could not call it into doubt. He then shows that merely surveying the whole affair — whose circumstances are simple, connected, natural, and even mixed with incidents the shrewdest planner could never have foreseen — makes it impossible that this was a clever fabrication or a staged intrigue. It is certain that two people were murdered; that a criminal was broken alive; and that thirty judges examined, followed, and judged the case with prodigious care. It is also certain that the peasant with the rod was the sole instrument by which the fleeing hunchback was discovered and arrested. Everyone who attests these incontestable points equally affirms that the villager succeeded in this search only with the help of his Divining Rod. Is there room for doubt after that? People act for a purpose; they do nothing without a motive; they don't exert themselves purely mechanically; in a concerted lie they aim at some advantage, some glory, or some profit. What do the judges of Lyon gain by acknowledging and declaring that Jaques Aymar followed the fleeing hunchback for forty-five leagues guided by his rod alone — unless it is the truth of the fact, the prompting of their conscience, and the duty of their offices that compel them to give this testimony?

M. Chauvin's letter also contains answers he gave to questions put to him by M. Terré, physician to the Cardinal de Bouillon. Since this clarification contains very curious facts, I thought it right to include them: "The villager can follow a killer without a rod, but he cannot find springs, gold, or hidden silver without it. Since with a killer the rod serves him only as an outward sign and a kind of relief, he doesn't bother to hold it constantly in his hands in the prescribed way. He admits, though, that a long pursuit of a killer tires him so badly that he is left drained by it. He doesn't need to set his feet on the killers' actual footprints; it's enough to be on their route — which is proved by the way he follows a murderer on a river. It's the nature of the inner sensation he feels at the moment he is, so to speak, magnetized at a murder scene that keeps him from being thrown off the scent. And though I can readily imagine the mechanism by which our peasant might recognize a loose woman, the good villager has never claimed to have that gift, any more than the gift of telling which of a murder's accomplices is guiltiest. The rod turns on the traces of an executed killer: indeed, it actually turns in the cellar where the murder was committed."

There, it seems to me, is the fact laid out in enough detail, and perhaps established firmly enough, to be doubted no longer. It's for those who read this story to examine their own minds about it, and to see how far Pyrrhonism would have to be pushed if it were considered legitimate to doubt facts bearing every mark of authority that human belief can demand.

[End of Chapter Two]

========================================

CHAPTER THREE

Nature has only one mechanism in all her operations — and the philosophy of corpuscles is the only one that can account for the marvels of sympathy, and for the movement of the Divining Rod.

First, a clarification. By the mechanism of nature, we don't mean some being that, without being God, acts ceaselessly throughout the world — the way the pagan philosophers imagined it, picturing nature as a universal soul animating and moving every physical thing. By the mechanism of nature we will always mean the general laws of motion that the Creator established, and by which He governs the whole universe.

A second point: just as it's certain that there is no effect without a cause — nothing can produce itself — it's equally certain that no cause can act on anything except by touching it. That's a principle of nature that must never be violated: nothing acts on what is distant: nihil agit in rem distantem. With that settled:

I. I say that since nature always works by the simplest means and never does anything pointlessly, she doesn't take a different route when she performs marvels than when she works, so to speak, in plain sight, with all her springs exposed. This principle matters enormously. It's because people ignored it when explaining natural phenomena that the School philosophers and the common people fell into opposite extremes, each of which equally held back the study of nature.

Ordinary people, used to never looking past what their senses show them, couldn't imagine that nature used invisible, intangible agents — so they credited sorcerers and demons with every effect whose workings they couldn't untangle. The School philosophers, not wanting to crawl with the crowd among such crude ideas, went the opposite way. When a surprising natural phenomenon needed explaining, they called in real qualities, substantial forms, and the pompous vocabulary of sympathy, antipathy, and occult virtues — terms they will always be accused of using to hide their ignorance.

Our plan is to walk between these two extremes. We'll leave to the crowd its big visible bodies, which are certainly not the instruments nature uses in her marvels. We'll also set aside the qualities, the substantial forms, and the occult virtues so celebrated in the Schools — which, true, aren't perceptible to the senses the crowd relies on, but which are at least as unintelligible as the most impenetrable secrets of physics. Between visible bodies and these inconceivable abstractions, we'll recognize a middle order of volatile agents, extremely fine and extremely active, which we'll call interchangeably corpuscles, particles of matter, atoms, subtle matter. The name hardly matters; the thing certainly doesn't deserve a schism between Cartesians and Gassendists — at bottom it's one and the same philosophy, and everything explained by Descartes's subtle matter can be explained by Gassendi's atoms.

You can see from this plan that I won't be calling on the four elements of the Peripatetics, or the three of the Chymists, to explain how the Divining Rod moves. That's no contempt for antiquity. If anything, it's out of respect for it that I proceed this way. However old Aristotle's doctrine and Peripatetic philosophy may claim to be, the philosophy of corpuscles is far older — at least that's the view of M. Boyle, who calls it the Phoenician philosophy because it preceded all Greek philosophy. To back this up, he cites certain ancient writers who affirm that before Epicurus, or Democritus, or even Leucippus had ever taught their philosophy in Greece, there was a certain physicist, a native of Phoenicia, who explained all the phenomena of nature by the motion and properties of the small particles of matter: "Scriptorum quorumdam antiquorum autoritate fretus, a quibus accepi Physicum quendam e Phoenicia oriundum..." [Relying on the authority of certain ancient writers, from whom I learned of a certain Physicist of Phoenician origin...]

Now, even though nature behaves differently when she hides in her works than when she acts in the open, it's still always the same mechanism. So when we see a body set in motion — pushed — even though the spring, and the way the push is delivered, escape us because our senses are so coarse and the agents nature employs so prodigiously tiny, we should still be convinced that this motion is produced by the same laws that move bodies openly and visibly. Just look at how Art, which only imitates nature, sets things moving: mechanics drives machines with the lever, the pulley, the wheel, the wedge, and the screw. So we should be convinced that when nature works by hidden springs, even if she doesn't use these crude instruments that mechanics uses to multiply human strength, she certainly substitutes equivalent ones — finer, and completely imperceptible.

The whole difference between the operations of nature that astonish us and the ones we don't notice at all is this: in the marvelous ones, her mechanism uses only organs and instruments our senses can't grip; in ordinary works she doesn't hide her craft, using only things we can see. The difference, then, lies in the agents — some perceptible, others discovered only by reason. As for the mechanism, it's always the same; everywhere the same analogy, the same conduct. When fire burns wood, for example, no one is surprised, because nature isn't hiding: you can watch the flame pierce and open the wood, splitting and forcing apart its parts so as to work in and consume it. All of that falls within the jurisdiction of the senses. It's different when the hot, dry fumes of mines scorch and kill the plants and trees growing above them; because those fine, acrid, biting exhalations don't register on the senses, reason had to step in to discover them. Yet it's the same mechanism. Nature, burning wood with fire, uses no other method than the one she follows to burn the plants over mines — the burning particles exhaled from the mineral matter pierce, open, tear, slice, and separate the parts of the plants to destroy them, exactly as fire does to wood. You can't pay too much attention to this point. I'd venture to say that here is a principle and a key for getting into the secrets nature seemed to want to keep from us — secrets on which the philosophy of the Schools has so far shed almost no light.

So philosophy has no trouble explaining what nature does in front of everyone, using big visible bodies; you can see the agent and the patient joined; the contact of the bodies is right there for the senses; the cause that acts and the thing acted on touch each other in a mathematical contact, body to body — the way a seal leaves its image in soft wax by pressing it directly. The difficulty comes when the agent and the patient are far apart, and you can't see what travels from one to carry its power to the other. That's how the action of a loadstone — like the Royal Society of London's, which moves a compass needle at a distance of nine feet — tortures a man who doesn't know that a whirlwind of subtle matter circulates through and around this magnet, with a sphere of activity extending nine feet around it. Though his eyes can't see these little agents, it is absolutely certain that they are what lets the magnet agitate the compass needle, touching it by a physical contact — that is, by small bodies acting as intermediaries between the agent and the patient, pressing on the needle all the same movements given to the stone. It's the same way the two cross-pieces joining the boards of a parallel ruler ensure that you can't move one board without the other always ending up in the same relative position.

II. These little bodies are too mysterious, and too useful, not to examine closely. 1. Sometimes they are a piece and an outflow of the very substance they come from — like the particles of vitriol that detach from the powder of sympathy and spread through the air. 2. Sometimes they are a third substance that carries the agent's power to the patient. The animal spirits are like this: a third substance that the brain, their reservoir, distributes into the nerves and from there into the muscles, producing in the animal all the movements we admire in it. 3. Sometimes they are just the air next to the agent, serving as a vehicle to carry its impression to the patient. The air around a bell, agitated by the motion of the bell and clapper, pushes the air next to it, that air the next, and so on in succession until it strikes, like a hammer, on the eardrum, producing the sound we then sense.

These little bodies are the whole mystery of what's called sympathy and antipathy — indeed they are its entire spring. Once you learn to recognize them, everything most occult about sympathy quickly comes into the open; and I hope our poets will stop singing:

There are secret knots, there are sympathies,
By whose sweet accord souls well-matched
Love one another, and let themselves be stirred
By those I-know-not-whats that none can explain.

That was true before the revival of the ancient philosophy of corpuscles, back when every philosopher, faced with nature's marvels, had nothing to offer but sympathy and antipathy — imagining they had said a great deal when they had paraded those pompous words, which are no more intelligible than the things they were supposed to explain. In those days, all of physics, wherever it touched the marvelous, ran on this magnificent vocabulary. Giambattista Porta says it's by the force of sympathy that the vine loves the elm, and by antipathy that the vine shuns the cabbage; that hemlock keeps away from rue; and that although hemlock juice is a deadly poison, it does no harm if, after drinking it, you swallow rue juice [bk. 1, mag. natur. ch. 1]. Cornelius Agrippa likewise explains by sympathy and antipathy everything in physics he doesn't understand. He says there is a great sympathy between the male palm and the female palm; between the vine and the olive; between the fig and the myrtle; and an irreconcilable antipathy between the scorpion and the crocodile, which seek each other's destruction; between the elephant and the pig; the lion and the rooster; the raven and the owl; the wolf and the sheep; the toad and the weasel [bk. 1, Philosoph. occult.]. Jerome Cardan philosophizes no differently. He says the lizard has a sympathy with man — it delights in seeing him and seeks his saliva, which it drinks greedily. He adds that it's by antipathy that a wolf's tail hung in a stable stops the oxen from eating [bk. 17, de subtil.].

To be fair, these philosophers did try to spell out what they meant by sympathy. They said it's an agreement or conformity of natural qualities, of humors, or of temperament, which makes two things love each other, seek each other, and rest content together. But they must admit in turn that anyone who says no more than that is admitting he understands nothing about it. No one doubts this agreement and conformity of qualities; the question is what produces it — what's the efficient cause. And that cannot be explained without the philosophy of corpuscles.

The Divining Rod has had the same fortune as the other secrets of physics. Its effects were chalked up to the sympathy between metals and certain plants — and that's as far as anyone got. Philip Melanchthon, in a discourse he wrote expressly on sympathy and antipathy, sorts the different sympathies he had observed in nature into six classes, and does the hazel rod the honor of placing it in the second. The second kind of sympathy, he says, is between metals and plants. From it comes the forked hazel rod used by mine workers to find veins of gold and silver, which for that reason they call the Divine Rod. He then makes some effort to explain the reason and secret of this sympathy that makes the rod turn over metals: it's because the hazel draws up through its roots the mineral juices in the soil, feeds on them and is marvelously strengthened by them, and from this is born its sympathy with gold and silver: "Cujus surculi vires augent, roborantque succi minerales." [Whose shoots the mineral juices augment and strengthen.] In those days you had to accept that coin, good or bad; nothing better was on offer. People didn't philosophize — they guessed, and unfortunately guessed very badly.

The philosophy of corpuscles takes us further today. It untangles, as well as can be done, nature's mechanism in the operations attributed to sympathy and antipathy. So we can say with confidence that the affection, the secret regard, we feel for certain people the first time we see them is caused by an emission of spirits or particles leaving those people, which make a pleasant impression on the retina or the optic nerve, or on the other nerves; reaching the brain, this affects the organ in such a way that the perception is agreeable to us. When, on the contrary, the sensation comes with a confused feeling of displeasure or aversion, we call it antipathy. There's a template for explaining every sympathy and antipathy to be found in the three families of animals, plants, and minerals.

M. Gassendi reports a rather funny example of antipathy that he witnessed himself. One day, he says, I saw with surprise a herd of pigs in the open market all start grunting at a butcher, eyeing him sideways as their mortal enemy, for as long as he was near them. I'll add one of my own: in a Paris street I once saw all the dogs come out of the houses and bark furiously at one of those rag-pickers who often try to catch them for their skins. This antipathy came from the fact that the butcher and the rag-picker were each surrounded by the spirits of the animals they had freshly killed: those particles, soaked into their clothes, had been torn out by violence and were therefore in extraordinary agitation, so they bore down fast on the bodies of the pigs and the dogs and struck them in a way that produced a deeply unpleasant sensation.

This is also why the blood of a murdered man stirs and flows from the wound in the murderer's presence — if it's true that this sometimes happens, as the laws, which seem to give it some weight, assume. The dead man's spirits, which entirely surround the murderer, and which were torn out amid all the horror produced by the presence of a cruel, bloodthirsty man, remain in such violent agitation that if the corpse is within their sphere of activity, they strike it hard and set the spirits remaining in the blood moving again. "Potest aliqua adhuc fieri colluctatio inter occisi spiritus in sanguine superstites, & appellentia ab occisore corpuscula, iis consimilia, quae occisionis tempore horrorem summum incusserint." [Physic. sect. 1, bk. 6, ch. 14: There may yet occur some struggle between the spirits of the slain man surviving in the blood, and the corpuscles arriving from the slayer, similar to those which at the time of the killing struck the deepest horror.]

It's said the rooster has a sympathy with the dawn, announcing its arrival with crowing and a beating of wings.
