Cicero reports that Democritus explained this sympathy very well, by the distribution of the subtle matter formed from digestion overnight and spread through every part of the rooster. By then, he says, digestion is finished; the blood has been distributed through the whole body; the rooster feels his strength restored by the fresh spirits filling him; so there's nothing marvelous in this bird, for whom song is natural, letting his joy burst out in crowing and wing-beating. "Democritus quidem optimis verbis causam explicat, cur ante lucem galli canant; depulso enim de corpore, & in omne corpus diviso, & mollificato cibo, cantus edere quiete satiatos." [De Divinat. bk. 2, n. 57: Democritus indeed explains in the best terms why cocks crow before daybreak: the food being driven from the crop, divided through the whole body, and softened, they give forth their song, sated with rest.]

The heliotrope's sympathy with the sun makes too much noise to pass over in silence. The reason this flower turns toward that star, when well exposed to it, is that the sun's rays dry the stem on the side they strike, shortening it as its spirits evaporate, and making it bend — just as a damp playing card curls when set before the fire or in the sun. That's the whole mystery that has tormented so many philosophers; and it's nothing at all, once you have the key of the philosophy of corpuscles.

A word, too, about electric bodies — the diamond, the sapphire, the opal, amber, jet, agate, Spanish sealing-wax — called electric because they attract bits of straw quite visibly. Everyone has seen how these bodies, and several other precious stones, once rubbed against cloth, pick up little straws and all sorts of very light things; but few people know how the attraction works. Those who tried to explain it by occult virtues said nothing at all. The philosophy of corpuscles untangles it perfectly well. When you rub the substance, you open its pores and increase the motion of the subtle matter transpiring from it; this sends out an abundant emission of spirits all around, whose rapid outflow drives away the adjoining air. But since air has the power to spring back — to retrace its steps, so to speak — it pushes the light electric bodies back; and returning, they catch and carry off the light things in their path. I won't stop to prove that air has an elastic power. The air-filled ball that schoolboys play with bounces only because, in falling, the leather is pressed in and compresses the air; the air, springing back into the hollow it had been driven from, makes the big leather ball bounce as many times as the air inside is compressed.

Electric bodies, then, attract straw the way the magnet attracts iron — with these differences: 1. The magnet needs no rubbing, because there is always around it an atmosphere of magnetic matter in motion. 2. The magnet attracts only iron. 3. The pull of iron toward the magnet isn't blocked by putting another body in between — which shows that magnetic particles, whose power passes even through marble, are incomparably finer than electric ones.

Now we must come to explaining the movement that makes the Divining Rod dip over water, over metals, and over the other things experience shows it turns above. This is where everything in this chapter has to be gathered up. I've run long only because the matter is important, and because the world needs preparing for a system many people wouldn't understand without the help of the principles I've laid down. 1. I've shown that nature has only one mechanism in everything she does. So we should study that mechanism in an effect we already understand, in order to recognize the same mechanism more easily in the dipping of the Divining Rod. No one will object to this method; it's plain good sense, which says you explain what you don't yet know about nature by what you already do know. 2. I've shown by several examples that only the philosophy of corpuscles can untangle what is most hidden in nature's marvels, and in everything called sympathy and antipathy. So we must seek out and follow these little corpuscles, since they will reveal the whole secret mechanism of nature in the movement of the hazel rod. That is what I hope to accomplish in what follows, in a way that will leave little to be desired on the score of evidence.

Before ending this chapter, I must make an observation that seems to me of the highest importance for both physics and medicine — and one I don't see that the philosophers have ever properly considered. More attention should have been paid to the extreme fluidity and liquidity of the air, and to its perfect analogy with water. It's made of particles so fine and so subtle that they easily free themselves from the bodies enclosing them to form a whole that is extremely liquid. We should therefore think of the atmosphere wrapping the globe as a river of prodigious immensity, in which humans and all the animals live in their own way, as fish and sea creatures live in water. The parts of air join and separate as easily as we see the particles of water join and separate. Air, like water, takes on cold and heat; it's easily impregnated with odors good and bad; it flows and seeps in like water the moment it finds the smallest opening, as we experience every day at doors and windows that don't close quite tight. I don't even doubt it can be tinted with all sorts of colors, the way water sometimes is.

EXPERIMENT. Everyone knows the party trick that makes people's faces look pale, livid, and hideous, like the dug-up dead. You burn, in a room, a glass of brandy with a pinch of common salt in it. The candles are put out, and the fire too. The air of the room is then so loaded with the evaporated particles of brandy and salt that faces seen through it look frightful.

Some take the trick further. EXPERIMENT. If instead of brandy and salt you evaporate, in a small room, half a setier of good spirit of wine, set with a piece of camphor in a glazed earthen dish over burning coals, then whoever enters afterward with a lit candle sees a spectacle that terrifies him. The whole room being filled with particles of spirit of wine and camphor — the most flammable stuff in the world — the air catches fire, and the person finds himself standing in the middle of flames. The fun of it is that it's a subtle fire, like lightning's, that harms nothing at all. The drawback is that camphor has such a violent smell, which many people dislike.

So air is absolutely fluid and liquid like water; and since it takes all the same impressions, we should reason about it as we do about water. Now, just as bathwater turns filthy and unbearably foul within two hours, the same sometimes happens to air. And on this point I cannot protest loudly enough against the barbarous ignorance of certain people who, visiting the sick, so misguidedly insist that the room be kept tightly sealed and shut up — which can have very dangerous consequences both for the patient and for those tending him. What happens to bathwater in a few hours — fouling and corrupting — happens to the air of the room in a few days; and it is a terrible cruelty to force a sick man to swallow back all the bad air that nature had discharged from him through unnoticed perspiration. A healthy person's basic cleanliness includes frequently refreshing the air of his room by opening a door or a window; and for a sick man you hope to restore, it's important to give the room a little fresh air from time to time. That's why M. Tschirnhaus, in the little book he wrote in Germany on remedies for the body, includes among his rules for conduct in sickness one that says: the sick man should be placed where the air is calm, moderately warm, and free of bad smells; and if the illness runs long, the air of his room should be refreshed from time to time. [Rule 9.]

Having shown that we should reason about air the way we ordinarily reason about water, I would move straight on to my system of the movement and dipping of the Divining Rod; but I feel obliged first to answer an objection that I fear will be raised against me at once.

[End of Chapter Three]

========================================

CHAPTER FOUR

We know enough about the nature of corpuscles to use them in explaining the phenomena of the Divining Rod.

The first objection I'm likely to face is that I want to explain the very obscure phenomenon of the Divining Rod's movement by something we may know even less about. Can particles of subtle matter — too fine to be detected by senses whose organs are too coarse — really demonstrate what makes the Divining Rod move over water, over metals, and over the footsteps of criminals?

That objection would be very Peripatetic in spirit, it's true; there's no denying the philosophers have handled the subject of corpuscles carelessly. They've barely acknowledged their existence. So it's no surprise they took so little trouble to investigate their different kinds, when they hardly examined whether they exist at all. Aristotle — and those who consider it a duty never to abandon him no matter what he says — chose to sort into two classes all the little bodies that detach from the two great masses making up the globe. Hot, dry exhalations is their name for the fumes rising from the solid part of the earth; cold, moist vapors is their name for what rises from the liquid part, the waters. Sometimes they even muddle these names and use vapors, exhalations, and fumes interchangeably. Writers with clearer ideas, who wanted to speak more precisely, have always kept the distinction.

I don't dispute that vapors are particles of water, separated from the rest and lifted into the air by the heat of the sun, or of the underground fires, or by the earth's circular motion — experience shows us that heat turns water into vapor. I'd just as willingly grant that exhalations are particles that the same heat raises from the dry parts of the earth.

But that division gets us no further. Not all particles fit into the family of cold, moist vapors and the family of dry, hot exhalations — the fumes of mercury and of opium are considered dry and cold. And even if the Schools' inventory of corpuscles were accurate, it would hardly make anyone more learned about the nature of things. I don't know a bull any better for knowing he has horns, since the goat, the rhinoceros, the stag, and several sea creatures have them too. Do I know the nature of amber, of cantharides, of cinnamon, and of tobacco just by knowing that the particles coming off them are dry and hot?

In place of that two-class division, which is no real help to physics, we'll consider three properties of these streams of subtle matter — properties that will serve us extremely well, not only for knowing the specific nature of these little bodies, but for finding the proximate, immediate cause of nature's most surprising phenomena, above all the effects of the Divining Rod, whose cause has until now been judged occult and impenetrable. 1. We'll see that these particles, spread through the air, though shrunk to an invisible volume, still keep the nature of the whole they separated from. 2. We'll find that our senses can tell, from the perceptible qualities of these particles, that they differ among themselves as much as the bodies they come from differ from one another. 3. We'll satisfy ourselves that these particles produce on certain bodies the same effects that the full mass of their parent substance would produce — which will let us know distinctly the proper, particular nature of these particles, and will take us deep into the most hidden physics.

I. I say that particles spread through the air, though shrunk to an invisible volume, still keep the nature of the whole they came from — and this becomes obvious the moment they reunite. In damp weather, the water vapor floating through the air turns back into water on marble, on pavements, on walls, and on every other surface cold enough to condense and hold it; or it settles over the land as dew and rain. Quicksilver itself shows us, through its various transformations, how it frees itself from the mixtures it's been forced into, and how slyly it sheds the mask and the borrowed costume under which people thought they had disguised it beyond recognition: distill it, with a properly proportioned fire, and it slips its chains, flies off as vapor, and is found again immediately, in its original form, in the receiver.

EXPERIMENT. The workmen who use mercury to gild copper or silver often learn at their own cost how true it is that streams of particles carry the same quality, good or bad, as the bodies they come from. As the gilders evaporate the quicksilver bit by bit over the fire, where it goes off into the air as fumes, they undergo the very process of distillation: just as in distillation the quicksilver, dispersed as vapor, regathers and resumes its fluid form in the receiver, so the fumes rising from it in gilding sometimes gather in the workmen's heads — and eventually kill them. That's why the gilders, and the chymists who use a lot of mercury, take a precaution against this hazard: they hold a gold coin in their mouths. The spirits of the mercury head for it so readily that when the coin is taken out, it looks like silver rather than gold.

EXPERIMENT. What the Chymists call flowers of sulphur is a substance formed from the condensed vapors seen rising from sulphur as it's purified — and it is nothing but true sulphur, the same in nature as what was put up for sublimation, as you can verify by melting: the flower reduces right back into a lump of sulphur.

I could prove with many other experiments from M. Boyle's works that particles ordinarily keep the same qualities as the bodies they evaporated from, but I'll limit myself to one that establishes the philosophy of corpuscles perfectly. M. Boyle relates that he asked a clever friend, who was leaving to take up an important post in the East Indies, to remember to make some physical observations for him during the voyage and report them. Among those the friend sent was this: as the ship approached the Isle of Ceylon — so famous for the abundance of cinnamon and fragrant gums gathered there — the wind from that quarter carried them a thoroughly pleasant scent, though they were perhaps more than twenty-five miles away. Air is a fluid that picks up the particles spread through it easily; and since it's a fluid far more subtle than water, wine, and every liquid we know, it's no surprise that streams of particles exhaled from bodies keep so long, travel so far, and make themselves felt at such distances in the air.

II. A second thing to observe about corpuscles — and it can be of very great use in physics — is that the senses can tell, even from the perceptible qualities of these particles, that they differ among themselves as much as their parent bodies differ from one another. It would be hard to decide whether the various vapors that the sun's heat and the stirring of the air visibly raise from the globe differ at all in color; the eye could hardly judge. But in certain products of art, at least, the eye can plainly see a diversity of colors among exhalations, even without any external fire to set them moving. M. Boyle assures us he often observed that above well-rectified spirit of nitre, even cold, fumes rose in waves inside the stoppered vials where he kept it — and in these little clouds a certain red was quite distinctly visible.

We have to admit that we have no organs that can report anything about the quantity, the shape, and the motion of these little material beings. Our organs, however admirably built, are too coarse to reach subtle matter. The unaided eye — one without a microscope — certainly cannot see those living atoms, as a poet would call them, that live in cheese, and that we discover with that instrument no philosopher should be without. Then we see, with surprise, that these little living specks, which we would never have known existed without this help, have organs, feet, eyes, and move like animals. How could we have known their shape, their motion, and their size, when without the microscope we didn't know there was any such thing in nature? From which we may judge: if little worms have been discovered in vinegar, in milk, in the blood of certain people, in the pustules on some people's skin, there are surely others elsewhere that we know nothing about — and that it might matter greatly to human health and life to know well.

Now one thing deserves special consideration, and it will help us explain the symptoms observed in the peasant with the rod when he stands where a murder has been committed. Touch is usually ranked the coarsest of the senses, perhaps rightly; but because this sense covers more ground than the others — it's spread over the whole body — it's certain that touch sometimes detects the presence of little particles the eyes can't grip. So there are birds, and even delicate or infirm people, who can foresee rain, storms, and changes of weather by the aches that the streams of invisible vapor filling the air produce in parts of the body once weakened by some serious ailment.

M. Boyle proves this important point of physics with very curious facts. He relates that he knew an intelligent lady, of an altogether tender and delicate constitution, who unfailingly knew when her visitors had come from a place with deep snow. She attributed this surprising discernment — which she didn't have at other times — when someone arrived from amid ice, to a certain impression which she believed was made on her through the same organ that judges odors. He adds that a very able physician among his friends, seized with a rather unusual fever, found his hearing become so acute that he heard distinctly even what people whispered low into one another's ears.

There's a story in Cicero that outdoes even all this — nothing ever looked more like a paradox. That great man says two friends traveling together arrived at Megara; one went to lodge at an inn, the other with a friend. The latter saw his companion in a dream, begging for help because the innkeeper meant to kill him. Taking it for an unpleasant dream with no truth in it, he went back to sleep; but at once his companion appeared again, saying that since he hadn't helped him while he lived, he should at least not leave his death unavenged: the innkeeper, after killing him, had just hidden his body in a cart under dung, and the friend should be at the gate of the inn early next morning, before the body was carried out of town. Cicero says this friend, shaken by so terrible a dream, ran there at daybreak, found the drover at the gate of the lodging, and asked what he was carrying in his cart; the peasant fled on the spot; the dead man was pulled out from under the dung; and after the matter was thoroughly examined, the innkeeper was condemned to death. [Cicero, De Divinatione, bk. 1, n. 57.]

Without reaching for prodigies to explain this, I'd say the man being so foully murdered spread into the air — by his cries, or by unnoticed perspiration — impressions capable of carrying far enough to reach his friend, who would have been more sensitive to them than anyone, through the bond a long friendship had formed between them. It's to this kind of impression — to these movements of particles spreading through the air as they detach from the bodies of people dear to us — that I attribute the premonitions we have of misfortunes befalling absent family and friends. Cardan, that endlessly curious writer, reports similar things. It's like two lute strings tuned in unison: the impression one of them makes in the air, when plucked, sets the other vibrating. And truthfully, I've long suspected the air can carry a word a very long way to a person whose hearing has the acuteness M. Boyle attributes to that feverish physician who heard clearly what was whispered in the ear — above all between people bound by mutual friendship.

There are clear proofs, then, of what particles exhaled into the air, after separating from some mass of matter, can do to the organs of the human body — particularly to touch. But to convince ourselves fully that the particles mixed in the air are not all of one kind — that they come in different sizes, different shapes, and motion that is not the same everywhere or always — just consider what good hunting dogs do: they pick out their master, after losing him, at a fair with who knows how many thousand people. M. Boyle reports something on this subject that proves beyond argument that there are particles of different quality, almost always keeping something of the substance they exhaled from. He says a gentleman, a relative of his, wanting to test whether his hunting dog was well trained, ordered a servant to go to a small town four miles away and continue from there through a market town three miles beyond, where a fair was on that day. Some time later the gentleman set the dog on the servant's track. The dog took the scent so well that he went to the small town, then to the market town, passed through the fair without once stopping among the endless crowd he kept meeting, went straight to a house the servant had entered, climbed to a room on the top floor, and there, in a packed company, picked out the servant — to the astonishment of the many people the gentleman had sent to follow his dog.

A professional huntsman, marvelously skilled at training dogs, once assured M. Boyle that the impression a stag leaves passing over turf lasts a good six or seven hours. But a clever man who happened to be present said he had old dogs of so fine and subtle a nose that if they came near a spot in a forest where a stag had passed the day before, after a little while they took the scent, and went straight to where the stag had bedded down. He added that he had dogs that could, in the hunt, single out a heated stag from a herd of other stags it had thrown itself among. He even maintained that from the way his dogs followed an animal, he could tell whether it was a hare or a fox. A fox, in fact, having much more odor, the dogs hunt it more hotly and carry their noses higher. So true is it that the matter of a hare's unnoticed perspiration differs from what exhales from the body of a fox.

These effects are ordinary, but no less admirable for it. It's hard to believe anyone's mind is so shuttered as not to admire the keenness of a good hunting dog, who detects the particles spread in the air, follows them, and steers by them so exactly and so accurately that he is never thrown off the scent. Yet M. Boyle doesn't seem greatly struck by this phenomenon. It is far more surprising, he says, that effluvia should act when they issue from a cold body than from a living, warm animal whose sense is infinitely sharper than anything found in humans; what is much harder to explain is how a cold, dry body can exhale matter active enough to derange the structure of something as hard as metal.

It should be noted, though, that it isn't absolutely true that particles spreading through the air always keep their quality unaltered. Sometimes they mix and combine with the particles of the air, in which they seem to vanish and lose themselves.

EXPERIMENTS. 1. Two viol strings tuned an octave apart, struck at the same time, seem — if you judge by ear — to give a single sound, though there are certainly two. 2. There are liquids that, mixed together, keep nothing of the color they had before; and the tongue finds in them not the least trace of what it found when they were separate. 3. Several flowers and fragrant herbs, dried and put into a small closed bag, make what is commonly called a potpourri: the odor-particles exhaling from them, mixed and combined with one another, produce an effect in which you'd be hard pressed to single out the smell of any one plant. 4. It's from this combining of the vapors, fumes, and exhalations rising from the earth that clouds, rains, and the other meteors of the air are sometimes born; and sometimes the combining makes the mists raised in the lower air precipitate and fall, giving us fair weather. 5. It's also this combining of different particles that makes the air pestilential and contagious: particles that had been fixed, concentrated, or blunted by association with the enveloping air become more active through certain mixtures, and once unchained, make all their malignity felt. Diemerbroeck, a learned physician, observed that while the plague was at Nijmegen, it ordinarily attacked no one in a house as long as the linen wasn't washed there with soap; but on the very day, or at latest the day after the linen was soaped, two or three people of the house caught the plague. He declares he painfully went through this himself in his own house, where most of his servants, who had put the linen to the soap, were seized with plague that very night. Isn't it strange that particles coming off soap should have the power to wake the pestilential particles in the air, which until then were at rest and harmless? 6. This combining of different particles is sometimes as beneficial as we've just seen it deadly. Georgius Sandys, an Englishman, relates that while he was in Egypt, the plague was at grand Cairo; and cruel and murderous as it was, it eased the moment the Nile began to flood. This is reasonably credited to the nitrous particles, which the water of that river carries in great abundance, and which, mixing into the air, envelop the plague particles and strip away whatever makes them pernicious: "Pestis quae enormiter hic saevit, ad primam fluminis intumescentiam subito cessat." [Sandys in Itinerar. bk. 2: The plague, which rages here enormously, ceases suddenly at the first swelling of the river.]

III. A third thing that would go far toward revealing the specific nature of these effluvia — or, as M. Boyle puts it, these swarms of corpuscles — would be to confirm that these atoms produce, not just on our sense organs (we covered that in the previous reflection), but on various bodies, the same effects that the full mass of their parent substance would produce. Now we know perfectly well that the streams of subtle matter exhaled from bodies work the same effects the bodies themselves would, if present and applied. Here is the proof.

1. The physicians assure us that the particles separating from venomous bodies, spread through the air, can poison just as the mass itself could.

2. Sennert relates that apprentice apothecaries, not yet hardened to the smells of drugs, never fail to fall into a deep sleep when the vapors coming off the liquids they distill for opium and sleeping preparations rise through the nostrils to their brains. [Sennertus bk. 6, part 7.]

3. Writers on the mandrake say its root, or its juice, taken as a drink, causes a lethargic sleep. And Levinus Lemnius writes that as long as he kept a mandrake apple in his study, he could never work: he immediately fell into a drowsiness he could only shake by removing the apple — after which the clarity and cheerfulness of his mind returned as before. [Levin. Lemnius, in explicatione herbar. biblicar. ch. 2.]

4. The contagious malignity in the particles coming off a mad dog, whether by its breath or otherwise, is sometimes the most fatal proof that the subtle matter evaporating from a body often produces all the same effects the body itself would.

5. Caelius Aurelianus says, after Aretaeus, that one man was struck with rabies from taking the breath of a mad dog too closely; another caught the disease from merely being scratched by the paws of an afflicted dog [Caelius Aurelianus bk. 3, acutor. morbor.]. And Matthiolus affirms that a man who hadn't been bitten at all caught that horrible disease from chancing to receive a little slaver from a mad dog.

6. Sennert says a painter opened a little box in which he had long kept realgar, and the fumes of that very dangerous mineral went to his head so violently that he was seized with vertigo, lost consciousness entirely, his face swelled horribly — and he would surely have died if he hadn't been rescued with antidotes they made him swallow.

7. M. Boyle says there are plants and minerals whose odor, fumes, and exhalations, spread in the air, have a virtue nearly as cathartic — that is, purgative — as if you had swallowed the substances themselves. He affirms that a physician friend of his had a quantity of black hellebore roots pounded in a mortar, and watched with amusement as everyone in the room — above all the boy doing the grinding — was purged with considerable violence. And Sennert says the same happens to some people from the mere smell of colocynth. He relates, after Nicolaus Florentinus, that a certain Lombard in Florence rashly burned a large, nearly black spider at a candle; a fume came off it which he breathed in through the nose, so violent that he passed out; his pulse weakened until it could scarcely be found; all night he suffered cruel gripings in his intestines; and he was only pulled through by dint of theriac and other antidotes.

I'll close this chapter with an observation made in America, which M. Boyle reports near the end of his admirable little treatise De natura determinata effluviorum. He says he learned from several intelligent people who had been to America that there is a poisonous tree there called the manchineel, and that the birds not only avoid the fruit of this deadly tree — most won't even perch on it. Which doubtless comes from the particles separating from it and warning them off.

[Conclusion of the chapter:] ...and over the footsteps of criminals — the particles that move the Rod and guide the peasant we call the man with the Rod. And no one can now complain that, to explain the surprising effects of this Rod, we rely on corpuscles about which nothing is known — since we now stand assured: 1. that these little beings spread through the air, though shrunk to an invisible volume, keep the nature of the whole they separated from; 2. that these particles of matter differ among themselves as much as the bodies they come from differ from one another; and 3. that the atoms produce on certain bodies the same effects that the mass of the substance they exhaled from would produce there.

[End of Chapter Four]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER FIVE

A system of the movement and dip of the Divining Rod over springs, mines, treasures, and the trail of thieves and fleeing murderers.

Having committed myself to explaining the previously unknown mechanism behind the Divining Rod's dipping by way of a mechanism we already understand, I had no trouble making my choice. I had barely let my imagination roam through the three kingdoms — animal, vegetable, mineral — before I saw at once that the movement and dip of the compass needle, or of a magnetized iron rod, is exactly the same thing as the movement and dip of the Divining Rod. To put it as I see it: I find the same mechanism everywhere, because nature has only one. If certain animals, like dogs, are drawn by the scent of a hare; if certain plants, like the male and female palm, seem to seek each other out; if among the metals quicksilver bonds greedily with gold — all of it happens for the same reason and by the same mechanism: a stream of particles traveling from the hare to the dog, from the male palm to the female, from the mercury to the gold. You only have to open your eyes with a little attention and look at the great theater of nature to find countless effects that are perfectly analogous to the one we admire in the hazel rod.

But none fits it better, frankly, than the movement and dip of the magnetized iron rod. The two are so much the same thing, down to the smallest detail, that it's astonishing that so many learned men and great philosophers, consulted on this matter and pronouncing on it, never even glimpsed the perfect analogy. Nothing could have offered itself to my imagination happier, easier, or more widely accepted than the magnetism that moves a magnetized iron rod and tips it toward the earth, as an explanation of the magnetism that makes the Divining Rod move and dip over springs, over veins of metal, and over the footsteps of criminals.

My system for the hazel rod, then, is the same as the system of the magnetized iron rod's dip; whoever understands one has as good as untangled the other. But the two have to be compared side by side, to demonstrate the resemblance — in physics, after all, nobody is obliged to take your word for anything.

1. Just as the magnetic particles circulating around the earth, when they meet the magnetized iron rod, line it up along their course and make it parallel to the lines they trace around the globe — so too, over underground watercourses, over mines, over buried treasure, and over the trail of fleeing criminals, there are particles rising vertically into the air which soak into the hazel rod and force it downward, to lie parallel to the vertical lines they trace as they rise. What happens there is what would happen to a magnetized iron rod at the earth's pole: it would dip straight down, because the magnetic particles rise vertically there.

2. Just as the magnetic particles in the air act on the magnetized iron rod because it's already soaked with similar particles — the ones left in it when it was stroked with a good magnet, the way water seeps more easily into something already damp — in the same way the particles rising from springs, from mines, and from a fleeing criminal's trail easily soak into the hazel rod, because Jaques Aymar, who is soaked with them first, passes a little whirlwind of them into it by touching it. It's the same way the particles of quicksilver evaporated in a room gather and precipitate into a glass containing two or three ounces of it: those little invisible atoms, drifting through the air at random, meet an atmosphere of similar vapors circulating around the mass in the glass, mix with them, and fall into the quicksilver. I noted on page 86 that you should reason about particles, atoms, vapors, exhalations, subtle matter — and even air itself, though coarser — the way you reason about liquids.

3. Finally, just as the iron rod has to be magnetized before it can take its direction from the whirlwind spread through the air and circling the earth — and it's magnetized by stroking it with a good magnet, which passes a little whirlwind of magnetic particles into it — so the hazel rod would feel nothing from the little bodies that make it dip if it weren't first, so to speak, magnetized: that is, touched by the hand of a man who, himself already thoroughly penetrated and flooded with the vapors, exhalations, and fumes rising from water, from metals, or from a fleeing thief's trail, passes a little whirlwind of them into the rod. That's why Jaques Aymar first had to take his impression at the scene of the killers' crime. That's why he sets his foot on a man's foot, to take his impression, when he wants to know whether he's the culprit he's after. That's why he likewise set his foot on the billhooks, to pick out the one used in the murder.

All this, however clear, can only make sense to people who understand magnetic dip — about which there is, I think, no longer any difficulty. So for readers who haven't studied these matters, and who will be glad to confirm that there is nothing in the Divining Rod's movement that isn't entirely natural, I'll repeat here what I said about the dip of the compass needle in my Treatise on the Magnet of Chartres, pp. 115–118:

"Dip, in the magnet, is the action by which compass needles, which balance level before being magnetized, lose that balance once they receive the magnetic virtue — because they become heavier at the end facing the pole nearest the place where the experiment is made.

"This dip comes from the direction the magnetic matter imposes on these needles, and on all iron rods free to move. We have seen that this matter moves in a circuit around the earth, curving from the equator C toward the poles S and M. Since this matter aligns iron rods to its own motion, making them parallel to the lines it traces, it follows that where it plunges toward the pole, the needle must plunge the same way. That's why the dip isn't equal in all climates. There is none at all at the equator, where the needle lies perfectly level, as the two arrows between C and A show, and as travelers' reports teach us. The dip must increase as you approach the poles, as the two arrows between S and A, and between A and M, demonstrate — and as a thousand experiments by the pilots confirm every day. The pilots, who at first didn't know the cause, found that when sailing north they had to stick a little wax under the south-facing end of the needle, because the other end plunged toward the northern pole. At the Line, the wax had to come off entirely, because the needle balances perfectly there. Then, conversely, wax had to go under the north-facing end once they crossed the equator toward the southern pole, where the end facing that pole plunged.

"Several experiments have taught us that the magnet dips at Paris about sixty-five degrees from the horizontal. The magnet of Chartres has this same dip. I found it by the method M. Rohault describes in his Physics, Part 3, ch. 8, p. 202, using a dip needle — a needle made expressly for this experiment. It's a steel wire a little over four inches long, crossed at its middle at right angles by a little brass wire, which supports the needle the way a balance beam is supported by its bearings. At first, so mounted, the dip needle balanced perfectly; but the moment its two ends had touched the two poles of the magnet of Chartres, once it was set in the plane of the meridian, the north-facing end tipped suddenly, and didn't stop until it had dipped about sixty-five degrees from the horizontal."

EXPERIMENT. To verify this dip without the expense and trouble of a voyage nearly around the world, you can watch, with a little iron wire three lines long laid in various positions on a round magnet, the same phenomena that befall the compass needle, or the magnetized iron rod, in the world's different climates. Without leaving your study, you can perform around a spherical magnet the same experiments the pilots have made around the earth. Carry the little wire to the magnet's equator and it will lie down of its own accord parallel to the magnet's axis, with no dip. Place it at the poles and it will stand as if it grew out of the magnet, continuing its axis. Put it between equator and poles and it will tip and dip at the end facing the pole — the closer to the pole, the more steeply. So from the different positions and dips this iron wire takes on a spherical magnet as you move it around, you can read off the same positions and dips the compass needle holds, along a single meridian, in the various countries of the world. This happens because every magnet is wrapped in a little whirlwind of magnetic matter, circulating around it and tracing lines, just as the great whirlwind of the same matter traces them around the earth. That's why Gilbert the Englishman said so well that the Earth is a great magnet, and a round magnet is a little earth.

Whoever really grasps this mystery of the magnetized rod's dip will easily grasp the whole secret of the Divining Rod's dip. It tips, as it does, only because the columns or lines of particles — which we'll shortly prove rise above springs, mines, treasures, and the trail of fleeing criminals — find the rod already soaked with similar little bodies, bear down on it eagerly, tilt it toward the earth, and pull it the way a silver thread or a little gold chain would. And this pull makes the hazel rod parallel to the vertical lines of the vapors and exhalations, just as the magnetized rod aligns itself parallel to the lines traced by the magnetic matter pulling it. In the same way, tie a tree branch to the stern of a boat and you'll soon see it swing lengthwise into the river's current, with which the branch always tends to align itself.

This system doesn't just show how the Divining Rod turns over underground watercourses, mines, and buried treasure; it also explains perfectly the whole story of the discovery of the Lyon murderer. It really is the same mechanism and the same conduct of nature throughout.

1. Just as the particles rising from springs and mines soak into the rod, drawn by the ones Jaques Aymar passed into it by touching it — so the particles exhaled by the unnoticed perspiration of a fleeing villain's body flood the rod the same way, the rod being already magnetized, as it were, by contact with the hands of the peasant, who was himself soaked first by the impression he took at the scene of the tragedy. I'm not yet examining how Jaques Aymar takes his impression; I'll cover that later, along with how the particles that make the impression pass from him into the Divining Rod — and I expect even the most demanding readers will be satisfied on that point.

2. Just as the vapors and fumes rising vertically from springs and mines, soaking into his rod, make it dip straight down above them — so the particles of perspiration likewise soak into the same rod and make it turn along the criminal's trail, where they have stayed lodged, and where they form a kind of column like the ones we observed over springs and mines.

You can see from this that nature behaves the same way in the Divining Rod's movement and dip over treasure, over springs, over gold and silver mines, and over the trail of criminals — it turns because of the vapors, fumes, and particles given off by all these different things. And this explains how Jaques Aymar, having first taken his impression in the cellar where the murder was committed, could follow those villains for so long: his rod, soaked first with the criminals' particles, stopped turning whenever he strayed from the track they had left along their route. Just as an iron rod hung on a pivot and set moving by a good magnet stops moving once it leaves the whirlwind of magnetic matter that makes up that magnet's sphere of activity.

This also explains how, among the prisoners at Beaucaire, he picked out the Hunchback and recognized him as the culprit he was after. There is around every person a whirlwind of particles exhaled by perspiration, as there is around a magnet a whirlwind of magnetic matter. Jaques Aymar, having been penetrated by the criminals' particles, couldn't take in another person's without noticing the change of sensation that would come over him. And if a good dog never abandons the track of the animal it's pursuing for another — because the heated animal acts more vividly on its sense of smell — how much more exact will a man of exquisite sensitivity be, when he adds attention and reasoning to it?

To put it more mechanically, I turn again to the magnet. When you have magnetized a knife by stroking it from a magnet's north pole to its south, you don't easily disrupt the flow of magnetic spirits it received by then stroking it the opposite way, south to north; you'd have to pass the magnet over the knife several times to erase the first impression. By the same logic, here's how the rod turns on a thief or a killer: around such people there is a whirlwind, a cloud, of matter perspired in extraordinary quantity through fear — matter that never leaves these criminals — and the rod, entering this whirlwind of perspired particles, is penetrated by it and tips so as to lie parallel to them.

Having spoken to the mind and the imagination, I should now speak to the eyes — to help readers who aren't used to philosophical reasoning, and who only grasp truths laid out for their senses. That's the purpose of the following figure, where you see Jaques Aymar armed with his Divining Rod, searching for veins of metal or springs of water. In front of him, and under his feet, you can see the particles rising from above the mines, or from above the streams hidden in the earth, and how this subtle matter penetrates him and passes from him into the rod, making it dip.

[Here the original contains a full-page woodcut engraving: a man in period dress holding the forked rod before him, surrounded by billowing clouds of corpuscles rising from the ground.]

Now, to use the language of the Schools of philosophy: the particles — both the ones passing from the man's hands into the rod, and the ones rising as vapor over springs, as exhalations over mines, and as columns of perspired matter on the footsteps of fleeing criminals — are the proximate efficient cause of the Divining Rod's movement and dip.

That is my system, and I think the better of it for being the simpler — which makes it the more conformable to the laws of nature, which does nothing useless. I reduce it to the mechanism of the magnetized iron rod's dip, which should make it all the more plausible, because nature has only one way of acting in everything it does, as M. Gassendi recognized very well: "ideo ipsis competit generalis familiarisque rebus naturae omnibus agendi, & patiendi modus." [Physic. sect. 1, bk. 6, ch. 14, p. 450: For there belongs to them the general and familiar mode of acting and being acted upon common to all things of nature.] It's no bad method, then, to explain what seems most surprising and marvelous in the Divining Rod by the most familiar effects of magnetic dip — whose whole workings are so well understood today — and it surely deserves preference over every other way of philosophizing. For as M. Gassendi adds, we shouldn't go imagining that the rarest, most obscure, most impenetrable effects of sympathy are produced by some other arrangement of organs, by some more intricate cause, than what nature works every day through the most common and visible springs: "sed non videtur existimandum alia ratione id peragi, quam quae soleat magis familiaribus effectibus intervenire." [But it does not seem that this should be thought to be accomplished by any other means than that which is wont to intervene in more familiar effects.]

And Father Kircher the Jesuit, speaking of how to untangle the cause of the most surprising effects, says you must first assume there is only one key to the sanctuary of nature's marvels — and that no one should imagine he knows anything about natural causes until he has found the unity of that key, the one that opens the spring of countless different effects: "Naturae clavis una est, quam is solus [tenet], qui in materiis dissimillimis unitatem complectitur." [magnetic. natur. reg. sect. 1, ch. 3, p. 12: There is one key of Nature, held by him alone who grasps the unity in the most dissimilar matters.]

Finally, I explain the hazel rod's sympathy with metals, and with the other things it dips over, by the outflow of subtle matter that escapes from all bodies and spreads through the air; and Father Schott the Jesuit declares this is the right way to untangle the effects that until now have been chalked up to occult qualities: "causam sympathiae ... oriri plerumque ... ex emissione tenuiorum quarumdam exhalationum, quas diffundi a multis corporibus certum est." [Mag. sympath. part 4, bk. 4, syntag. 1, ch. 3, p. 369: The cause of sympathy arises for the most part from the emission of certain finer exhalations, which it is certain are diffused from many bodies.]

Having laid out my method and backed it with reason and with the testimony of those who have applied themselves most to physics, I understand perfectly well that however simple my system is, not everyone will be satisfied with it until I first demonstrate that there really are vapors over the waters, exhalations over the mines, and a subtle perspired matter on the path where a thief or murderer has passed; and that these vapors, exhalations, and particles of unnoticed perspiration are fine enough, and forceful enough, to penetrate Jaques Aymar's pores and to impress on the rod the rapid movement we see when it turns. I hope to put all this in such plain evidence that it will count as rigorously demonstrated among those who know what demonstration means in physics.

[End of Chapter Five]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER SIX

Vapors rise over underground watercourses, and these make the Divining Rod dip.

Few things in the world are more useful than springs, yet people agree neither on the material nor on the manner by which nature produces them. We know so little of what goes on inside the earth that it's no surprise the physicists are so divided about where springs come from. The reader may enjoy seeing the different opinions the most celebrated philosophers hold on this curious question — all the more because it leads us toward the vapors I need to discuss.

1. Aristotle believed springs originated from the air. Here is his hypothesis: vapors rise from deep in the earth, meet vault-like rock formations at the tops of mountains, thicken into droplets, and condense into water there, as in the head of a still; this water then flows out at the foot or on the slope of the mountains. [Aristot. bk. 1, meteor. ch. 13.] This view is rejected, because it's unlikely the earth contains enough air to supply water for so many springs and such large rivers.

2. Others say springs originate from rainwater, which seeps through the pores of the earth and the cracks in rocks, gathers in underground hollows as in reservoirs, then flows through underground channels and emerges to spread over the land. This is the view of the late M. Mariotte of the Royal Academy of Sciences, in his Treatise on the movement of waters, which M. de la Hire of the same Academy had printed, with much care and labor, after that learned man's death. Here is how M. Mariotte puts it: "The fallen rains penetrate the earth by the little channels they find there... What falls on the hills and mountains, having sunk through the surface — especially where the soil is light and mixed with pebbles and tree roots — often meets clay or unbroken rock, along which it flows, unable to get through, until, reaching the foot of the mountain, it comes out into the air and forms springs." [Part 1, second discourse, p. 19.] M. Mariotte defends this opinion so cleverly and so learnedly that Aristotle, who once couldn't accept it, would have preferred it to his own had he read it with the graces this able Academician gave it.

Father Kircher attacks it too. Generally speaking, he says, it isn't true that all springs come from rainwater, since it doesn't rain on the mountains of Gilboa, as the sacred text says, nor in many places inside and outside the torrid zone where springs are nevertheless found. [Kirker. Mund. subterran. bk. 5, ch. 1, p. 249.] Add to this that most rain runs off in torrents and rivers and makes its way to the sea; and however much the earth soaks up during the longest rains, in the end it penetrates no deeper than ten feet: "Pluvia non ultra decem pedum profunditatem humectat terram" [Rain does not moisten the earth beyond a depth of ten feet], says Varenius in his Geography, bk. 1, ch. 16, propos. 5, p. 235. Seneca wouldn't have adopted M. Mariotte's opinion either: he says the rain is absorbed in the earth and entirely used up before it can sink very deep: "Omnis humor intra primam crustam consumitur, nec in inferiora descendit." [Quaest. natural. bk. 3: All moisture is consumed within the first crust, and does not descend to the lower parts.]

3. The third opinion, the one I'd prefer, is that springs originate from the water of the sea, or of the rivers, carried by underground conduits into the very heart of the mountains and to all the places where we see sources. So we have an admirable circulation: after bringing the springs from the sea through the veins of the earth, it returns the waters to the sea by the channel of the rivers — according to the words of Scripture: "All the rivers run into the sea, yet the sea overflows not: unto the place from whence the rivers come, thither they return again, to flow once more." [Ecclesiastes 1:7.] Besides agreeing with holy Scripture, this system is highly probable. You'll easily be persuaded that the sea supplies water to all the springs if you consider that some springs are salt; that some rise and fall with the ebb and flow of the tide; that most sources never run dry; and that the rivers, which are accumulations of them, pour into the sea continually without making it any fuller — which would certainly happen otherwise, since we know of more than a thousand great rivers emptying into the sea. So these same waters must leave it again by underground channels: "Plures quam mille fluvii in mari se exonerant; & majores ex illis tanta copia, ut aqua illa, quam per totum annum emittunt in mare, superet totam tellurem." [Varenius, loc. cit., p. 258: More than a thousand rivers discharge themselves into the sea; and the greater among them with such abundance, that the water they send into the sea through a whole year would exceed the whole earth.]

Father Paul Casati the Jesuit has not only declared for this opinion but also explains, rather ingeniously, how springs come from the sea. He first assumes there is a central fire in the earth, and that the earth has veins and conduits; then he adds that this central fire makes the seawater boil in its abysses and turns it to vapor. The upper vapors, pushed continually and forced to rise by those below, are condensed again by the cold near the surface of the earth into water, which finally follows the slope of the mountains and gives us the sources we see flowing from them. And as if to answer M. Mariotte's calculation — which reckoned how a year's rainfall can supply everything the springs and rivers pour over the earth in a year — he says: anyone curious enough to roughly calculate how much water the rivers carry to the sea in a year, and then to work out how much must have entered it over more than sixty centuries, would surely find it should already have flooded the whole face of the earth several times over. Yet that has never happened — proof that the sea discharges, through the underground conduits that form the springs, as much water as it receives from the rivers. Here is Father Casati himself: "Quid igitur supersit: quam ut infusa per telluris venas aqua, ex subjecti in centro ignis calore attenuata in vaporem, sibi per rimas, quas invenit, exitum quaerat in superiora, donec demum vi frigoris, & urgentibus posterioribus halitibus iterum vapor constipetur, concrescat in aquam." [tract. 3, de Igne, p. 724: What then remains, but that the water poured through the veins of the earth, rarefied into vapor by the heat of the fire seated at the center, seeks an exit upward through the cracks it finds, until at last, by the force of cold and the pressing of the breaths behind, the vapor is again compacted and condenses into water.]

II. You can see from this discussion of the origin of springs that the subject bears too directly on the vapors for me to skip so agreeable a point of physics; and what I've set down here quietly prepares the mind to accept these vapors — which according to some physicists are the raw material of springs, and which I hold to exist above underground watercourses. I've met people of merit, even of learning, who took up arms whenever these vapors were mentioned, and denounced them as the most incredible paradoxes. We'll show later how all bodies, even the hardest and most solid, ceaselessly give off a fine matter that detaches from them and spreads through the air. Metals, marble, even the diamond are not exempt from these perpetual emanations, which make inevitable breaches in the structure of the firmest bodies and cause the continual wearing away of all material things. If these emanations occur in solid bodies, whose parts are tightly bound and hold strongly together, how much more must they occur in bodies fluid and liquid, whose parts are in constant motion!

So although the physicists disagree about the origin of springs, they all agree that there are vapors over the places where hidden watercourses lie. In fact this follows necessarily from their different hypotheses, if you look closely. The men called Aquileges — water-seekers or well-finders — who have shed some light on how to find sources, have all listed the vapors seen over certain spots in the morning around sunrise as a sure sign of an underground watercourse. And so it must be. Since vapors are particles of water that the underground fires, or the heat of the fermentations going on endlessly in the earth, have detached from the rest and lifted into the air, there has to be water at the place these vapors come from. Every day we see experiments confirming that heat turns water to vapor.

Bacon, Chancellor of England, says in his Natural History that there are lakes and wells, like the Avernus of Campania, from which vapors rise so noxious that birds flying over them drop dead, and people who linger too long nearby die as if plague-struck: "Lacus & putei, ut Avernus, volucres supervolantes pestiferis exhalationibus enecare dicuntur, & homines diutius astantes." [Hist. Natur. cent. 10: Lakes and wells, like Avernus, are said to kill with their pestiferous exhalations the birds flying over and the men standing too long beside them.]

But these vapors rise not only from waters lying open on the surface, but also from those whose course nature keeps hidden inside the mountains. Pliny knew these moist vapors that rise where watercourses run, since he counts them among the surest signs that a source lies at the spot where they appear: "Certior multo nebulosa exhalatio est, ante ortum solis longius intuentibus, quod ex edito quidam speculantur, prona terra matutino attingente." [Hist. Natural. bk. 31, ch. 3: Much surer is the misty exhalation, to those looking from a distance before sunrise — which some observe from a height, lying prone upon the earth in the morning.]

Vitruvius had the most thorough knowledge of these vapors seen undulating over places where water flows underground. To find the spots where there is water, he says, you should, a little before sunrise, lie face-down with your chin resting on the ground and look out across the country: with your chin steadied, your sight won't rise higher than it should, but will run level; and if in some place you see a moist vapor rising in waves, dig there, for this doesn't happen where there is no water: "Sin autem non profluunt, quaerenda sub terra sunt capita, & colligenda; quae sic erunt experiunda, uti procumbatur in dentes, antequam sol exortus fuerit, in locis quibus erit quaerendum, & in terra mento collocato, & fulto, prospiciantur hae regiones. Sic enim non errabit excelsius quam oporteat visus, cum erit immotum mentum: sed ad libratam altitudinem in regionibus certa definitione designabit. Tunc in quibus locis videbuntur HUMORES se concrispantes, & in aera surgentes, ibi fodiatur: non enim in sicco loco hoc signum potest fieri." [Vitr. bk. 8, ch. 1: But if waters do not flow openly, their heads must be sought underground and collected; which shall be tried thus — that one lie face-down before the sun has risen, in the places where the search is to be made, and with the chin set and propped upon the ground, survey those regions. For thus the sight will not wander higher than it ought, the chin being motionless, but will mark out a level height in the country with sure definition. Then in whatever places MOISTURES are seen curling and rising into the air, there let digging be done: for this sign cannot occur in a dry place.]

M. Perrault of the Royal Academy of Sciences, who translated and annotated Vitruvius, notes on this passage that Palladius describes in the same way how to locate sources by the vapors — but insists on taking precautions so as not to be misled.

Theodoric, King of the Ostrogoths, has it said to the water-seekers — through the pen of Cassiodorus, his Secretary of State — that over places where there is water, fine vapors are seen rising and forming a kind of column in the air: "addunt etiam in columnae speciem conspici quendam tenuissimum fumum." [Theodoric. Epist. 53. Cassiodor. variar. bk. 3, p. 58: They add also that a certain very thin smoke is seen in the form of a column.]

Sidonius Apollinaris, writing to a friend who welcomed men of letters and advanced them in the world, compares him to the sun's rays, which, by lifting with their heat the moist vapors formed by watercourses hidden in the veins of the earth, expose, so to speak, what nature pretended to keep secret. You can see from this that these vapors and moist atoms were a well-recognized thing, since Sidonius Apollinaris mentions them in a letter to a friend — a form into which one admits hardly anything but familiar matters: "Sic ingenia producis, ut solet aquam terrae visceribus absconditam per atomos bibulos radius extrahere solaris? Cujus lucis aculeo non sola penetratur aut arena subtilis, aut humus fissilis: sed si saxei montis oppressu fontium conditorum vena celetur, aperit arcanum liquentis elementi secretorum caelestium natura violentior." [bk. 9, Epist. 10: Do you so bring forth talents, as the solar ray is wont to draw out, through thirsty atoms, the water hidden in the bowels of the earth? By the sting of whose light not only fine sand or cleavable soil is penetrated; but if a vein of hidden springs is concealed under the weight of a rocky mountain, the nature of heavenly secrets, more forcible still, lays open the mystery of the liquid element.]

Father Kircher the Jesuit likewise says the well-finder should, in the morning at sunrise, stretch out full length on the ground to watch for any trace of the undulating vapors that form a light little cloud in the air; and if he spots anything of the kind, digging should be done at the spot, because there is certainly water there: "Aquilex mane orientem versus, ante solis tamen ortum, pronus in terram prostratus, observet utrum alicubi humores in tenuem nubeculam se crispent, & tremulo motu aerem feriant: quod ubi comperit, fodiat de aqua condita securus." [Mund. subterran. bk. 5, ch. 2, p. 266: Let the water-seeker in the morning, facing east, yet before sunrise, lying prone upon the earth, observe whether anywhere the moistures curl into a thin little cloud and strike the air with a trembling motion: which when he has found, let him dig, assured of hidden water.]

Father Jean François the Jesuit, in his excellent treatise The art and management of waters, says of these vapors: according to Palladius, in the month of August, when the pores of the earth are open and give free passage to the vapors, you should look with your line of sight grazing the ground, and watch for trembling fumes rising in a swirl from some spot; and that is where you must dig to find the water that feeds these rising vapors — the other places, having no water, being unable to produce any. [ch. 1, p. 6.]

Father Gaspar Schott the Jesuit says: we very often see, plainly enough, vapors climbing into the air from damp places, though we can't always tell whether they are exhalations coming from some earthy matter instead: "Vaporem non obscure videmus saepe e locis humentibus, atque ex ipsis terrae glebis sole splendente, ascendere in aera; quamquam non distincte vaporem ab exhalatione discernamus." [bk. 11, Mirabil. Meteor. ch. 1, §1, p. 1182: We see vapor, not obscurely, often rising into the air from damp places, and from the very clods of earth when the sun shines; although we do not distinctly discern vapor from exhalation.]

Father Déchales the Jesuit, describing how to locate sources and reporting Vitruvius's method, ends with this: dig the earth at the places over which vapors are seen rising in waves into the air, because it's a sign that a vein of water lies beneath: "Tum in quibus locis videbuntur humores se concrispantes, & in aera surgentes, ibi fodiatur... ex vapore enim se attollente judicium fertur de vena intus latente." [Mund. Mathemat. vol. 2, de fontib. nat. proposit. 16, p. 100: Then in whatever places moistures are seen curling and rising into the air, there let digging be done... for from the vapor lifting itself, judgment is made of the vein lying hidden within.]

These vapors are so universally accepted, so beyond dispute, that Tostatus uses them to explain the words of verse 20 of the first chapter of Genesis, which seem to say that God formed from water not only the fish but the birds too — as Saint Basil, Saint Ambrose, and several other Fathers teach. To support this view, Tostatus says there are two things in water: 1. a thick, heavy part, which was suitable material for forming fish; 2. another, lighter part, which rises into the air and goes off as vapor, as you can see over boiling water. He then adds that this second, subtle part of the water suited the nature of birds, which lift themselves and fly in the air: "Conveniebat aquae, quod ex ea aves producerentur, quia in aqua est aliquid crassum, & ponderosum, quod ad naturam piscium competit: aliud autem est subtilius resolutum in modum vaporis, quod elevatur in altum sicut apparet in aqua bullienti; ad hanc partem subtilem pertinerent aves, & ideo elevarentur in altum." [Quaest. 225 in Genes.: It was fitting to water that birds should be produced from it, because in water there is something thick and heavy, which suits the nature of fishes; but there is another part, more subtly resolved in the manner of vapor, which rises on high, as appears in boiling water; to this subtle part the birds would belong, and therefore they would be lifted on high.] That is why the early Christians ate, in Lent, not only fish but fowl, claiming that according to Moses, God had drawn both from the water — as Socrates reports: "alii cum piscibus volucres etiam manducant, easque ex aqua, ut est apud Moysem, nasci asserunt." [Hist. bk. 5, ch. 22: Others eat fowl together with fish, asserting that these too, as it stands in Moses, are born of the water.]

III. It's not enough to have shown these vapors exist over the watercourses; we must explain, 1. how they get into the hazel rod; 2. how they can make it tip toward the earth.

1. You'll have no trouble believing that the particles of vapor enter the Divining Rod once you consider how easily the parts of water itself work their way into plants and trees. Everyone has noticed how the branches of trees standing at the edge of springs and along rivers lean toward the water. This surely comes from the watery particles that penetrate them, weigh them down, and align them, as far as may be, with the little columns of vapor sometimes seen rising above the water's surface.

EXPERIMENT. Plants are known to draw their main nourishment and growth from water. Last summer we saw a very pleasant experiment proving the point. A little sprig of balm — otherwise called mint — was put into a vial full of water; not only did this sprig, just four fingers high, take root, but it grew to a foot tall, put out many branches, flowered, and finally produced seed in that water, just as it would have in open ground.

EXPERIMENT. Van Helmont performed a very fine experiment that proves admirably the affinity between the pores of certain trees, above all, and the particles that detach from water. I took, he says, a great earthen vessel into which I put 200 pounds of earth well dried in the oven, and watered it with rainwater. After this preparation I planted in it a willow trunk weighing 5 pounds. At the end of five years this tree, which had grown enormously, weighed 169 pounds and about three ounces. I added rainwater, or distilled water, whenever it needed watering; I took great care to cover the vessel with sheets of tin pierced with many little holes, to keep dust from falling in. Note further that I did not weigh all the leaves that fell in abundance over four autumns. Finally I dried the earth as before, and found my 200 pounds of earth again, perhaps two ounces short. So 164 pounds of wood, bark, and root had been produced from water alone. "Librae ergo 164 ligni, corticum, & radicum, ex sola aqua surrexerant." [Joan. Baptist. Van Helmont, Complex. atq. mist. Element. figm. p. 68, num. 30: Therefore 164 pounds of wood, bark, and roots had arisen from water alone.] To establish the affinity between plant fibers and the imperceptible parts of water, this philosopher points to the plants that always float on the waters and take no nourishment except what the water gives them: "Quin etiam, natantes herbae aquam tegunt, solo aquae fucco potissimum contentae." [Van Helmont, Imag. ferment. impraegnat. mass. semin. p. 72, num. 31: Nay more, floating plants cover the water, content chiefly with the juice of the water alone.]

Anyone who understands how the vapors in the soil climb into plants to nourish them won't doubt that the vapors spread in the air over springs can work their way into the Divining Rod — since they are parts of water, of the same nature as the whole, and since for both effects nature has one and the same mechanism.

The physicists know that plants have woody fibers running lengthwise, like so many pipes, from root to tip; and that it's through these pipes, dipping at their lower end into the juices of the soil, that nourishment is delivered to the whole plant. I've even observed with a fairly good microscope that the wood of hazel, alder, and beech — the woods usually used for seeking watercourses — looks like nothing but a bundle of fibers laid side by side, as if built of many little glass tubes. As for beech in particular, I've found by experiment that it's so thoroughly made of these little tubes that if you soak one end of a thick beech branch two feet long in water, you can easily force out the water it has absorbed, in little air bubbles, by blowing fairly hard at the other end. This wood must therefore be extremely porous. That's what makes it the fitter for the Divining Rod; and that's what makes it the easier to burn, because the fire finds in it those little spaces, those gaps, into which it easily works its way. Which leads me to guess that boxwood and ebony, which don't catch fire so readily because of their hardness, would be useless in the search for sources.

The physicists also say that the soil's juices enter the pores of the roots through the stirring heat of the fermentations they undergo, when rain, together with the heat of the sun or of the underground fires, dissolves the various salts spread through the earth's outer crust. These juices then have to climb into the plants, because the weight of the air pushes them up, and because they make a passage more easily through the plants' pores than through the air itself.

Here is the application. When Jaques Aymar walks into a cloud of vapors hanging in the air over a spring, I say these vapors — pressed by the air weighing on them, and pushed by the vapors coming up behind — are forced to work their way into the pores of the hazel branch, and rush into it, the way water long held back by a dike pours through fast once it finds an outlet. These vapors are certainly heavier than air, since they rise so little above the ground while the air floats above them, according to this principle of hydrostatics: "corpus humido levius, posita paritate molis, non mergitur." [A body lighter than the liquid, bulk for bulk, does not sink.]

2. It remains to explain how these vapors, entering the Divining Rod, make it dip over the springs. Recall what I said on page 129 about the dip of the magnetized iron rod, which everywhere on earth takes its direction from the path the magnetic effluvia keep as they circle the globe. If these particles move beneath the equator in a line parallel to the earth's axis, the magnetized iron rod there will lie parallel to that same axis. If this magnetic matter traces at Paris a line inclined at 65 degrees, the magnetized iron rod there dips at 65 degrees. Finally, if at the poles these little bodies rise vertically out of the earth, the magnetized iron rod will dip straight down over the pole, as if continuing the earth's axis. The same thing happens to the divining rod: it lines itself up with the paths traced by the vapors rising above the springs. These rise vertically out of the earth; so by the laws of magnetism, the rod must dip straight down, to lie parallel with the columns the vapors form as they climb into the air. And these lines of vapor are like little chains pulling the rod and holding it down, just as the magnetic matter does with the dip needle or rod.

I didn't invent this vertical motion by which the vapors rise in columns. I found it in Cassiodorus, who says flatly that it's a commonplace among the well-finders that the moist vapors mount above the sources in the form of columns in the air: "Addunt etiam in columnae speciem conspici quendam tenuissimum fumum."

This mechanism — moist vapors making the Divining Rod dip over springs by exactly the same laws that magnetism follows in the dip of the magnetized iron rod — must be thoroughly grasped, because it is the single rule I'll use to explain the rod's dip over mines, over buried treasure, and over the trail of fleeing criminals as well.

To help the imagination picture this dip — whose imperceptible agents our outer senses can't detect — turn to the visible experiment in which the vapors of mercury, spread through the air of a room, regather from every side to become running, liquid mercury again, as before the evaporation. Or, if you prefer: just as the little smoking particles that linger a moment at the wick of a snuffed candle serve as a vehicle to carry back to it the flame of a burning candle brought near, relighting it — so we may suppose the same thing happens around the Divining Rod in the hands of a man who has passed into it, by direct contact, a little of the water-vapor he was himself first soaked with at the site of the source. That small load of moist particles already inside the rod draws in abundance the ones scattered in the air. They gather and reunite there because of how easily they slip into the rod — whose pores, already shaped in plants by nature's design to fit the figure of water particles, have besides been freshly opened by the particles that the unnoticed perspiration of Jaques Aymar's hands has already pushed into it.

It's the same way a mass of water stirred by the wind spreads like a torrent that soon carves itself a wide passage, provided it can find in the sand one little spot where the ground is already wet: the principle of homogeneity — likeness of nature — draws the waters toward that spot, and they immediately flood the whole neighborhood. This is so clear that I can't imagine the application won't leap to the eye — above all if you remember that vapors are liquid, like water itself; and that the Divining Rod, in Jaques Aymar's hands, becomes by his touch moistened with that imperceptible liquid, which attracts the liquid I have shown to be spread in the air over the watercourses.

[End of Chapter Six]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER SEVEN

Exhalations, or fumes, rise over every kind of mine, and over treasures hidden in the earth, and these make the Divining Rod dip.

Metals — gold, silver, copper, and so on — are hard bodies that are malleable and fusible: they stretch under the hammer, and they turn liquid in the fire of the smelter. They are generated in underground places called mines. To hide nothing: the men so eager for them nevertheless don't know how, or from what, nature forms them inside the earth — at least, nothing is known of it except by guesswork. If physics had anything solidly established about the formation of metals, the philosophers wouldn't have split into so many camps on the question.

I. The Peripatetics and the Chymists have long battled over the principles that go into the generation of metals. A celebrated philosopher says it wouldn't be hard to reconcile them, since at bottom they agree and only quarrel about words — and they'd soon have settled among themselves what idea to attach to those words, if these philosophers had patience enough to listen to one another: "forte in rebus conveniunt, verbis discrepant" [perhaps they agree in things, and differ in words], says M. Duhamel, Physic. part 3, quaest. ult. p. 546.

Aristotle says metals are composed of vapors and exhalations [bk. 3, meteorolog. ch. ult.]. Agricola maintains it's a refined mixture of earth and water [bk. 1, ch. 21, de natura fossilium]. The Chymists, who are men of the trade, hold that sulphur and mercury form the matter of metals. Albert the Great is of this view, and calls sulphur the father of metals and mercury their mother: "sulphur est quasi pater, & argentum vivum mater metallorum." [Praefat. metallic. bk. 4: Sulphur is as it were the father, and quicksilver the mother, of metals.] Father Kircher the Jesuit says sulphur and mercury aren't enough — a salt is needed to give the metal hardness and solidity: "materiam proximam metallorum ... vaporem, & exhalationem sulphureo-sale-mercurialem dicimus." [mund. subterran. bk. 10, ch. 1, p. 182: We declare the proximate matter of metals to be a sulphureo-saline-mercurial vapor and exhalation.] M. Descartes believes the innermost part of the earth is metal, and that what the miners dig out is only like a trickle of water branching from its source, or a tree branch parting from the trunk. M. Régis takes another line: metals are composed of several long, branching constituent parts, whose different sizes and shapes account for all the diversity found among the different kinds of metals. [Physiq. bk. 4, part 3, ch. 4, p. 371.]

Plainly, most of the philosophers who take sulphur, mercury, salts, water, mineral oil, juices, fumes, and exhalations for the matter of metals are only giving us the immediate material, not the first principle — since we'd still need to know what this sulphur, this salt, this mercury, etc., are themselves made of. It's as if I told someone that bronze is an alloy of metals, mainly copper melted with a portion of tin: I wouldn't teach him much if he didn't already know what copper and tin are. M. Régis, to be fair, philosophizes more rigorously; it can't be denied he has given the first matter of metals. And if we barely know what metals are made of, we know no better how they're formed.

Still, I'd most willingly join the camp of those who believe the underground fires are the efficient cause of the generation of metals. These fires set the mineral matters and vapors in motion and shoot them like little cannonballs toward the surface; the juices then cool, congeal, and form what we call metal. The most volatile parts of these juices, unable to be pinned down so easily in the veins of the earth, break free, keep going, rise into the air, and only stop when the column of air pressing down on them by its weight has gradually stripped away all the impetus they received from the underground fires.

As for these underground fires, they can't reasonably be doubted. They announce themselves, and make themselves known, in too many places for their existence to be denied. They make themselves felt in the burning springs and the hot baths. I'm aware the heat of the hot springs could be attributed to fermentations in the earth, with no need of the central fire — that's even an opinion M. Charas of the Royal Academy of Sciences seems to want to establish, on the strength of an accidental experiment in his laboratory, reported in the Academy's Memoirs [p. 158].
