But the fermentation explained there doesn't destroy the central fire at all; I'm not even sure it isn't necessarily presupposed, to set the minerals and juices in motion, pushing and mixing them with the water in the underground channels it passes through. If you can't understand how the underground fires keep burning forever, neither do I see how the juices and minerals that produce the fermentations — and so the hot springs — will keep replenishing themselves forever without running out. The difficulty is exactly equal on both sides, if I'm not mistaken. For the rest, I don't think the central fire needs feeding any more than the sun does, which never wastes away.

And might we not think of this central fire what Lactantius says of the fire God's justice kindled to burn the wicked eternally? He declares that fire is very different from the one we use for so many needs of life. Our household fire is fluid and flowing; it can't sustain itself, and goes out the moment it has no fuel to fasten on and devour. But that divine fire, into which the demon was hurled with his angels, is a fire that sustains itself, without any nourishment. That fire is pure, because it depends on no outside matter; it is liquid like water. "At ille ignis divinus per seipsum semper vivit, ac viget, sine ullis alimentis ... est purus ac liquidus, & in aquae modum liquidus." [Lactant. bk. 7, Divin. Instit. ch. 21: But that divine fire lives always by itself, and flourishes without any nourishment ... it is pure and liquid, and liquid after the manner of water.]

Nothing, then, stops us from regarding this central fire — which nature uses for so many marvelous generations inside the earth — as a liquid fire, a fixed fire, a standing fire; or, if you like, a pond of fire, which needs fuel to subsist no more than a pond of water does. So philosophizes Father Casati the Jesuit in his third Dissertation de Igne, p. 71.

As for M. Charas's point on page 157 — that if the heat of the hot waters came from underground fires, you'd find scorch marks at the sources of these waters, which haven't been observed — the reasoning may well not hold up. If it's as certain as it is probable that spring water is ordinarily filtered through the pores of the earth, that percolation would never let it carry up to us the scorch marks one would like to see before believing in underground fires.

The great number of volcanoes — mountains that vomit flames and ash, observable in so many parts of the earth — are so many further proofs of the truth and reality of this central fire. Mount Gibel [Etna] in Sicily; mount Hecla in Iceland; mount Vesuvius in Campania; others in the Moluccas, the Philippines, in Peru; finally the mountain near Guatemala in America, from which chunks of rock sometimes shoot out with the violence of a cannonball — these often give the inhabitants of those countries fatal proof that there really are horrible fires in the bowels of the earth.

I can't get over the fact that there are still people who believe the generation of metals deep in the earth is an effect of the sun's heat. That's an old daydream of the ancient philosophers, who didn't consider that if the heaviest rains penetrate no deeper than 10 feet, there's no reason to think the sun's rays reach much farther. The mine workers — who deserve belief sooner than people who have never gone down a shaft — assure us that the deeper you go into the earth, the more plainly you feel the heat increase. Jean-Baptiste Morin says that, descending into a mine in July, he found the upper section very cold down to a depth of about 480 feet; below that, as he went down, the heat increased so much that the workmen could only labor in the depths completely naked. [Relatio de locis subterran. p. 131.] Joannes Beguinus reports the same of the mines of Hungary. At the summer solstice he went down a silver mine about fifteen hundred cubits deep, five hundred paces from Schemnitz; and he learned from the workmen — who because of the extreme heat worked stark naked — that mineral vapors are often seen rising from the center of the earth, which put out their lamps and would suffocate the men themselves if they didn't withdraw at once. "Cum enim superiori aestate in Hungaria, medio milliari in Schemnitz, in argenti fodinam mille quingentos circiter cubitos profundam descendissem; a fossoribus ejus, qui ob summam minerae aestum vestibus & ipso indusio exuti laborabant, didici: Vapores minerales a centro terrae frequenter sursum ferri, eorumque lucernas, & ipsosmet, ni subito recedant, extinguere." [Tyrocin. Chimic. bk. 2, ch. 14.] Surely it would be a joke to credit the sun with those terrible blasts of heat that sometimes suffocate workmen at the bottom of a mine fifteen hundred cubits deep. But fine — credit them, if you like, to the sun, or to fermentations in the earth: either hypothesis leads equally to the conclusion that there must be fumes and exhalations over the mines, since the volatile salts and the finest particles of the metals would be set moving just as well by either of those two agents as by the underground fires.

II. And indeed, everyone who has written about minerals with any care and any solid knowledge has mentioned these exhalations or fumes — the very ones to which we attribute the Divining Rod's movement and dip over the mines. How could they have forgotten them? The eyes themselves can spot them easily enough in the morning, at sunrise.

Pliny, on silver mines, says a vapor rises from them that no animal — dogs above all — can stand: "Odor ex argenti fodinis inimicus omnibus animalibus, sed maxime canibus." [Hist. natur. bk. 33, ch. 6: The smell from silver mines is hostile to all animals, but most of all to dogs.]

Francis Bacon, Chancellor of England — who can be ranked with the greatest philosophers of antiquity, and who was the first in modern times to grasp the necessity of experiments for securing our reasoning and perfecting natural history — knew of these malignant fumes from the mines. The mines, he says, exhale deadly vapors that kill the workmen, whether by suffocating or by poisoning them: "Saepius eructant fodinae vapores mortiferos, seu suffocatio sit, seu venenata mineralis natura." [Hist. natur. cent. 10, n. 918, p. 308.]

Tomaso Garzoni, author of the book la piazza universale, says the mountains containing mines are recognized by the fumes and exhalations they ordinarily send up into the air: "i monti che contengono minere, sogliono mandare fuori qualche essalatione, o fumosità." [Discor. 70, p. 245.]

Joseph Maria Maraviglia, Professor of Moral Philosophy in the College of Padua, explains very well how the underground fires push fumes, vapors, and exhalations into the air without ceasing — the raw material of winds, clouds, rains, and the other meteors. After all, he says, experience shows that in the seasons when the sun cannot warm the depths of the earth, there is still a heat in its abysses, which must be natural to it. This is proved further by all the volcanoes — all those flame-throwing mountains; by the hot springs; by the boiling fountains; and by certain vapors, little cloud-like puffs, that sailors sometimes see rising from the bottom of the sea, never failing to brew winds and storms soon after. From which he concludes there must be, beneath the waters of the sea, a heat that doesn't come from the sun, and which must be acknowledged as the cause of these fumes: "Ex quibus perspicuum fit alium quam solarem calorem infra maris fundum vigere, quo vis illa tanta halituum excernatur, sursumque propellatur." [Proteus Ethico-politic. leg. 11, p. 57: From which it is plain that a heat other than the solar flourishes beneath the bottom of the sea, by which that so great force of breaths is secreted and driven upward.]

Among the experiments of the Royal Society of England is the extract of a letter that Doctor Edward Browne wrote specifically on the vapors found so abundantly in the mines of Hungary. We learn there that the effects of these metallic fumes are so terrible and so deadly to the workmen that they are sometimes suffocated by them. He adds that in those underground galleries 28 men were found suffocated at the same time by these spirits exhaling from the metallic matters; that these malignant exhalations are very often poisonous; and that the problem is only fixed by tubes that pipe good air into those underground places and drive out the bad. He also mentions a well 900 feet deep in which the workmen were extraordinarily tormented by these fumes rising from the earth. "Chemnitii mihi referebant 28 viros interiisse eodem tempore in 4 cuniculis, 7 in singulis; & in fodiendo puteo Leopoldi, qui 150 orgyias profundus est, multum vexabantur vaporibus." [Acta Philosoph. June 1669, p. 147: At Chemnitz they told me that 28 men perished at the same time in 4 galleries, 7 in each; and in digging the well of Leopold, which is 150 fathoms deep, they were much troubled by vapors.]

The curious can consult Agricola's book de re Metallica to see the machines used to draw this bad air out of the depths of the mines and replace it with purer, healthier air. We also have, in the Journal des Savants of March 23, 1682, a summary of these same remarks of M. Edward Browne on the exhalations of the gold and silver mines of Hungary. "M. Browne affirms that the gold and silver mines of that country exhale very thick and even very malignant vapors; that these vapors rise not only from muddy and damp places, but even from the driest parts of the mine; that there are damp spots in these mines, like sorts of sumps, where these vapors become extremely perceptible — almost palpable, so strong are they; that in his presence a man tried in vain four or five times to enter one of those places, because the lamp kept going out from the thickness of the vapors; that these vapors are sometimes so malignant they suffocate the workmen in a short time; that sometimes they only weaken them gradually and wear down their health; and finally that these very thick vapors and fumes are also found over the wells, and over the PITS DUG perpendicularly in the earth, and in the galleries or chambers cut into certain wells."

I'll mention here, in connection with this passage of the Journal des Savants, that M. Browne's remark about the fumes over the mines, the wells, and the pits dug in the earth is what suggested to me, seven or eight years ago, the physical reason for the movement of the Divining Rod — which I had watched workmen using to find iron-rich deposits in the mountains of the Alps. I then formed the system I'm now publishing, to which I've added or changed almost nothing of substance, though at that time I didn't yet know that this same rod dips over the corpses of murdered people, and over the very footsteps of fleeing criminals.

But in case anyone imagines it's only in Hungary that there are vapors and exhalations over the mines, note that these fumes serve the mineralist philosophers as signs for recognizing where metal veins lie. Georgius Agricola says in general that to find mines, you should watch for fumes rising over some spot of the mountains, because it's a sign that metals are hidden in the earth there: "Venae enim siccum expirant calidumque halitum." [For the veins breathe out a dry and hot exhalation.]

Father Kircher the Jesuit likewise counts these exhalations, often seen on mountain summits, as the seventeenth standard mark for confirming that mountains hold metals inside: "ex montium apicibus, in quibus plerumque vapores expirare solent, metallorum latentium indices sunt." [Mund. subterran. bk. 10, sect. 2, ch. 7, p. 200: From the peaks of mountains, where vapors are commonly wont to breathe out, are the signs of hidden metals.]

Caesius the Jesuit not only says these streams of fine matter, appearing like little clouds at certain spots on the mountains, are marks of metal veins in those places; he adds that these dry, hot fumes cause the barrenness that reigns over the mines, because they parch and kill the plants and trees there, burning them down to the roots: "Ea namque opera efficiunt calidi, & sicci venarum halitus, qui ne radicibus quidem arborum parcunt." [De mineralib. bk. 1, ch. 7, sect. 3, p. 124: For these effects are produced by the hot and dry breaths of the veins, which spare not even the roots of the trees.]

Joachim Becher, so famous for his excellent book Physica subterranea, takes the existence of these metallic fumes over the mines as so obvious that, without bothering to prove it, he goes straight to considering how these vapors move. He says first that these metallic particles rising from the mines are much finer and thinner at the surface than in the depths, because, filtered through the long series of pores they must pass before reaching the top of the mines, they leave the coarser parts below. That's why, the deeper you dig, the larger the veins and the fuller they are of that agreeable slime that gives men so much pleasure. Then he adds that these vapors always keep the same pattern of motion, from the center of the earth toward its circumference: "Ejusmodi vapores certum ordinem in motu suo a centro ad circumferentiam terrae observant." And in the following chapter he compares the circular movement of these metallic fumes to that of the vapors that heat lifts from the matter in the cucurbit toward the head of the still, and that the circulating tubes carry back down from the head into the cucurbit. He thereby illustrates how the exhalations coming from the depths of the mines return there by a perpetual circulation: "perpetua naturae circulatio." [Becher. Physic. subterran. bk. 1, sect. 5, ch. 6, pp. 97–98.]

Father Tylkowski, a Polish Jesuit, says flatly that if a sort of little cloud is seen always at the same spot on a mountain, it's a mark that there are metals inside. And he affirms that if in April and May vapors are seen at sunrise on a mountain under a clear sky, it's a sign of a quicksilver mine: "Metalli intra terram signum est, si loco nebula incumbat ordinarie ... In Aprili, & Maio sereno caelo vapores in montibus sub auroram instar nebulae indicium sunt mercurii." [Philosoph. Curiosa, vol. 7, sect. 8, ch. 1, p. 10.]

M. Boyle not only recognizes exhalations over the mines; he's strongly inclined to believe these fumes are hot. That, he says, is doubtless why Agricola listed among the indicators of mines the speed with which snow vanishes over places where there are metal veins — and why hoar-frost is never seen there; provided, he adds, there are no stones or rocks in the ground diverting the exhalations and stopping them from rising vertically. There must, he says, not only be fumes over the mines, but a great heat in the earth to drive them up: for I know from people who traveled to Hungary expressly to see the gold mines that the leaves of the trees in those parts are very often found coated with a golden color by the force of the metallic exhalations: "Folia arborum ... saepius aureo colore obducta inveniri ab aurifodinarum exhalationibus metallicis." [Boyle, de Temper. subterran. region. p. 16.]

This explains how the Divining Rod turns over wells, over pits, and over treasures hidden in the earth: it's certain, as M. Edward Browne found, that vapors and fumes rise from them just as they do over springs and mines. The earth put back into a pit where a treasure has been hidden never settles again the way nature originally arranged it; the disturbance makes it more porous, so the fumes rising from the ground crowd toward that spot, where they find an easier passage.

Our soldiers, who lack no experience in this department, know this physics well. No sooner are they billeted with their hosts in winter quarters than they make a point, whenever frost or snow has fallen, of scanning the garden for the spots with neither snow nor rime — certain that the earth there has been freshly turned, and that this is where the host has buried whatever he values most. They know perfectly well that the exhalations escaping more abundantly there melt the snow and frost above.

Don't imagine that even if the pit were filled with basins of silver, or some other metal, the seepage of the underground vapors would be blocked. Fumes pass through metals — indeed, abundant fumes rise from every kind of mineral. Note carefully that the learned M. Boyle in fact recognizes that these vapors and exhalations rise vertically — straight up out of the earth — unless their course is deflected by stones and rocks that these streams of fine matter cannot penetrate: "Quod directa calidorum effluviorum ascensio impedita fuerit per obstacula rupium, aut aliorum lapidum, quae penetrare effluvia non poterant." [Boyle, de temperie subterran. region. p. 16.]

This vertical motion of the mineral exhalations doesn't contradict the circular motion Becher attributes to them. The two are reconciled by that thoroughly established rule of motion: if a body moving in a straight line meets an obstacle in its path, it will swerve from that line — but in such a way that the angle of deviation is proportioned to the size of the obstacle. So you can easily see that these vapors, rising vertically from the earth at first, then meeting resistance in the air, swerve from the vertical; and since they meet ever new obstacles as they climb, they're forced to trace curved lines, which curve the more, the bigger the obstacles they encounter.

There, then, are the metallic exhalations rising vertically over the mines up to a certain height — just as Cassiodorus said of the vapors that climb into the air in the form of a column over the watercourses; and this admirable uniformity, recognized by these great men in the rising of vapors and exhalations, shows that nature has everywhere one and the same mechanism. There, too, is the reason the rod dips straight down over the mines and over the metals hidden in the earth. For there's no doubting that metals — coined gold and silver included — give off perpetual fumes that form a kind of atmosphere around them, as Father Malebranche puts it so well: In short, he says, far more humors escape through the imperceptible pores of the arteries and the skin than leave by the body's other passages; and even the most solid metals have no pores so narrow that nature doesn't still contain bodies small enough to pass freely through them — otherwise those pores would seal up. [Recherche de la vérité, Book 2, ch. 3, p. 137.]

OBJECTION. One difficulty remains. Why does the rod sometimes dip over metals with such force that it snaps — which never happens over watercourses?

ANSWER. That force, I answer, demonstrates the great difference between water vapors, which are cold and moist, and metal exhalations, which are dry and hot, as M. Boyle recognized very well. These dry, hot particles do to the rod what fire itself would do: they penetrate it, open it up, and make it writhe — the way a hazel branch writhes before a fire. (It's well known that it twists on its own long enough to let you watch a small bird, fastened to it, roast completely through.) The subtle fire exhaling from minerals works on the long fibers of the hazel even more violently, and faster, than ordinary fire, which takes much longer to do the same.

This answer, which I think excellent — and which I'll reinforce in an entire chapter devoted to the force of corpuscles — isn't an invention I should take credit for. I have it from a man who made a great name in the chymical world, and to whom the full glory of it belongs: the celebrated Basil Valentine, one of those who carried the use of the Divining Rod furthest. He says more than ten times, in chapters 22 through 28 of the first book of his Testament, that all the rod's movement originates in the dry, hot exhalations rising from above the mines, which have scarcely less heat than fire itself, though not stirred to the point of bursting into flame. Above all he says, in chapter 29, that when the exhalations grow heavy and sink back into the mines where work is going on, no man alive can remain there, and a lit candle carried in goes out at once.

OBJECTION. Joannes Matthaeus, doctor of medicine, who attacked the magnetic cures in giving his opinion on the so-called unguentum armarium, takes up the hazel rod and claims to demolish, with a single dilemma, the view that it has a power for discovering metals. He begins by saying each metal has its own particular juices; then he reasons: either the hazel branch dips over all kinds of metals, or over one only. If it turns over all, then it contains within itself powers entirely contrary to one another. If it bends over only one, then tell us why it's used to find every kind of metal: "Si omnium dixeris? habebit corylus naturam sibi invicem contrariam: si unius? quaero quomodo ergo omnia metalla uno coryli surculo investigentur?" [Theat. Sympath. p. 561.]

ANSWER. This physician poses his difficulty with great confidence, in a tone meant to suggest no man alive could answer it. Yet it takes no great effort to topple this supposed Achilles. 1. Just allow pores of several shapes in the hazel — as there most certainly are. There's the door opened for the different juices of the metals, and for every other kind of particle. 2. Just as we say an iron rod can be magnetized first by one end and then by the other in completely opposite directions, made to switch its pole — because iron is supple, and its parts can bend repeatedly in different directions without breaking — we likewise say the fibers of hazel are even more supple than iron's, and after arranging themselves for the passage of particles streaming from gold, they take a different set to let through the ones escaping from silver. 3. The difficulty is as amusing as a man who couldn't conceive how, with one and the same sieve, you can strain water, beer, wine, milk, and so on, one after another. It deserves no more of our time.

Since this chapter is already very long, I save for the next one the task of showing the fit between the pores of the rod and the particles of water, of minerals, and of unnoticed perspiration — so the reader may be the better convinced that all three kinds of vapors can easily work their way into the pores of the hazel rod.

[End of Chapter Seven]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER EIGHT

The bodies of thieves and fleeing murderers give off, by unnoticed perspiration, many particles that linger on their trail — and these make the Divining Rod dip.

What I've said so far about the rod's dipping over springs and mines probably won't draw many objectors. Short of leading the vapors and exhalations visibly into the hazel branch, I've done, I think, everything that can reasonably be demanded to show they enter it, and that the cause of the rod's movement needn't be sought anywhere else. But now I've reached the point where I'll apparently have to endure everything that the lively imagination of people better at declaiming than reasoning will prompt them to say, with great heat and confidence, on a subject perfectly suited to their talent. The field is wide open for such people, I admit. The subject lends itself to every shape a sophist might want to give it. Grand rhetorical flourishes, dramatic gestures, even raptures can join the party; you'd have to be a very poor rhetorician not to find a way to work them in. But let's leave the declaimers and speak to the philosophers, whom we'll find in a more reasonable frame of mind.

What makes the imagination balk at the story of the Lyon murderer's discovery by the Divining Rod is that no one sees how Jaques Aymar could have made out the fleeing criminal's trail and recognized the bed he slept in, the table he ate at, the jugs and glasses he touched. The philosophy of occult qualities and substantial forms, it must be said, doesn't have a nose fine enough to detect the traces a murderer leaves in the places he passes through. And it would be a real blessing if those who never learned more physics than what's in the notebooks they brought home from college could just be persuaded that there's still something left for them to learn in nature — and that the famous distinguo, which made them invincible on the debating benches, is an excellent shield for parade-ground skirmishes and nearly useless in a real war.

The philosophy of corpuscles will teach us that minerals, plants, and animals constantly give off many fine particles by way of unnoticed perspiration, and that these emanations go far beyond anything ordinarily imagined. Once I've demonstrated this, as I propose to do, I dare promise that no one will be any more surprised to see the peasant of Dauphiné follow a criminal's trail for 45 leagues than we are watching a dog course a hare or a stag without ever losing the scent.

I. When I say that all bodies are porous, and that imperceptible parts perpetually break away from them in continual emanations, I'm saying nothing I can't prove — 1. by the testimony of the greatest philosophers, and 2. by reason backed by countless experiments.

1. M. Gassendi, who spent part of his life reading the ancient philosophers, says flatly that if our senses are too coarse to detect these particles, reason must help us perceive them — as it helped Hippocrates, and so many other great men, who all believed that bodies are extremely porous and permeable, and that continual, reciprocal, imperceptible exchanges pass from one to another: "Aut si ipsis ratio audienda videtur, quae pridem persuasit & Hippocrati, & tot aliis magnis viris, corpora nisi omnia, saltem plurima esse tota perspirabilia, & patere continuo ex his in illa, ex illis in haec fluxiones." [Gassend. Physic. sect. 1, bk. 6, ch. 14, p. 410.]

2. Since there's no reason to reject the Chymists' division of bodies into three kingdoms — minerals, vegetables, and animals — we'll follow the same arrangement, and show that bodies of all three kinds emit a fine matter that spreads ceaselessly through the air.

1. Minerals most certainly perspire. Our eyes can't see the pores in them, but they have them all the same — as in fact every body does, even the hardest and most compact. And however small these pores are, nature contains bodies fine enough to pass through them. M. Boyle observed that black marble, the ruby, the agate, and the diamond — the hardest body known — give off an atmosphere of fine matter, which must be the cause of the electric virtue that lets these bodies, after a little rubbing, attract small straws and very light flecks of wood. [Boyle, de atmosph. corpor. consistent. pp. 4–12.] Once more: however solid these bodies are, there's no doubting they're riddled with countless little holes through which a swarm of particles escapes without ceasing. After all, bodies would have to be made of nothing but cube-shaped subtle matter in order not to be porous and permeable. Which can't be — for then all bodies would be homogeneous, identical in nature, with no individual differences. Particles of different shapes must enter into their composition. And if so, as it can't be doubted, then the fabric of bodies must of absolute necessity contain gaps — pores — since you can't conceive of particles of different shapes (spherical, oval, cubic, triangular) joining without their arrangement always leaving countless little empty spaces, through which the imperceptible matter of perspiration flows. Finally, just as pores are necessary in the fabric of bodies, it's equally necessary that particles pass through them, for otherwise the gaps would seal up. This is Father Malebranche's reasoning: even the most solid metals, he says, have no pores so narrow that nature doesn't still contain bodies small enough to pass freely through them — for otherwise those passages would close. [Recherche de la vérité, bk. 2, ch. 3, p. 137.]

It's so well established that metallic bodies give off a little whirlwind of fine matter that some people find these emanations downright harmful. I know a man who can never enter the halls of the Paris Mint when much coin has just been struck without being forced to leave in a hurry shortly after — he's attacked there by a failing of his natural faculties, and by an oppression that drives the spirits back toward the heart, deserting the rest of the body. This fits well with what Fracastorius teaches: the emanations of particles detaching from metals can cause in a man a sudden loss of feeling, with damage to the principal faculties of the soul, difficulty breathing, and all the same symptoms seen in apoplexy: "ex tractatione metallicorum apoplecticum hominem fieri: exhalant enim ex his, & circumquaque feruntur insensibilia corpora." [De Contag. bk. 1, ch. 7.]

I haven't seen the little book entitled Specimen cogitationum de ortu & effluviis metallorum. But I've learned that the author, M. Oudelius, a Swedish gentleman, notes there that copper perspires so prodigiously that if you've just made blue water with turnsole, and there happens to be copper in the house — even in a different room — the water reddens from the impression it takes from that metal.

2. Vegetables are at least as subject as minerals to this perpetual wasting through the escape of particles. We've already noted that a friend of M. Boyle smelled, at sea, 25 miles from the Isle of Ceylon, the scent of the cinnamon and fragrant gums that land bears in abundance. The Chevalier Digby noted the same of the rosemary growing on the coasts of Spain, whose scent carries thirty or forty leagues out to sea. The smell of hemp, or of bean flowers, also carries very far. Fracastorius, naming plants that perspire heavily, lists the onion, pepper, the iris, tobacco, nightshade, the poppy — some of which sting the eyes badly, others provoke sneezing, and the last puts you to sleep by emitting its drowsy spirits. And he says flatly that this perspiration forms a swarm, a whirlwind, an atmosphere of particles circulating around the edges of those plants, and even at some distance from them: "Seminaria contagionum ad distans feruntur, & in orbem." [De Contag. bk. 1, ch. 7.]

M. Boyle says this evaporation of particles goes on in winter, more abundantly than you'd believe, in apples and in fruits even wrapped in a tough skin. He affirms that one day, holding these fruits in a very exact balance made expressly for such delicate experiments, he found a quite considerable loss going on without pause. What he adds is stronger still. He had a turner make him a vessel of very solid wood, holding about a pint, and he could never pin down its weight with final precision: the vessel was shedding particles in so quick and prodigious a stream that scarcely had he added grains to balance his scale when a fresh burst of evaporation outran his diligence, carried off more particles, and made the vessel lighter. And if anyone were curious enough, adds this learned physicist, to have an exact balance built by a craftsman versed in statics, what pleasure he'd have in tracking and computing the progress of this continual wasting, which spares not even the bodies we consider hardest and most solid! [Boyle, de Atmosph. corpor. consistent. part 4.]

Plants have pores through which they perspire — no doubting it. The admirable weave of their fibers gives them kinds of organs that seem to make them capable of something fairly close to animal sensation. There are, after all, sensitive plants. There are modest ones. The juices that sustain them are believed to circulate like the blood of animals; and Campanella, who grants the use of feeling to all material bodies, gives it to plants as to animal flesh, and in a far higher degree than he gives it to stones, minerals, or even the bones of animals: "Plantas vero praestantiore vigere sensu, simili sensui carnis." [De sensu rerum, bk. 2, ch. 12, p. 98.]

3. Animals perspire — that's beyond dispute; and of all animals, man is perhaps the one in whom perspiration is most abundant. Organized bodies are pierced with countless little holes through which fine matter evaporates continually. The eyes can see some of these pores; the microscope shows more; but the smallest, which are the most numerous, escape both eyes and microscope. Even if the flow of sweat didn't convince us these little gaps exist, a moment's attention to how animal bodies are built would show they must be riddled with a prodigious number of pores. An animal's body isn't a lump of crude, shapeless matter; it's a composite of parts whose structure and arrangement are admirable. These membranes, fibers, bones, cartilages, ligaments, veins, arteries, nerves, muscles, being nothing but little pieces of matter of different shapes, arranged differently for all the various movements the Creator had in view — the fabric of these organic parts must contain a very great number of little empty spaces, without which the animal could never bend or stretch its body. And though these pores are essential for the body's various flexings, they have other uses too. They receive the animal spirits flowing from the brain, which cause the mechanical movement of all natural, organized bodies. It's in these pores that the nutritive juices lodge, replacing in each part what escapes by unnoticed perspiration. In the end, these pores are like so many little drains by which nature relieves bodies of the useless weight of the humors that must be vented through unnoticed perspiration. On this, Father Malebranche says that far more humors escape through the imperceptible pores of the arteries and the skin than leave by the body's other passages. [Recher. de la Vérit. bk. 2, ch. 3, p. 137.]

Van Helmont proves the necessity of this perspiration plainly enough. Every person, he says, certainly makes at least seven to ten ounces of blood a day, above all once full-grown, when the body grows no more; from which he concludes that just as much must waste away each day — otherwise the body would swell to a frightful bulk. That's why, having compared to a dew the purest substance produced from food, which becomes the immediate nourishment of animals, he says: "atqui (saltem in aetate consistente) necesse est, tantundem sanguinis in dies consumi, quantum de novo generatur. Alias namque homo mox in immensum fieret." [Imag. ferment. impraegnat. Mass. sem. n. 4, p. 70: And yet (at least at the age of full stature) it is necessary that as much blood be consumed daily as is newly generated. For otherwise a man would soon grow to an immense size.]

No physician has treated unnoticed perspiration with as much care and skill as Sanctorius, professor of medicine in the College of Padua; before him, no one had really understood how much fine matter is lost by this evaporation. Here are his observations, which he cast as aphorisms. Section I, aphorism III: Only he who well understands how far unnoticed perspiration goes, when it must be stimulated, and when its excessive losses from the body must be made good, is capable of working to preserve or restore human health. IV. The weight of what escapes a man's body by unnoticed perspiration exceeds what leaves it by the visible evacuations. VI. Of eight pounds of nourishment taken in a day, a good five pounds are lost by unnoticed perspiration. XXI. In winter, more than 50 ounces of fine matter will escape a perfectly healthy man in 24 hours. LIX. In a night of quite tranquil sleep, the unnoticed perspiration of a sleeper is about forty ounces; of a man awake, 20 ounces.

Here is Sanctorius's own text: "Sect. 1, aphorism 3: Ille solus qui sciet quantum & quando magis, vel minus corpus occulte perspirat, poterit quantum & quando erit addendum vel auferendum pro sanitate conservanda & recuperanda. 4. Perspiratio insensibilis sola solet esse longe plenior, quam omnes sensibiles simul unitae. 6. Si cibus & potus unius diei sit ponderis octo librarum, transpiratio insensibilis ascendere solet ad quinque libras circiter. ... 21. Ille halitus invisibilis qualis hyeme uno die naturali ad quinquaginta uncias, & ultra exhalare potest. 59. Spatio unius noctis ... quadraginta & ultra per occultam perspirationem evacuari, ut plurimum solent. Sect. 2, aphorism 25: Aestate temperata corpora sunt minoris ponderis, quam hyeme, tribus libris circiter. 41. Ab aequinoctio autumnali ad solstitium hyemale, qualibet die minus libra circiter perspiramus, inde usque ad aequinoctium vernale incipimus liberius perspirare. Sect. 3, aphorism 8: Caro vervecina facile concoquitur, & est vaporosa: perspirat enim noctis spatio trientem librae magis, quam cetera solitaque edulia. Sect. 4, aphorism 5: Somnus inquietus impedire solet trientem solitae perspirationis. 20. Perspirationem insensibilem cursu septem horarum in dormiente, inveni in multis esse quadraginta unciarum circiter, in vigilante viginti."

These observations are so curious, and so worthy of a learned physicist, that M. Boyle — after calling Sanctorius's book a little book all of gold — declares he was curious enough to run similar experiments on himself. Then he says: my observations, joined to those of a great and very curious Prince, who had a static machine for tracking the quantity of this continual perspiration, and who was kind enough to share his results with me, convince me that Sanctorius advanced nothing but what is thoroughly reliable — as anyone may verify, provided allowance is made for differences of climate, which can shift the numbers. For Sanctorius wrote in Italy, where perspiration is more abundant than in England, where I made my experiments. "Quaedam autem experimenta sollicite circa meipsum facta, ac quibusdam aliis Curiosissimi, Magnique Principis experimentis addita, qui quodam utebatur instrumento, ipsosque mihi operationis eventus indicare dignabatur, omnino sunt in causa, cur Sanctorii observationes non rejiciam, observato tantum Italicum Clima inter, in quo scripsit, atque Anglicanum, ubi experimenta nostra facta sunt, discrimine." [De corpor. animal. porositat. ch. 3, p. 7.]

Since the goal of all our studies in physics is to find the means of preserving or restoring the health of the human body, no one will mind if, with so useful and so universally sought an end in view, I step aside from my subject a moment to say that nothing could do more to perfect medicine than a thorough knowledge of unnoticed perspiration. Sanctorius can't be praised enough for trying to steer the practice of medicine toward it; the excess or deficiency of this perspiration is the most common cause of our diseases. Yet Sanctorius, whatever his good intentions, has had more opponents than followers. Hippolytus Obicius, lecturer in medicine at Ferrara, certainly tried to make Sanctorius's doctrine ridiculous in his dialogues entitled Statico-mastix. This dialogue-writer hands the victory to Galenic medicine, having no wish to abandon it whatever the man he's fighting may say. "Sanctorius," says the illustrious M. le Président Cousin in his Journal des Savants, "is the only one who has treated this perspiration thoroughly, in a book printed at Venice in 1614 under the title Ars de Statica medicina, aphorismorum sectionibus septem comprehensa; and M. Cusac has incorporated its whole doctrine here." [Journal des Savants of Monday, January 26, 1693, p. 44.]

So although it's nearly 80 years since Sanctorius published that admirable little treatise, I haven't noticed physicians adopting the very easy and entirely safe approach of curing the sick by way of unnoticed perspiration. I don't see that any of them ever thought of profiting from so important a discovery. But I now learn with pleasure, from the same Journal des Savants, that M. Cusac intends to put Sanctorius's theory into practice, and has recently written a book on the method of curing diseases by the routes of perspiration and evacuation.

This perspiration, by which an invisible matter constantly leaves all bodies, probably constitutes the need all corporeal things have of the maintenance God gives them, to repair the breaches made by the departure of these fine, imperceptible parts. It's in this sense that the author of Psalm 103 says to God: All things wait upon Thee, that Thou mayest give them their food in due season. When Thou givest to them, they gather; when Thou openest Thy hand, all things are filled with Thy goodness. But if Thou forsakest Thy creatures, they return to dust and perish; yet when Thou fillest them with Thy spirit of life, Thou renewest the face of the whole earth. [vv. 27–30.] It's doubtless this continual repair that allows some bodies to shed a perpetual atmosphere of particles for a hundred years without appearing to shrink at all. Ambergris, and Spanish leathers, give off fragrant vapors for more than a century without any visible change.

II. I don't believe anything reasonable can be set against what I've said so far about unnoticed perspiration. All of that will pass without difficulty. But one more point remains, and it matters enormously, because it's from here that we'll draw the light to explain everything most obscure in the effects of the Divining Rod. I say not only that people perspire; I add that there are occasions and circumstances that increase or decrease the unnoticed perspiration. Mental distress, violent exercise, turbulent passions, hurried journeys open the pores, drain the body's strength, set the animal spirits racing — and so cause a more abundant escape of fine matter in anyone in those states. Sanctorius says flatly that bodily exertion drains the strength through excessive perspiration, and that the pains and anxieties of the mind make a terrible run on the animal spirits, above all in the heart and the brain, where the soul performs its principal functions: "duo sunt exercitia; alterum corporis, & alterum animi; corporis, evacuat sensibilia excrementa: animi, insensibilia magis, & praecipue cordis, & cerebri, ubi sedet animus." [sect. 5, aphorism 16.]

From what Sanctorius says there, we can guess what an abundant, furious perspiration must have poured off the murderers of Lyon. Beyond the physical exhaustion of their headlong flight, their minds must have been racked by the mortal alarms of so enormous a crime, and by perpetual dread of the ultimate punishment — which, as Scripture says, is always at a villain's door. The Holy Spirit captures the strange condition of such wretches perfectly in a few words: the wicked man flees when no one pursues him: "Fugit impius nemine persequente." [Proverb. 28:1.]

Picture, then, around the fleeing villains, an atmosphere of particles streaming continually through the pores of their skin, spreading along their route from Lyon to Beaucaire, and from Beaucaire to Toulon. Or better: think of this outflow of particles as a stream spreading through the air, whose source the villains carry with them everywhere. Now gather up everything we've seen about perspiration in Sanctorius, and you'll grant that I'm assuming nothing here but what is probable, and what I've already demonstrated. There is, then, on the criminals' footsteps a cloud, an atmosphere of particles hanging in the air, which make the Divining Rod dip in Jaques Aymar's hands when he follows their route exactly.

III. What I've said still isn't enough, because we need to know whether these particles, exhaled from the bodies of thieves and murderers, are shaped right to slip into the Divining Rod. It would be easy for me to show that wood has pores of enough different shapes for some of them to match the fine matter of unnoticed perspiration. But people inclined to guard themselves against my arguments will listen more readily to the voice of nature, which I'll let speak through some very curious phenomena that make the truth almost palpable.

PHENOMENON. M. Polisius, in the 43rd Observation of the Journal of Medicine of the Academy of the Curious of Nature in Germany, 1685, reports that a sprig of rosemary, placed as is the custom between the hands of a dead man, grew in such a way that it spread on every side over the beard and covered the whole face of the deceased with its greenery — as was discovered with much surprise, some years later, when the coffin was opened. This effect is entirely natural, and even easy to explain. The humors remaining in the corpse, set moving by the salts, produced a fermentation that pushed outward an atmosphere of fine matter; and since this matter escaping from the corpse happened to match the pores of the rosemary, it worked into the fibers of the branch and produced the growth M. Polisius describes. Nothing extraordinary there. Since nothing in the world carries as many salts as human blood, you shouldn't be surprised that certain fermentations — and so certain vegetations — occur in corpses. The hair and nails of corpses have often been seen to grow considerably. Paré tells of a corpse he embalmed and kept for 20 whole years without any decay, whose nails kept growing back very long though he trimmed them often. [Paraeus bk. 28.] Campanella says this evaporation of fine parts stops in time, once the corpse is entirely dried out and exhausted: "At temporis mora exhalat attenuatus, remanetque cadaver siccum." [De sensu rer. bk. 4, ch. 3, p. 298.]

So there, in the phenomenon M. Polisius reports, the fit between the particles of perspiration and the pores of rosemary is well recognized and well established. I even remember watching gardeners practice something that bears on the point: when they had a sick tree without vigor, they buried a dead dog near the root, and the particles coming off it never failed to enrich the tree and make it grow extraordinarily. Why does a flower wither so fast in certain people's hands, if not because the hot matter escaping their hands works into the stem and drives out the moist parts that are the juices of a plant's nourishment and growth?

But the rosemary's growth shows us something more: how entirely possible it is for the Divining Rod to turn over corpses — and sooner still over the bodies of those who died violently. They die full of their blood and their animal spirits, of which a great wasting, perhaps a total destruction, occurs in people who die of illness — especially when frequent bleeding has come to the heirs' assistance, and a rich invalid has had the privilege of dying by the Galenic method. It's this consideration that led Paracelsus and Van Helmont to prefer the Mummy taken from the corpse of a man condemned by justice over the kind drawn from the bodies of those who die by inches. [Paracels. Philosoph. Tract. 5, p. 504; Van Helmont de magnet. vulner. curat. n. 96.]

This is why the Divining Rod dips so strongly over the corpses of murdered people. No one knew it before Jaques Aymar's experience taught us — and he didn't know it himself when, searching for water near his home, he declared on the strength of his rod's rapid movement that water must be close. He was wrong, as he soon learned: digging the earth turned up, instead of water, the corpse of a strangled woman. The reflection that plain good sense suggested to him reveals an effect of the rod no one had ever thought of: he concluded that it also dips over the corpses of the murdered. I remember hearing several times, from people obsessed with hunting treasure by the hazel rod, that nothing was ever found where it dipped but dead men's bones — at which those people decided certain ceremonies must be added to the business. That is an error, and a thoroughly crude stupidity, since the rod turns only by the movement these particles of perspiration impress on it. And I have no doubt it would dip as readily over the body of a man executed for his crimes as over a murder victim's — and in general over anything that perspires heavily, as the experiments that will now be made will confirm every day.

NEW EXPERIMENT ON THE ROD. We've just learned that the rod dips over the magnet. This discovery favors my system all the more in that the trials newly run in several places in Paris answer perfectly to the mechanism I've explained. It's certain the rod dips over the pole of a good magnet: there's the dip. It's equally established that if you then present it the other pole, from which the magnetic matter flows in another direction, the rod, instead of dipping, straightens up and bends backward — because it's penetrated, and as it were magnetized, by the first impression it received in dipping over the pole first shown to it. In this the hazel rod imitates, point for point, the mechanism — the movement and dip — of the magnetized iron rod. That alone demonstrates the truth of my system, and the absurdity of those who claim the Divining Rod's effect comes from the demon rather than from nature.

Objections have been put to me which I won't hide — all the more because my answers always follow from my system.

FIRST DIFFICULTY. How could Jaques Aymar recognize the jugs, the glasses, the billhook, and the other things the killers had touched?

ANSWER. Hands perspire — no doubting it. You can even see it plainly when someone touches a well-polished silver plate: the trace of the fingers prints itself there like a little vapor, which the movement of the surrounding air detaches and dissipates soon enough. Besides, just as material particles from an animal's body cling to the ground it passes over, even at a run — so that, says M. Digby, the dogs of England will follow by scent, for several leagues, the trail of a man or beast that passed some hours before — in the same way, on everything those wretches touched, material parts of their perspiration stayed behind, and these moved the rod. This won't seem incredible if you remember that good dogs pick out, from a pile of stones, the one their master has touched, as M. Digby relates of the keen-nosed dogs of England. [p. 54 of the Powder of Sympathy.] Father Schott the Jesuit has written something far more surprising: in the time of the Emperor Justinian there was at Constantinople a showman who, having drawn a crowd, told the assembly to throw the rings from their fingers into the square, and his dog would pick them up and return each man's own to him without error — which was carried out as promised. [Schott. bk. 8, mirabil. animal. terrest. ch. 4, §4, p. 836.] This could only work because each ring kept the individual particles of its owner, which steered the dog toward the person around whom it found an atmosphere of matching particles. For everything that enters a cloud of perspired matter is soaked in it — wetted, if you like — like a piece of gold dipped in water; with this difference, that the particles of unnoticed perspiration, being infinitely finer than particles of water, sink deeper into whatever they flood, and let go of it only with more time and difficulty.

SECOND DIFFICULTY. How is it possible that from such small objects — a few gold or silver coins, over which the rod turns — enough fine matter can escape to produce so large an effect, without any visible change in them?

ANSWER. I confess that however much reason I have to be convinced, I wouldn't yield easily if several experiments I cannot doubt didn't demonstrate to me, plainly, that every body sheds a swarm of atoms, an atmosphere of particles, which have all the more activity and force for being smaller. And the surprising part is that the bodies undergoing this perpetual wasting are not visibly the lighter for it. EXPERIMENT. Everyone knows that emetic wine, which purges upward and downward with great violence, is made with glass of antimony soaked in wine. Everyone also assumes the glass of antimony must have shed a great stream of its particles into the wine to give it such violent effects. But perhaps not everyone knows that this glass of antimony, taken out of wine it has steeped in a hundred times, shows no loss at all, on an ordinary balance, from the weight it had when first put in.

THIRD DIFFICULTY. Why does the rod tip toward the earth?

ANSWER. I've already noted that it moves this way in order to lie parallel to the lines of the fumes hanging over the criminals' footsteps. There's no doubt the fumes the eye can't see rise upward, since the ones the eyes see every day move that way. The evaporations by which fine matter leaves certain bodies carry the fumes upward; and that, says Fracastorius, is the first movement observed in them: "Quae circa contagiones contingunt evaporationes, circumquaque feruntur... Exhalatio omnis multum diffunditur, magis autem sursum, & primo." [De contag. bk. 1, ch. 7: The evaporations occurring about contagions are carried in every direction... Every exhalation diffuses itself much, but more upward, and first.]

FOURTH DIFFICULTY. Why was the man with the rod never thrown off the scent in following the fleeing criminals for 45 leagues, when presumably plenty of other people traveled the same route?

ANSWER. This is explained so pleasantly in the first letter to M. l'Abbé Bignon that I can do no better than borrow the words of the person of quality who wrote it: "It's easy enough to see that what makes good dogs hard to throw off the scent is this: just as each individual animal differs from another of its species, the spirits exhaled from its body also differ from those of another animal of the same species; and these differences, called individual in the Schools, can't be doubted by anyone who has noticed, with the microscope, how hard it is to find two grains of sand, of mustard, or of poppy exactly alike. From which it follows that a good dog, used to following the spirits of his hare, won't easily abandon them for those of another hare, which don't make the same impression on him. The same must be said of the spirits left on the traces of various villains — only the ones the man with the rod has accustomed himself to will make the strongest impression on him."

If it surprises you that the man with the rod can follow one criminal and pick him out among a hundred others, after soaking himself in the particles streaming from the murderer's unnoticed perspiration, which he has registered and tasted, so to speak — you should be much more surprised to see a dog pick out his master in a great fair, follow him, and recognize him every time. That surely happens because the particles leaving that man's body by unnoticed perspiration act on the dog's nose in a way wholly particular to his master.

But let's hear what Jaques Aymar himself answered when the question was put to him. M. Garnier, physician of Lyon, asked whether his rod turned only over what he had set out to find, and how he would manage on a road where several thieves and murderers had passed, with a spring, or hidden silver, or even a murder victim's body along the way. M. Garnier tells us that "Jaques Aymar didn't deny he could be misled if, along the same line where there was water, there was also hidden silver, or thieves had passed; because the rod turns in his hands for all three without his being able to tell the difference; and that, as for murderers, he can only follow the one for whom he has magnetized himself at the scene of the murder." [Letter of M. Garnier, pp. 60–61.] Aymar's answer is plain good sense: it matches the truth, and agrees entirely with my principles.

FIFTH DIFFICULTY. Wasn't it just chance that led the peasant so exactly along the footsteps of the Lyon murderers?

ANSWER. This doubt was raised by a great Prince, as distinguished by the greatness of his courage and the beauty and range of his mind as by the royal blood in his veins. M. l'Abbé Bignon sent it on to the person of quality we've mentioned, who answered him from Lyon in these terms: "You would answer the curiosity of M. le Duc de Chartres far better than I, Monsieur, if you chose to; and you would no doubt tell him that one cannot attribute to chance a thing that always happens necessarily, given certain conditions. Now it is certain that every time the man in question finds himself at a place where a murder or theft has recently been committed, his rod always turns, infallibly; and consequently this cannot be put down to chance." [Third letter to M. l'Abbé Bignon.]

I won't dwell further on these difficulties, which are already sufficiently demolished by the principles I've laid down — they need only be applied to each doubt to clear it up. But I wanted to offer this small assistance to readers unaccustomed to this kind of intellectual work.

[End of Chapter Eight]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER NINE

The particles perspired by the Lyon murderers, spread through the air, could easily enter the man with the rod through unnoticed respiration. How much this observation can contribute to perfecting medicine. The magnetic cures.

We've seen that Jaques Aymar can't stand in a place where a murder has been committed without immediately feeling heart-sickness, convulsive movements, and other rather strange symptoms — and the same happens to everyone who has the gift of the rod. Here is how one of the letters to M. l'Abbé Bignon describes it: "The usual symptom is an inner agitation, which in some people produces tremblings, sweats, headaches, and in almost all of them palpitations of the heart and rapid pounding of the arteries. But I have only observed these symptoms in cases of murder. In the other cases, those who have this power feel only an inner agitation, which most of them notice only by the turning of the rod. The agitation, and the symptoms that follow it, are more violent on land than on water. But it is the same in a cellar as in the open air, and the same whether those who have this power are well or unwell." Effects this tangible deserve to have their cause investigated.

So, having examined how a swarm of particles leaves a murderer's body by unnoticed perspiration, we now have to show how those same particles work their way into the body of the man with the rod by an unnoticed respiration. It's plain enough, after all, that the particles leaving the one are received by the other.

1. Ordinary, visible breathing — done through the larynx and windpipe, carrying air into the lungs, from which it's expelled by the 65 muscles of the chest — is not the kind I mean here, though it's certainly one route by which contagious atoms scattered in the air can spread very quickly through a man's whole body. I'm confining myself to the unnoticed respiration, which opens the pores, gives passage to the particles mixed in the air, and draws them into the body. This respiration apparently restores, or replaces, the volatile spirits lost through perspiration. The previous chapter laid out everything about perspiration relevant to my subject; this one will examine what respiration can offer toward explaining Jaques Aymar's symptoms at murder scenes. We're already convinced, beyond any need of more proof, that bodies perspire through the pores; now we have to establish that they also breathe through those same pores; and then we'll ask how these particles could have been breathed in through the pores of the peasant of Dauphiné.

Bodies breathe imperceptibly — that's beyond doubt. The Chymists (not the ones who promise mountains of gold, but the ones who study nature in their fine experiments with tireless labor and application) say that calcined bodies attract the surrounding air — Tartar above all, which draws to itself nine times its own weight of air.

EXPERIMENT. Expose to the air, says M. Digby, a pound of well-calcined salt of Tartar, and it will yield ten pounds of good oil of Tartar — attracting and absorbing the air around it, and even the other particles spread in the air. This is what happened to M. Ferrier's oil of Tartar, which, having been made in the season of roses, when the air is full of little atoms evaporating from those flowers, took on the scent of roses; the scent vanished in winter, and returned when rose season came back.

EXPERIMENT. Nearly all salts share this property of easily absorbing the bodies mixed in the air; and you can reckon that if the salt on the table is damp, it's a sure sign rain isn't far off, since the dampness shows the air is loaded with moist vapors.

There are people so perfectly attuned to the air that they feel every change in it with great exactness. Watch such people, and with a little experience you can forecast the weather. They are like human hygrometers: always following the disposition of the air, they announce fair or foul weather. They're cheerful and agreeable when the air is pure and clear; but they turn heavy, peevish, and unbearable with their perpetual spitting the moment the weather sets toward rain.

But as to the unnoticed respiration of the human body, nothing is more direct or more striking than the well-known story of a Nun of Rome, who through too many fasts, vigils, and mortifications had so overheated her body that she seemed all on fire, her bones thoroughly dried out and calcined. This put her body in such a state that it drew in air as abundantly as we said of calcined Tartar. The intake of air went beyond anything imaginable, for this holy woman gave it back as water, over some weeks, in a prodigious quantity. M. Digby, who says he heard it from the Nun's own mouth, reports that she gave back as much as 200 pounds of it in 24 hours. And Petrus Servius, physician to Pope Urban VIII, adds that she even produced more than 200 pounds in a single astronomical day.

This intake of air is precisely the route by which contagious diseases spread; and since the attraction is stronger in dry and calcined matter, you might conclude that the most overheated and dried-out people are the most prone to drawing in the surrounding air — and so the most exposed to diseases caught by contagion.

But here is an effect of contagion I would never have imagined. I owe it to an observation of M. Boyle. A person who has once been struck by a contagious disease retains, even after a complete cure, a strong disposition to catch it again — because once you've drawn in particles of a certain kind, you stay disposed to breathe in similar ones more easily. On this point, the celebrated physicist affirms that some men are of such a temperament that, having once had the plague, they can easily tell — by a disposition they've contracted, which makes them acutely sensitive to the impact of pestilential atoms — whether contagion is loose in the air. M. Boyle proves it with three examples that bear too directly on my subject to leave out. 1. Three years before the horrible plague that ravaged London in 1665 was noticed there, a woman consulting her physician about a tumor her husband had in the groin said the patient insisted the plague would soon strike the city; and the reason he gave for this premonition was that the last time the plague had visited London, he'd had a tumor exactly like it. Which, unhappily, came to pass as he predicted. 2. A celebrated surgeon named Fabricius Hildanus had had a plague-carbuncle in his youth. He kept from it a certain disposition that made him, for the rest of his life, feel pain in the same spot every time he went into or near a house infected with the disease. 3. A physician of Breda never visited plague victims without noticing, a few moments later, a very bad odor coming off his own body; and that night he was unfailingly seized with a headache, heavy sweating, and even diarrhea. [Boyle, suspic. cosmic. p. 2.]

From this you can see how easily the particles given off by an unhealthy man work their way into another, and the sudden disorders they cause there. Here, then, is a strange commerce that the corpuscular philosophy reveals between people — and medicine could act on it, by seeking the secret of neutralizing the contagious atoms in the air, or of shielding people from them with fumigations designed for the purpose. Since people perspire and breathe imperceptibly with such ease that they're purged of — and just as commonly, and just as quickly, filled with — the bad vapors in the air, why hasn't medicine turned in that direction? Why has it neglected the means of making the impure particles perspire out and the balsamic, healing ones breathe in — when this would be such a quick and easy way of relieving the sick, instead of amusing oneself draining them of blood and wrecking their whole constitution with repeated bleedings, and with so many other procedures whose look and apparatus are as repellent as the diseases themselves? I have no wish to offend anyone. I honor, as much as any man living, those who practice a science as useful to society as medicine has always been; but since a physicist, in all his studies and vigils, must aim at preserving human life, no one should wonder if I keep returning to these reflections. I'm thoroughly convinced that physics and its finest curiosities deserve our attention only insofar as they can serve the good of man, for whom God deigned to make everything in heaven and on earth.

Let's say, then, that if nature left pores and gaps in the skin, it wasn't only to expel the corrupted matter that must escape by unnoticed perspiration; it was also to let in the healing atoms that must enter by unnoticed respiration. You can therefore be cured by contagion, just as you can be sickened by contagion. True, the eyes witness nothing of this mutual traffic of particles expelled by perspiration and received by respiration; but it's no less real and effective. Father Malebranche puts it very well: regarding the passages by which this air is exchanged, the mind's reach must not stop where the senses stop; the mind must penetrate what is impenetrable to them, and fasten on things over which neither the senses nor even the imagination has any hold. The subtlest parts of the air we breathe enter our heart, and there sustain, with the blood and the chyle, the fire that gives life and movement to our body; but according to their different qualities they bring great changes into the fermentation of the blood, and into the animal spirits. [Recherche de la vérité, bk. 1, ch. 5, pp. 136–138.]

I expect anyone reading this will get ahead of me and see for himself that it's an effect of contagion that gives the man with the rod such violent symptoms — pain, nausea, dizziness, sweating, and so on — when he stands where a murder was recently committed, or follows close on a killer's footsteps. The place is flooded with the particles the dying man perspired, in the struggle or in his death-agony; the murderer himself perspires extraordinarily in his flight; so Jaques Aymar must be flooded with them whenever he's within their sphere of activity. This fine matter, given off by the dying man and by the killer, is carried by the peasant's ordinary breathing into the branches of his windpipe, then into the venous artery, finally to mix and ferment with the blood in the heart. But the effect can just as well be credited to the unnoticed respiration alone, whose action is no less prompt, since it happens at once over all the parts of the body; and the road through the pores of the skin to the blood of the veins and arteries is even shorter and straighter than through the windpipe.

I'll explain later why Jaques Aymar and certain people are more sensitive to this impression of the air, and of the particles swimming in it, than so many others who feel nothing of the kind. That will come in its turn; we must avoid confusion. For now, it can't be denied that the particles — whether from the dead man or from the one who killed him — cause the symptoms seen in the peasant on these occasions. What aren't those little particles capable of, that leave objects and enter the eyes? What terrible effects doesn't a cruel, bloody spectacle work in tender, compassionate people, even though the disagreeable object strikes only at the back of the eye, on the retina? Don't you sometimes feel a shudder in the parts of your own body corresponding to the parts you see wounded in someone you have no connection with at all? How many delicate, frail people — moved mechanically rather than by reason — can't hear an animal beaten or crying without some distress of mind! And what we've said of the eye must be said of the ear. A sound — a movement of air variously modified — striking the eardrum, that little membrane stretched at the back of the ear to receive the impressions of agitated air and produce the sense of hearing, sometimes orders or disorders the whole machine of the body. A word of consolation restores someone in total despair. A taunt completely unstrings a weak mind and robs it of all its good sense. People laugh and cry by turns at the adventures of a hero who never existed. So let's return to the point: if little streams of particles, reaching only a tiny part of the body like the optic nerve or the eardrum, stir up such commotions in body and mind, what should we expect when a whole cloud of this fine matter wraps and touches a man's entire body? For you should know not only that the organ of touch is as delicate as any other external sense, but that it covers more ground than all the others together: it's spread over the whole body, and there are nerve fibers in the skin, in the fleshy membrane, in the membranes, and in the flesh, all answering to the brain — the immediate organ of touch.

We're now sufficiently convinced that Jaques Aymar soaks himself in the particles perspired by the murdered person when he's at the scene of the crime, or in those streaming off the killer when he pursues him with his rod over land and water; and that this impression produces in him the symptoms we've described.

II. Anyone can see that this mechanism of nature — which under the same circumstances always works the same heart-sickness and the same faintings on the peasant — points us straight toward the art of slipping benign, healing atoms into a sick body by unnoticed respiration, atoms that could drive out, by unnoticed perspiration, the matter lodged, as M. Boyle says, in the subtlest parts of the blood. Are we really never to be purged except by cathartic potions, which are so disgusting, and which don't produce all the effect one would want? There have already been curious men — they can't be praised enough — who found the secret of taking remedies by unnoticed respiration. M. Boyle mentions certain physicians of his acquaintance who purged children with preparations applied externally. He adds that he knew a man so practiced in this little routine that, when he wanted to purge himself, he did no more than apply a kind of plaster to his body, after which everything went perfectly. But then he tells a story that wouldn't readily be believed if it didn't come from M. Boyle. One day, he says, a clever man, a friend of mine, dismissed as fantasies everything he was told about this new way of purging the humors. He was well punished for it. A learned Chymist, whom I had told of this friend's skepticism, asked him for his hand. He gave it. The curious man rubbed it lightly with an oil he had prepared. A few moments later our friend felt himself pressed, as if he'd taken a cathartic potion that morning, and was obliged to excuse himself four times in a very short while — all of it, however, without cramping, without pain, and without any upset: "quaterque brevi tempore omni absque tormine, dolore, intemperie dejecit." [De corpor. animal. porositat. ch. 3, p. 8.]

M. Boyle, speaking of topical remedies — the kind applied to the afflicted, painful part — says it's an error, pardonable only in the ages before the circulation of the blood was known, to imagine that external medicines work only on the spot where they're applied. On the contrary, it's certain that they insinuate their efficacy, through the capillary vessels with which the skin is filled, into the whole mass of the blood. In fact, he relates how he cured himself several times of the tertian fever, the double-tertian, and even the quotidian, with a single mixture of Corinth currants, hops, and common salt ground together and applied to the wrist. He affirms he made this experiment with the same success on several other people with fever: "Ex uvis Corynthiacis, lupulis, & nigro sale, simul bene contusis, ipsemet cum aliis non paucis a simplici tertiana, aut duplici etiam tertiana, aut quotidiana liber evasi." [Boyle de Corpor. animal. porositat. ch. 4, p. 11.] He says in several places in his works that, after being plagued by a hemorrhage for a whole summer and trying several remedies in vain to stop the bleeding, he succeeded merely by touching the moss that grows on a dead man's skull.

I'll add a very curious fact, told to Zwelfer by a physician of the Estates of Moravia. This physician, who visited plague victims every day, affirmed that neither he nor any of his servants had been struck by the plague, because they wore troches of toad-powder prepared by Van Helmont's method. The reason, says M. Boyle, is that these gave off a ceaseless emission of particles, which scattered or blunted those of the contagion. On the strength of this he declares himself strongly inclined to believe that the amulets of the ancients, worn at the neck, were not entirely useless, and that he wouldn't want to condemn their use outright.

Democritus showed at the end of his days that he had grasped the help men can draw from the philosophy of corpuscles for preserving their lives. That great philosopher, all broken with age, according to Diogenes Laertius, noticed a few days before his death the grief in his sister's face — he was apparently going to die during the Festival of Ceres. He told her not to grieve: she could go take part in the public ceremonies, and he would stretch his life past the festival, provided warm bread was brought to him every day. Which he did. For three days he nourished himself by breathing nothing but the particles rising from the warm bread: "Panes igitur naribus cum admovisset, vivum se dum ea celebritas transiret, servavit." [Diogen. Laert. de Vitis Philosophor. bk. 9: Having therefore held the loaves to his nostrils, he kept himself alive until that festival had passed.]

Remedies applied externally must even work with more potency than those that pass through the esophagus — which are much altered by the acids dissolving them in the stomach, and which only enter the blood after being mixed and combined with the chyle. Medicines produce, all by themselves, without any need of heat, substantial effluvia in which their action consists; we have several experiments in which particles move and penetrate, cold, the membranes of even very hard bodies. Ambergris, musk, and the flowers give off their fragrant particles regardless of heat. There's even a flower called Geranium noctu olens, which has a perceptible scent only after sunset, and loses it the moment the sun comes back above the horizon.

It's also well established that external medicines keep their nature and lose none of their force from being filtered through the pores of the skin in unnoticed respiration. Several observations could prove it, but I'll content myself with one of M. Boyle's — his rigor in experiments makes me prefer one remark of his to a hundred from credulous, sloppy people. He affirms he has observed several times that a little opium mixed into external medicines took away acute pains in the innermost parts of the body — clear proof that filtering opium through the gaps in the skin destroys neither the nature nor the efficacy of the remedy, and that those who insist medicines act only where they're applied are badly mistaken. For an external specific certainly gives off particles which, penetrating the skin, immediately meet the capillary vessels, where they mix with the blood and spread at once through the whole body — since by M. Rohault's calculation of how long the blood takes to circulate, all the blood makes three full circuits in the space of one hour. [Physique, part 4, ch. 14, p. 343.]

These remarks aren't just fine and useful for medicine; they belong here, because they demonstrate beyond argument how easily external particles can enter Jaques Aymar's pores, and from those pores pass into the capillary vessels strewn through the skin, and from there in a few minutes into the heart, and by the circulation of the blood into the whole body.

I'd be accused of skipping what is most conclusive for my subject if I failed to discuss the magnetic cure of diseases by Transplantation, and the magnetic cure of wounds by the Powder of Sympathy and by the ointment called Unguentum Armarium; for according to the learned, these cures work by streams of fine matter which, after spreading among the particles of the air, go on to insinuate themselves into the wound, or into the sick person's whole body. Besides, I've already declared that physics should only be pursued with a view to perfecting medicine. So let's see what the able physicists have said on these most curious questions, whose discussion will throw a great deal of light on the subject of this chapter.

OF THE MAGNETIC CURE OF DISEASES BY TRANSPLANTATION.

When a good magnet touches a piece of iron, a magnetic stream of particles flows from the stone and magnetizes the metal — that is, passes the magnet's power into it, as the compass needle shows. Certain physicians claim that the sick, giving off disease-bearing particles, can by this route transfer their disease to something else and free themselves of it — a cure they call magnetic, because of its analogy to the effluvia passing from magnet to iron. The learned are at war on this point. Some say there is indeed a propagation of diseases, only too real, by which a sick man can give his disease without losing it; but that the supposed transplantation is pure fantasy. Hermannus Grube is of this opinion, and maintains, in a little book entitled De transplantatione morborum Analysis nova, printed at Hamburg in 1674, that nothing is more uncertain or less possible than this magnetic cure. Bartholin fights with all his strength for transplantation: he shows by several examples that the thing is possible, and he doesn't fail to call in reason to prove the cure is entirely natural. The Journal des Savants published, some years ago, an extract of this thoroughly curious book. A great number of able men hold the opinion Bartholin followed, which he explains very clearly by the philosophy of corpuscles.

Here, roughly, is how the Journal des Savants describes it. The transplantation of diseases is when a person is cured of an illness by passing it to some animal, or to a tree, or to a plant. Thus Bartholin says one person struck by a quartan fever was cured by putting warm bread under his armpit and feeding it, all soaked with that sweat, to a dog; and another was cured of jaundice by making a cake kneaded of urine and flour and feeding it to a cat. Robert Fludd relates how, by means of transplantation, one Joannes Ramelius Pharamundus cured the gout without fail. This doctor of medicine, says Robert Fludd, took toenails and hair from the gouty man's legs and put them into a hole he bored in the trunk of an oak down to the pith; having plugged the hole with a peg of the same wood, he covered the top with cow-dung. If the disease didn't return within three months, he concluded the oak had had force enough to draw the whole disease into itself.
