[Philosophia Mosaica, bk. 2, memb. 2, fol. 120, sect. 2.] This learned Englishman claims the transplantation works entirely naturally, by the outflow of the Mummy — the spirits residing in the blood — which can be made to pass into an animal, a tree, or a plant: "Anima spiritualis cujus sedes est in sanguine microcosmico ex corpore humano, mediante quadam substantia magnetica ex eodem subjecto electa, extrahi potest, atque in bestiam, arborem, vel plantam transplantari; ita ut hac etiam ratione morbus depelli possit ab eo in dictas creaturas transferri."

He proves this outflow of spirits by the example of dogs that, having lost their master, picked him out at a great fair, followed everywhere he had gone — though he was on horseback — and finally found him, guided by the scent of the specific Mummy ceaselessly perspiring from the master's body, which left traces of his person in the air long after he was gone. Granting this as settled, nothing more is needed for the transplantation of diseases than to find a material the Mummy of the diseased part can easily attach itself to, so that this material serves it as a vehicle for transport into an animal, a tree, or a plant — or, as Paracelsus puts it, gets it adopted by animals or vegetables. So, according to Robert Fludd: for phthisis, or consumption of the lungs, apply flax-seed or juniper-seed over the region of the heart; for dropsy, put pimpernel or wormwood on the patient's belly; for ruptures and bruises, take plantain or St. John's wort; on tumors and wounds, apply persicaria, or the lesser or greater comfrey; for diseases of the teeth and eyes, use the spotted persicaria. Along with the seed or the plant, a little prepared earth is also applied to the ailment, then mixed with other earth in which the seed or plant is planted. The plants are left to grow until they've drawn the Mummy into themselves; after which they're burned with the earth, if the disease is moist; or set to dry, if the disease is excessive in neither heat nor moisture — and as the plant dies and dries, the sick man recovers. If the disease comes of heat, as in consumptives, the plant and the earth are thrown into running water. Finally, if the plant soaked with the disease-particles is fed to some animal more robust than the patient, the beast will take the disease and the patient will be rid of it. That is the operation of the transplantation of diseases, as Robert Fludd teaches it and as he and his friends practiced it. Note that this Mummy is collected not only by unnoticed perspiration, but also from sweat, urine, blood, hair, or by gathering what flakes off the skin when you scratch it somewhat hard.

EXPERIMENTS. 1. So an English nobleman cured a man of jaundice at a great distance, provided he had some of his urine. He did it this way: he mixed the urine with ash-wood ashes and formed 3, or 7, or 9 little balls; at the top of each ball he made a hole, put in a saffron leaf, and filled it with the same urine. Then he set the balls aside where no one touched them; and from that moment the disease began to fade. Robert Fludd affirms that more than 100 people of every rank were cured this way by this English lord. 2. So Balthasar Wagner affirms he often cured redness and inflammation of the eyes by applying, bound tightly to the nape of the neck, mallow-root gathered when the sun is near the fifteenth degree of Virgo. 3. Take nail-parings from a dropsical man's feet and hands, fasten them to the back of a crayfish, and throw it into the river: the patient is soon cured. 4. Rub warts hard, almost to bleeding, with a piece of beef, and bury the meat — as it rots, the warts dry up and disappear. 5. So a man of quality used to cure the gout by laying on the seat of the pain a piece of beef moistened with a little wine; lifting it six hours later, he found it quite rotted, and fed it to a dog, into which the disease passed after several such operations. Epilepsy can be cured the same way. 6. Panarolus says that if you touch hemorrhoids with a tuberose bulb, experience shows they dry up as the bulb dries; and if it rots, the same will happen to the hemorrhoids — which is why he strongly recommends setting the bulb to dry by the chimney. [fascicul. arcanor. 1, p. 210.]

Several physicians have risen against this magnetic cure and called it superstitious. Bartholin — whom Frommann calls the shining star of Denmark — answers that it contains no superstition whatever, since only natural things are used, and everything is done without words, without symbols, and without any ceremony. He adds that there are examples in holy Scripture; that Moses practiced something similar; and even the Son of God, when He made the demons pass from the body of a possessed man into swine. [Cent. 5, histor. 56.] And prompted by Hermannus Grube's little book against the transplantation of diseases, Bartholin composed a letter establishing, by reason and by experiments both sacred and profane, that this transplantation is a true and natural thing. The arguments he leans on most are drawn from unnoticed perspiration, and from the streams of fine matter escaping through the pores of the human body: "Data per Grubium occasione transplantationem morborum defendi posse existimavi, & ratione & experientia. Rationem e poris, corporum effluviis, advocavi. Experientiis tam sacris, quam profanis, quae in dubium vocari non possunt, rationes roboravi."

Since you can catch a disease through the pores, why couldn't you get rid of one the same way? I've never seen good arguments against this transplantation of diseases. More than half the question is already settled by the certainty — the sad experience we have — that there are diseases, like the plague, that pass between people with terrible ease. All that would remain to settle is whether the person who passes on the disease loses it. Here I would draw a distinction and proceed methodically. 1. I would say that a disease deeply rooted in the blood, in the humors, and in the marrow of the bones could not be transplanted in such a way that the patient is quit of it. A gouty man, for example, who inherited his disease from the very man who gave him life, owns it outright, and I'd strongly doubt transplantation could get him out of the affair. The same goes for a kidney stone, a ruptured vein in the body, a lost eye, and so on. 2. It's different with a disease that hasn't grown old or sunk deep roots; I'd readily believe its transplantation could happen entirely naturally — provided the subjects are present, within the cloud of particles perspiring from the sick man.

EXPERIMENTS. 1. So Frommann affirms that a schoolboy with a malignant fever gave it to a dog he put to sleep in his bed; the schoolboy escaped it, and the dog died of it. [De Fascinat. magic. p. 1014, §34.] 2. Thomas Bartholin relates how his uncle, who had a very violent colic, was cured of it by a dog laid against his belly, into which it passed. He says his maidservant, putting the same dog against her cheek, was relieved of a very sharp toothache; and when the dog had run off, its movements and cries showed plainly that the pain had passed into it. 3. Hoffmann says a man tormented by gout was delivered of it by a dog that caught it from sleeping in his bed; and from time to time the poor animal had the gout, as its master had had it before. [l.c. p. 967.] 4. Borellus says this is the true means of diagnosing the diseases hidden in the human body. Put a little dog to sleep for a fortnight with a sick man, he says; feed it the leavings of what the sick man eats, and let it lick his spittle, and it will certainly take the person's disease. After that you have only to open the dog, and you find, in the part that contracted the malady, the patient's part that needs treating. He even prescribes putting little dogs in a gouty man's bed, because they draw off at least part of the disease — so that you see them, in a short time, in a state where they can hardly stand. [Borellus Cent. 3, observ. 28.]

I don't believe anyone can fault these kinds of transplantations, which are founded on the outflow of the disease-particles that unnoticed perspiration drains from the sick man's body. You would have to be of a very sour humor not to praise in this the order of nature — or rather the infinite wisdom of the Creator, who left men so easy a means of remedying the terrible number of infirmities they suffer through the whole course of their lives. Accordingly Christianus Frommann, who examined without prejudice everything said for and against the transplantation of diseases, declares — with a moderation worthy of a philosopher — that the honor of the marvels worked by transplantation must not be taken from nature and handed to the demon. He wrote a dissertation on the subject; and after hearing the parties, who plead their cause hotly, he settles the dispute in four paragraphs containing his opinion, of which I'll report only the headings, referring the curious to the work itself, where they'll find his reasons, which are not frivolous. 1. The cure of diseases by transplantation must not be banished from the domain of nature: "Transplantationem morborum curam non esse simpliciter a censu rerum naturalium proscribendam dico." [p. 1021.] 2. Whatever is alleged for or against transplantation, it must be admitted that in so obscure a matter there are difficulties on both sides: "Quaecunque transplantationis causa in re hac obscura adducitur, ea, fateor, sua non caret difficultate." [p. 1028.] 3. Those who, examining the sympathetic cure, attribute its principal cause to the spirit that keeps harmony among all the parts of the universe seem to me to point to its most plausible cause: "Qui spiritui universi in sympathetico transplantationis negotio primas deferunt, probabiliorem videntur assignare causam." [p. 1035.] 4. Although the transplantation of diseases is natural, I would not want it used often, and the physician must be very circumspect here, for fear of scandalizing the ignorant and damaging his reputation: "Quamvis transplantatio morborum sit naturalis, ejus tamen usus sit rarus, & circumspectus in hoc sit Medicus, propter tam metuendum scandalum, &c." [p. 1046.]

But if you want a witness even more unimpeachable in every respect than Frommann, I'll give you Father Lana the Jesuit, a man celebrated for his piety and for his deep study of physics. When he wants to prove that particles travel through the air very far from the substance they perspire from, he cites the transplantation of diseases, and says: I pass over the transplantations of diseases for now; yet I have learned, by experiments of my own, that these transplantations happen even at a great distance. "Omitto morborum transplantationes, quarum aliquas etiam ad magnam distantiam fieri didici propriis experimentis." [De motu Transpirat. bk. 2, ch. 2, Proposit. 4, p. 56.] This Jesuit Father teaches us not only that he considers the transplantation of diseases entirely natural, but that he has run experiments on it himself that succeeded, even with a great distance between the sick man and the subject into which he passed the disease.

OF THE MAGNETIC CURE OF WOUNDS BY THE POWDER OF SYMPATHY.

The cure of wounds by the Powder of Sympathy is by now familiar to everyone, so I won't linger over it. Its effect runs exactly opposite to the cure by transplantation: in transplantation, the disease-particles pass from the sick man into the animal or plant; here, on the contrary, the balsamic particles in the vitriol that makes up the Powder of Sympathy pass into the sick man's wound. But both cures work by the outflow of particles — which is the occult mechanism of nature in her marvelous operations. Nothing more curious or more learned could be wished on this subject than the excellent discourse the illustrious Chevalier Digby, an Englishman, delivered publicly before the University of Montpellier — where he had gone, out of horror at seeing the infamous Cromwell reign in England to the injury of the august royal family. He can be counted among those who did the most to restore the corpuscular philosophy, the very idea of which had then nearly been lost.

Here is how the Powder of Sympathy is prepared. Take whatever quantity you like of Roman vitriol, toward the end of July or the beginning of August — that is, when the sun is in the sign of the Lion. Dissolve the vitriol in water (rainwater is best); then filter the water through blotting paper. That done, set the water over a low fire to evaporate, and the next morning you'll find at the bottom of the glass the vitriol in little stones of a very beautiful green, which are set out in the sun to calcine and whiten. This dissolving, filtering, coagulating, and calcining is done three times, to make the substance of the vitriol purer and more uniform. After that the whole is exposed to the sun's rays so the vitriol finishes calcining and whitening completely. That is what's called simple Powder of Sympathy. If you want the compound version, add half its quantity of gum Tragacanth or Arabic, ground to an almost impalpable powder. This marvelous powder is kept in a glass vial in a very dry place, because the least humidity would turn it back into vitriol. Some practitioners use the vitriol straight from the druggist, and find it serves them well enough.

You must not touch the vitriol with a knife while preparing the Powder of Sympathy. This isn't superstition, as some ignorant people have believed, but good sense: the spirits of vitriol fly to iron with great ease, and the powder would be stripped of the very volatile spirits in which all its virtue consists. The powder is put not on the wound but on a linen cloth, or on a sword, wherever there's blood or pus. The wound is kept covered with very white linen; the linen is lifted every day, and a little fresh Powder of Sympathy is sprinkled on the matter it draws from the wound — continuing until a complete cure. Take care not to keep the cloth carrying blood and powder in too hot a place, or inflammation will set into the wound; nor must the place be too cold or too damp. This powder stops blood-loss, soothes toothache, and greatly reduces all kinds of pain in any part of the body — not by putting powder on the part, but on blood drawn from it, wrapped in a linen cloth. The most experienced users say the cloth must sometimes be moved to a different spot according to the changing condition of the wound: if great heat is felt in it, put the cloth somewhere cool. Good sense teaches this without need of further detail. I'll only add that the celebrated Father Lana the Jesuit — one of the greatest philosophers of our time, as his great work Magisterium naturae & artis shows — declares he has often used the Powder of Sympathy with much success; that there is, in its preparation and use as I've just described them, no superstition, still less any pact with the demon; and that nature acts there entirely alone, through the effluvia of the fine matter of the vitriol, which are the intermediate agents — so that in this admirable cure the wound and the vitriol touch each other by a physical contact: "Propterea cum vis hujus medicamenti omnis sita sit in partibus volatilibus, seu effluviis ipsius vitrioli (neque ulla intercedit superstitio, aut pactum cum daemone) ejus praeparationem & usum hoc loco describere opportunum existimavi; quae ego ipse multoties certissimis experimentis comprobavi." [Lana de motu transpirat. bk. 2, artific. 11.]

OF THE MAGNETIC CURE OF WOUNDS BY THE OINTMENT CALLED UNGUENTUM ARMARIUM.

This ointment is applied to the sword, or to the iron that made the wound, and it cures the patient at a great distance, without seeing him at all. There have been furious disputes over this ointment. Tempers can scarcely run higher than the physicists' have over this wholly marvelous cure; they've proved that philosophers have bile as well as phlegm. I'll say this: the most unbridled combatants in this contest were the least qualified to judge it. Having been curious enough to read what's been written on the matter, I noticed that the loudest voices belonged to people to whom the corpuscular philosophy was completely unknown — without which, nevertheless, it's certain you will never understand anything in all that is occult and marvelous in nature, since the corpuscles are the little invisible agents by which she works her miracles. Some have claimed this cure — which is real, not imaginary — is a pure effect of nature; others have attributed it to the demon; and some have asserted it was nothing but a fraud, and that no one was ever cured this way. Francis Bacon, Chancellor of England, Van Helmont, and Goclenius speak of this ointment as a thing that cures naturally.

But Father Lana the Jesuit, examining this ointment — whose composition he reports after Francis Bacon — says the surprising effect of the cure can perfectly well be explained by the effluvia of particles coming off the highly spirituous and highly volatile ingredients of which the Unguentum armarium is made. And if anything, he says, seemed to make this cure suspect, it would surely be the great distance between the sword the remedy is applied to and the sick man who is cured. But certainly that should be no more of an obstacle than the immense distance that sometimes lies between wine — fermenting, for example, in Germany while the grapes flower in Gaul: "maxime obstare videretur ingens distantia, quae aliquando intercedit inter vinum, e.g. effervescens in Germania, dum uvae florent in Gallia." [De motu transpirat. bk. 2, ch. 2, proposit. 22, p. 70.] And Father Lana is so little troubled by this distance — the only real difficulty — that he seems ready to believe these streams of fine matter might travel as far as the stars; he even adds that if the atoms perspiring from the terrestrial globe were not carried up to the stars, and then brought back from the stars to the earth, as in a perpetual ebb and flow, there would be no physical commerce between heaven and earth. [Idem ibid. proposit. 44, p. 63.]

After that, the reader won't mind learning the recipe for this marvelous ointment. It's in the Natural Magic of Battista Porta, bk. 8, ch. 12, who credits its invention to Paracelsus. It's in Goclenius's treatise de Unguento armario, who says Paracelsus perfected the secret but did not discover it. Francis Bacon, Chancellor of England, also gives its composition in Sylva sylvarum, cent. 10, n. 998. Father Lana the Jesuit copied Bacon's and put it in the second volume of his Philosophy [bk. 2, ch. 1, Experiment. 76, p. 43]; and here it is as I've taken it from Goclenius: Take of moss grown on the skull of a strangled man, two ounces; of Mummy, and of human blood, each half an ounce; of earthworms washed in water or wine and dried, two ounces; of human fat, two ounces; of the fat of a bear, and of a boar, each half an ounce; of linseed oil and of turpentine, each two drachms. These are mixed in a mortar, and the compound is kept in a long, narrow vial. It is made when the Sun is in the sign of Libra. Into this ointment goes the iron that made the wound, if it can be had; otherwise another, gently introduced into the wound, which has soaked up the blood and the animal spirits residing there. Every morning the wound is washed with the patient's urine, or with very pure water; after cleaning it well, it's bound with white, very clean linen. The iron must be anointed often if you want a quick cure; if not, it's left a day or two untouched. Goclenius says it's a very well-known fact that the Emperor Maximilian used this ointment.

Since everything that could raise any difficulty about Jaques Aymar's symptoms, and about the magnetic cures, comes down to knowing for certain whether the matter of perspiration is as abundant as I've said, I think it right to close this chapter with two fine observations of Father Lana the Jesuit, which put the matter beyond question.

1. Father Lana was so convinced that perspiration flows very abundantly — above all in sleep — that he taught a way of collecting from it a watery matter, perhaps more exquisite than the tinctures the alembic draws from plants. EXPERIMENT. You need a small chamber, very tightly sealed, so the air cannot leave it. A hole is made in a window, into which is set a matrass with a very long neck, so that the body of the flask is exposed to the cold outside air — for this experiment must be done in winter. That done, if several people sleep in this room, the effluvia of their perspiration, finding no exit but the hole in the window, will pass into the vessel; and the cool air will condense them into water. [Lana de mot. transpirat. bk. 2, ch. 5, artific. 2, pp. 73–74.]

2. Father Lana the Jesuit says something far more surprising. He maintains that we live in the air surrounding our body as in a perpetual bath, now cold, now temperate, now hot; that the body steeps in this liquid air, and sits in it as if an infusion were being made of it; and that nature then works like the chymists, drawing by this infusion delicate tinctures of the subtle spirits of our body, with which the air remains thoroughly impregnated. I declare this is entirely favorable to my system, and that it gives me great pleasure to see such great physicists say it first. It's astonishing that this fine physics was discovered so late.

Let no one be surprised any longer, then, that Jaques Aymar — a man of delicate sensitivity — falls into convulsive movements when he finds himself in air as corrupted as the air three infamous murderers have just passed through. You'd need an amusingly twisted imagination to convince yourself these symptoms are the raptures suffered by the Priestess of Apollo on the tripod before delivering her oracles — as was rather flippantly suggested in a letter that appears in the Mercure Galant of January 1693. That supposed rapture is a fantasy. How often does one feel a certain faintness of heart in the company of certain unclean people, whose presence one can't endure? It takes only slightly corrupted air to make a great change in our constitution. Would you step without repugnance into a bath a disgusting person had just left?

Be that as it may, here are Father Lana's words, which are very fine, and which can prompt most useful reflections: "Ut proinde dici possit aerem, quo corpora singula ambiuntur, esse veluti liquorem aliquem in quo fiat similis omnium corporum infusio, & quo mediante, longe delicatiores, & subtiliores tincturae, seu spiritus puriores a corporibus omnibus eliciantur, natura chymicam, ut ita dicam, artem exercente." [Lana vol. 2, bk. 2, ch. 1, num. 151, p. 82: So that it may be said that the air, with which all bodies are surrounded, is as it were a liquor in which a like infusion of all bodies is made; and by means of which far more delicate and subtle tinctures, or purer spirits, are drawn from all bodies — nature exercising, so to speak, the chymical art.]

Anything I could say after that would only weaken the fine ideas the reader will have taken from this ingenious reflection of Father Lana.

[End of Chapter Nine]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER TEN

The particles of vapors, exhalations, and unnoticed perspiration are fine enough to work their way into the Divining Rod.

Here is a difficulty some people press hard. They'd readily grant that these particles slip easily into Jaques Aymar's pores; but they balk when we say the same atoms penetrate just as easily into the little empty spaces scattered between the fibers of the rod. That's what must now be proved, and I'll do it with curious, incontestable experiments — though I've already shown more than once the fit between the pores of the rod's wood and the particles of water, of metals, and of perspiration. But nothing should be spared when it comes to convincing everyone on a matter about which I've never met anyone indifferent. So an observation I've already scattered pieces of through the preceding chapters gets a chapter of its own.

EXPERIMENT. 1. The marvelous experiments of the magnet are well suited to give a fair idea of the astonishing fineness and agility of the corpuscles. Saint Augustine relates, in Book 21, ch. 4, of the City of God, how his brother and colleague Severus, dining one day with Bathanarius, Governor of Africa, watched that lord take a magnet-stone and hold it under a silver plate with a piece of iron on top: the iron followed every movement of his hand, while the silver between the two registered nothing. So magnetic particles pass in an instant through the pores of silver, gold, or copper, moving the iron resting above and impressing on it every motion given to the magnet beneath. No one should be surprised: a compass needle can be moved through a wall; and the gentlemen of the Royal Society of England assure us that Doctor Edward Cotton presented them a magnet-stone weighing 60 pounds which, though weak, still moved a compass needle at a distance of nine feet. [Act. Philosoph. month of March 1666, p. 25.]

Few bodies in nature are as hard and as dense as glass, and yet the magnetic particles pass through it. You can see this by fixing an iron wire in place and moving a magnet beneath it: the magnetic matter, by the motion it causes in the wire, carries its impression through the glass. But since someone might imagine the particles acted not through the glass but by rising around and over it, the way smoke from a snuffed torch would, the experiment has been pushed further. A sewing needle was sealed inside a little glass tube, hermetically closed; it was set floating on water, and with a good magnet it was drawn this way and that at will — proof that the magnet drove its particles through the glass.

We have an experiment of M. Boyle's on cochineal that is beautifully designed to show the divisibility of matter to infinity; I find it more conclusive than the gold-division example M. Rohault borrowed from the gold-beaters. It's certainly more vivid. And it shows how far we are from grasping the surprising fineness of the particles that come off certain bodies.

EXPERIMENT. M. Boyle took one grain of cochineal, dissolved it in a moderate quantity of spirit of urine, and with this solution tinged red 164 ounces of very clear water. Reckon each ounce at 576 grains, and you have 152,064 grains of water flooded with the substantial particles separated from a single grain of cochineal. Give this experiment to chew on to anyone who asks whether a small object like a coin can shed enough fine matter to soak the Divining Rod. I have in my hands a little magnet-stone weighing only 20 grains, which has passed its magnetic power to a very large number of little pieces of iron without yet losing anything of its weight — though what it passes on is most certainly a material substance.

M. Boyle also mentions a witty lady who amused herself raising silkworms, from whom he learned that one of the cocoons they spin around themselves holds more than 300 ells of that fine silk thread — and that those 300 ells weighed only two and a half grains. So you can reckon a single grain of this thread contains at least 120 ells. [De mira subtilit. effluvior. part 3.]

Nothing demonstrates the fineness of vapors and exhalations better than quicksilver. That liquid metal gives off fumes so fine and so penetrating that if you stir it with one hand, you'll find that a piece of gold clenched tight in the other comes out coated and whitened by the quicksilver that has worked its way through the hand. The same happens to a gold coin held in the mouth, as the gilders often learn. Mercury insinuates itself so thoroughly that if you put it with gold, silver, tin, etc., its particles penetrate the pores of those metals so forcefully that, hard as they are, they soften and reduce to a paste called amalgam. Seal quicksilver in a little copper tube and heat it slightly, and the quicksilver penetrates the copper and passes through as through a sieve.

PHENOMENA. There's no doubting that the particles evaporating from mines are also fine enough to slip into the pores of the Divining Rod. Here is solid proof. Alexander ab Alexandro relates how, over the gold mines in Germany, vine-branches have sometimes been found entirely gilded, and even some leaves of pure gold. This happens, he says, because the earth in those places contains metallic atoms that enter the plants through the root, just as the juices meant for growth would. As for the fact itself, this author won't tolerate any doubt of it: he affirms not only that the occurrence isn't rare, but that several Dukes and Princes who were presented such branches, and keep them in their cabinets, can testify to it; and that celebrated physicians, consulted about the prodigy, attributed it to the gold-vapors over those mines: "Suxisse ex radicibus coalescens aurum & ita germina concipere, unde aureas emittere crediderunt." [Genial. dierum. bk. 4, ch. 9, p. 199: They believed it drew up gold coalescing from the roots, and so conceived buds, whence it put forth golden shoots.]

In fact, the distance between metals and plants is smaller than people think. Not only do some plants admit metallic particles through the pores of their roots — metals themselves have been found to vegetate: to grow, sometimes, into trees, dividing into roots, trunk, and branches. The observations of the Curious of Nature in Germany mention a piece of vegetated gold that a peasant found in the river Tartza in Hungary, now to be seen in the Emperor's cabinet among other rarities of nature. Other vegetated metals are kept in several cabinets across Europe. And the Curious of Nature in Germany say these vines and trees drew up the metallic particles through their roots — very fluid substances, easily able to slip into the little spaces by which roots take their nourishment: "Denique putant vites & arbores per radices suas attraxisse humorem metallicum, fluidum, adeoque facile obsequentem." [p. 262.] Father Kircher says this seepage of metallic humor into plant roots happens all the faster when the plants growing there have pores matched to the fine matter of the metal — for then the root draws into the gaps between its fibers the nourishment suited to it. [De Magnet. bk. 3, ch. 5.]

These particles we're discussing are of a prodigious fineness — fine enough to penetrate walls and the most solid bodies. Sympathetic ink is perfectly suited to letting you touch the point with your finger.

EXPERIMENT. SYMPATHETIC INK. 1. The secret of sympathetic ink lies in two different waters which, perfectly clear on their own, turn opaque and very dark brown when mixed. They're made like this. Boil, for a quarter of a half-hour, half a half-pint of distilled vinegar with about an ounce of litharge of silver in it. That's the first. The second is made with a piece of quicklime and a little orpiment, set to steep for 24 hours in a sufficient quantity of water, using new, very clean glazed earthen pots. The two liquids are filtered separately, and come out perfectly transparent. Here's the use. Write with the first water whatever you don't want seen — the writing vanishes the moment it dries; but when the recipient passes over the paper a sponge just barely moistened with the second water, the writing emerges in a reddish color tending to black. When these waters are freshly made, and care has been taken to keep the quicklime pot well covered, the moistened sponge needn't even touch the writing to bring it out; passing it at a slight distance is enough. It has been seen several times that the lime-water was so potent that, with the letter written in the first water spread on a table and covered with a quire of paper, pouring the second water on the top sheet — the only one wetted — still made the letter's writing darken.

SYMPATHETIC INK, WHERE THE VAPOR OF A LIQUOR PENETRATES A BOOK OR A WALL. 2. Though this experiment is nearly the same as the previous one, which is M. Rohault's, what differs in it deserves a place here. Get some Impregnation of Saturn, made from lead reduced to powder by calcining: melt lead in an unglazed crock and stir it over the fire with a spatula until it's all powder. The lead-powder is then dissolved in distilled vinegar; this liquid, clear as spring water, is called Impregnation of Saturn. Take a book four fingers thick, or thicker if you like; write with the Impregnation of Saturn on a paper you place between the leaves of the book; turn the book over, and having roughly marked the spot opposite your writing, rub the last leaf with a cotton soaked in the liquid made with the orpiment-lime; even leave the cotton on the spot; immediately put a doubled paper over it, and having quickly closed the book, give it four or five blows with the hand; then turn it over and put it under a press somewhere for a quarter of a half-hour; take it out, open it, and you'll see that your ink, which was invisible, has appeared. The same thing works through a wall, provided you take care to set planks against both sides to keep the spirits from evaporating away.

Cheats have sometimes used these secrets — passing themselves off as great sorcerers — to make answers appear, on carefully sealed blank papers, to questions posed by simple, ignorant people. The people naturally believed the Devil had written the answer; and on the depositions of these simple dupes, ignorant judges have condemned to death supposed criminals who were no more guilty, and no more sorcerers — though better physicists — than the judges themselves. The physical cause of these pleasing phenomena lies in the force of the lime-water; and that force consists in volatile spirits that pass through bodies with marvelous subtlety, and carry very far. That's why M. Lémery warns that the two liquids must be compounded — and perhaps even stored — in different places, for fear the volatile spirits of the lime should spoil and, so to speak, kill the Impregnation of Saturn if brought near it.

To apply these considerations to our subject: the vapors of metals can clearly pierce the pores of the Divining Rod with great ease. They are ordinarily fumes of mercury, which are astonishingly fine. I could settle for proving it by the testimony of several learned men, who assure us mercury pierces and works its way even into the marrow of the bones, and has likewise been found in the skulls of those who follow the gilder's trade. But here is an experiment of M. Boyle's that anyone can try and be convinced of how easily mercury penetrates even the most solid wood.

EXPERIMENTS. 1. Few people don't know what a long wooden blowpipe is — the kind used in the Maldives to shoot arrows, or in Europe to kill birds with little balls of baked clay blown by mouth. M. Boyle says that, having filled such a blowpipe with quicksilver to a certain height, the weight — of the air or of the quicksilver — forced the portion at the very bottom to pierce the wood and ooze out through the pores in little drops, as if it had passed through a chamois skin — the way it does when squeezed out of an amalgam paste. Which made, says M. Boyle, a rather pleasing show for those present. 2. Not only does quicksilver pass through the pores of wood — air does too. Here's an experiment M. Boyle once performed before several witty people, who found it a thoroughly entertaining spectacle. He fitted a board to the pneumatic machine and pumped out the air; he was agreeably surprised to find the air pressing on the board work its way through the pores of the wooden plank and enter the machine. The air, he says, then did what the quicksilver had done in the previous experiment. He adds three further experiments in which he made the vapors of a liquid of his own composition pass through wooden planks and visibly color copper coins. Note that all these experiments were done without the fumes being stirred up by any heat, which would surely have made them more active and more penetrating. "Quod gratissimum erat, ac jucundissimum intuentibus spectaculum." [Boyle de Corpor. solid. porosit. ch. 4, pp. 18, 20: Which was a most welcome and most pleasant spectacle to the beholders.] 3. There's scarcely a denser body than the steel of a sword with a well-tempered, well-polished blade. Its pores are presumed very tight; yet the particles coming off an animal's blood still force a passage, work their way in, and stay a very long time in those little pores — and neither the outside air nor the cloth you wipe the sword with can drive them out. Only fire can make these spirits of the blood evaporate. Hold the sword over burning coals, and you'll see appear, on the side of the blade away from the fire, a little moisture like the mist breath leaves on a mirror; look at it with a strong magnifying glass and you'll see this dew of spirits consists of tiny swollen bubbles or blisters. This experiment teaches surgeons to gauge the depth of a wound without probing it, since the little effervescence I've described appears only on the part of the sword that entered a living body. It also teaches us that the blood-particles of the people massacred at Lyon, having stayed in the billhook with which the murderers killed the wine merchant and his wife, are what let the man with the rod tell that billhook apart from the two others by the same maker, among which it had been deliberately mixed to test his talent. 4. Father Lana the Jesuit reports an experiment of his own that shows the strange fineness of water particles. More than once, he says, I have melted a very thin glass tube at the enameler's lamp and drawn it out into a thread so fine it could rival spider silk for thinness. Yet this almost imperceptible thread was hollow along its whole length. The bore must have been of a smallness scarcely imaginable — the eyes could detect nothing of it; I myself only learned the thread was hollow because I bent one into a siphon, and we watched the water climb up one branch of the siphon and descend by the other; but it moved so slowly, because of the tininess of the bore, that only one drop fell in four hours, though the water never stopped climbing. From which we should judge that those water particles must have been of extraordinary fineness. [Lana de motu penetrat. bk. 1, ch. 2, Proposit. 23, p. 31.] 5. Venomous animals show us vividly the extreme fineness of subtle matter, by the way they deliver their venom. Scaliger says there is in Aquitaine a species of spider whose poison is so active and penetrating that if you step on the insect unawares, the venom passes through the shoe and wounds you: "Tanta ejus veneni vis, ut calcatus calceorum soleas transmiserit cujusdam Vincentini." [Exercitat. 186, p. 612: So great is the force of its poison that, when trodden on, it passed through the shoe-soles of a certain man of Vincennes.] The History of Brazil mentions a venomous fish that poisons by the merest contact; it's even said to numb and paralyze the fisherman's foot, however warm, much as the European torpedo-fish does. [Piso bk. 3, ch. 1.] Everyone knows the torpedo is a little fish, rarely much over 18 ounces, from which flows a cold humor said to cause the numbness it produces in the fisherman's hand, whether he fishes by hand or with a net. Some maintain you must touch it directly to feel the numbness. Mattioli wrote that no man has an arm strong enough to hold a live torpedo for long. Venom notwithstanding, its flesh is eaten, and Hippocrates sometimes recommends it.

Since experiments confirm reasoning, as reasoning guides, regulates, and explains experiments, the two shouldn't be separated where we can help it. So having seen what nature does, let's hear what reason says. Only a little attention is needed to grasp something of the extreme smallness of the imperceptible particles, and to satisfy yourself that some far outstrip others in fineness.

1. The particles on the trail of a hare that a dog is chasing — the ones that guide him — are certainly finer than the atoms perspiring from musk and ambergris; for the hare's particles escape our sense of smell, while the fragrant particles of perfumes register on it plainly.

2. The particles of air must certainly be finer than the matter perspired by the hare; for that matter registers on the dog's smell, and air falls under the jurisdiction of none of our senses.

3. The rays of the sun are certainly finer than air and water; for the particles of light pass through window-panes, which the particles of air and water cannot do.

4. The magnetic particles flowing from the magnet are certainly finer than the rays of the sun; for magnetic matter moves a compass needle through wood, ivory, and the hardest metals — things impenetrable to the luminous atoms.

5. And perhaps there are particles infinitely finer still than the magnet's. Nothing stops us from judging that those little animals visible only through the microscope have blood composed of particles finer than everything we've just considered. These little animals, which no eye had ever seen before the microscope was invented, surely have organs and ducts for taking and digesting food; they have eggs for propagating their kind; inside those eggs are other, still smaller animals, feeding there. The juices destined for their nourishment must therefore be of a strange fineness. The imagination would lose itself if it had to trace the whole economy of blood-making in these animated atoms, and find there the spirits distributed to every part of the animal to finish forming it. Things one can scarcely conceive are excellent to review now and then, to train the mind in considerations that don't depend on the senses. And nothing serves this philosophical exercise better than examining that little animal at the very start of its life — still wrapped and concentrated in the germ of the egg, where it seems to steal away from our imagination and escape the eyes of the mind, just as nature hides it from the eyes of the body: "Ideo vix concipi potest, quanta sit exilitas, & subtilitas istius alimenti, quod ductus embrionis pervadit," as M. de Stair, an Englishman, puts it so well. [Explorat. 21, n. 4, p. 625: Therefore it can scarcely be conceived how great is the smallness and subtlety of that aliment which passes through the conduits of the embryo.]

6. But what shall we say of the animal spirits, generated in the ventricles of the human brain — volatile atoms which must be of an inconceivable lightness? After all, they are the organs the soul uses to give the body movement by means of the nerves and muscles; they are the little messengers it employs to carry its orders and commands throughout the body; they are, so to speak, a substance midway between the body and the faculties of the soul — which should lead us to imagine them astonishingly fine.

[End of Chapter Ten]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER ELEVEN

The particles of vapors, exhalations, and unnoticed perspiration have force enough to move and tip the Divining Rod, and to produce in Jaques Aymar the symptoms already described.

Jaques Aymar's strange symptoms, and the rod's rapid movement — sometimes violent enough to hurt his hands — are things even the proudest physicists can't pass over. The author of the letter on the rod published in the Mercure of January 1693 made sure to have his fun with this point. Since he thinks and writes with fire, he states the difficulty at full strength: "Do you believe, Monsieur, that there is no absurdity in supposing that from a little piece of metal — a four-sols coin, say — there flows a quantity of particles great enough to twist a rod to the breaking point, or to hurt the hands of the man gripping it?" [p. 32.]

There is no absurdity in believing metals are porous and give off matter — on the contrary, it would be absurd in a physicist to deny it. I've already shown how abundant is the fine matter that escapes, by perspiration, from even the most solid bodies. This perspiration wasn't invented to explain the rod. Sanctorius, Boyle, and Father Lana the Jesuit, who wrote so solidly on unnoticed perspiration, had no Divining Rod in view. Ordinary people are convinced that only causes that act with noise and commotion can be expected to produce great effects — never considering that nature has her quiet, hidden ways of working, in which she employs little agents the senses can't grip. These operations are often visible in their results. We see plants feed and grow, for example, but nature hides the how: no one has ever watched the juices of growth slip into a plant's pores. We see the rod dip, but the way it happens isn't perceptible. The particles that impress the movement on it are neither visible nor tangible; and the extreme smallness we attribute to them may even worsen the difficulty for those who can't accept that nature contains invisible agents this powerful. Nevertheless, everyone raised in the principles of true philosophy maintains that these particles — these little wedges nature uses in her mechanics — are the stronger and more active the finer they are. I'll demonstrate it in the rest of this chapter: 1. by very fine experiments; 2. by unanswerable reasons.

I. Consider that the extreme smallness of the particles is offset by the enormous number of atoms making up the vapors and exhalations, and that they act together, per modum unius [as a single thing], and perhaps you'll protest less at our crediting the rod's rapid dip to their united force. Have you forgotten that the torrents and floods that sometimes ravage the countryside and drown whole provinces start out as nothing but vapors — imperceptible at first, spread in the air — which condense into raindrops, and which, united, cause those frightful inundations of which we have only too many examples? These invisible vapors, before they ever form rain, make themselves felt plainly enough in the extraordinary difficulty of opening and closing doors and windows, however solid and dense the wood. A sure sign the weather is setting toward rain. Everyone has heard of those new instruments called hygrometers, barometers, and thermometers. They're known to be the ornament of the cabinets of the curious and the learned, serving to read the degrees of dryness or humidity, of cold or heat, and every change in the air. But perhaps not everyone knows that the whole secret of these machines rests on the discovery that the vapors and exhalations mixed in the air make it cold or hot, dry or humid, more or less heavy; and that this is read off from the action these vapors and exhalations exert on the material the instruments are made of — which we should regard as standing proof of the action and force of the corpuscles. These little machines, the delight of clever people, are in the taste of the times; and besides, they're well suited to helping the imagination of those who can't believe vapors and exhalations capable of an action as strong as the rod's rapid dip. Two reasons more than sufficient to discuss these fine curiosities of physics here.

HYGROMETERS. 1. What's called a hygrometer, or hygroscope, is an instrument that registers the dryness or humidity of the air. One invented in England — its description and figure are in the Journal des Savants of 1677 — is made of two very thin fir boards moving in two grooves as the humidity or dryness of the air makes them swell or shrink. Their movement turns a needle at the middle of one board, which marks the degrees of humidity or dryness.

SECOND HYGROMETER. Since Father Emanuel Maignan found the secret of making a hygrometer from a single straw of a perfectly ripe ear of wild oats, topped with a pointer, everyone has taken up this design, which has become the most famous. A little straw from this oat-ear is set, the way you'd plant a pivot, in the bottom of a little box like those of Dieppe sundials or compasses; the rim of the box is divided into sixty degrees; on the tip of the straw is fixed a pointer reaching the scale. The straw, twisting or untwisting with dryness or humidity, then marks on the rim by how many degrees the air is drier or damper than the day before. EXPERIMENTS. 1. Bring this hygrometer within five or six paces of a fire and you'll see it move slowly enough; but at three or four feet, it turns so visibly that it's a pleasure to watch. If the straw is a little long, it will make as many as four full turns. 2. When the straw has reached the last degree of dryness, it stops turning. It's then twisted and all curled; but touch it with a drop of water and you see it straighten at once, retrace its steps with opposite turns, and stand quite straight again. 3. The straw's movement near fire isn't strictly continuous; it goes by jumps and starts. 4. With humidity, the straw turns from east through south to west; with dryness, the reverse, from east through north to west. 5. Put little bits of this straw on a well-heated iron plate and you'll see them wriggle, bend, and straighten like little worms — like the snippets of lute-string strewn on meat fresh off the spit, a little prank to make the simple believe worms are crawling on it. Finally, M. de Monconys relates, on p. 130 of the first part of his Travels, how M. Torricelli gave him some oat-straws for making hygrometers, and he ranks this as a great favor. So true is it that Fortune has trained the philosophers to be content with little.

THIRD HYGROMETER. M. Sturmius, having observed that this whole little mystery comes from the natural twisting of plant fibers in the presence of dry or damp — the way green wood twists and writhes before the fire — looked to art for what had so far only been borrowed from nature. He chose a lute-string, judging that nothing could be more sensitive to the smallest changes in the air; and here's what he makes of it. He takes a little cardboard box two inches high and three across. At the bottom, where you'd set a pivot, he glues a piece of lute-string a little over two inches long, standing up above the box; at its tip he glues a little paper figure, which also holds in its hand a little length of the same string reaching to the rim of the box, which is divided into sixty degrees. That done, it's a prodigy to watch this little figure make more than a full turn if you carry the machine down into a cellar or some other damp place, and retrace its steps, though a bit more slowly, when you carry it back to a study or some other dry place. Note that the string wouldn't stand straight if it weren't supported by pieces of cardboard it passes through. M. Sturmius prefers this hygrometer to all the others, both for its sensitivity and because it works just as precisely after several years as the day it was made.

FOURTH HYGROMETER. Who would believe the ear could judge the degrees of dryness and humidity in the air? It can be done, though, like this. Tune a lute string, or that of another instrument, to the pitch of a flute or flageolet — instruments very little affected by changes in the air; put them perfectly in tune; and the next day, or six hours later, if the air has noticeably changed, you'll see by how much the string has risen with dryness or fallen with humidity. It's simple: if the two instruments are still in tune, the weather is the same. If the string sounds sharper, the air is drier; if the pitch has dropped, the weather is damper.

FIFTH HYGROMETER. You can also make a hygroscope with one of those small, free-moving balances. In one pan put salt extracted from some hot-natured plant, or well-calcined niter-salt — substances that soak up humidity so greedily that, with even a little of it in the air, the whole mass dissolves into water and weighs three or four times more than before. When you load this material into one pan, put metal — small lead shot — into the other to counterbalance it exactly. Then however little the weather changes, you see it at once in the balance, which is no longer level: the salt pan sinks lower as the humidity grows, or rises as it falls. You can mount a quarter-circle marked in degrees above the balance, and the needle will then mark the various degrees of humidity and dryness on the arc. Formerly, instead of these salts, people used wool, or a sponge, or some other material that readily takes up the air's moisture; but the salts are incomparably better.

SIXTH HYGROMETER. It's said that formerly, at the Court of Turin, to know whether the hunt could go out the next day, a stag's antler was hung by a string in some open hall; and from its movement they forecast whether the fair weather would hold. If the antler stayed steady, they were confident there'd be no change. M. Sturmius says, very agreeably, that in that country they consulted a brute oracle — "brutum hoc oraculum consulebant." It worked for them; and from the quarter of the sky — east, south, west, or north — toward which the antlers turned, they augured the day's weather in that direction. The experiment is easy for anyone who wants to test whether it's really true.

SEVENTH HYGROMETER. At the Emperor's Court another kind of hygrometer was used, very simple, and with no more magic in it than the others. You make a large round disk of wood, like those used to play draughts, except half a foot wide and a finger thick; divide its rim into 60 degrees; hang it by its center from a thread so that it sits perfectly horizontal — all its parts in exact balance. It must be somewhere the wind can't stir it. Frequent trials have shown that this little machine turns right or left as the air turns dry or humid. To know exactly by how many degrees, just set a little pointer beside it aimed at the scale, and you'll see with pleasure by how much one day outdoes the last in humidity or dryness, and so guess whether the weather will be fair or rainy. To keep the air from jostling this hygrometer and ruining it, it's covered with a kind of glass bell with a hole at the top to give free passage to the thread, so the disk can move unhindered. The physics of all this rests on something thoroughly settled: water vapors spread in the air slip easily into all bodies through their pores. This makes the bodies expand and take up more space, and hence produces the hygrometer's various movements. Everyone knows what happens to doors and windows, hard to shut in damp weather, because everything made of wood, even the hardest and most solid, swells with humidity. In that condition you may as well call them hygrometers. Even curled hair is a hygroscope you carry on your head without thinking about it: it falls flat when the air is damp and announces rain; it curls and crisps when the air is dry, a sign of fair weather. That is the entire mystery and the whole philosophy of hygrometers, which takes only a little attention to grasp: the more moist particles there are in the air, the more of them seep into the material these little oracles of fair and foul weather are made of.

EIGHTH HYGROMETER. Without any of this contrivance, you can make a hygrometer with an ordinary cord. Fasten it by its two ends against a wall, leaving it a little slack. Then tie another cord to its middle, one end hanging down along the wall, with a little lead weight to keep it plumb. To track its movement, mark degrees in the form of a ladder along the wall, and the hygrometer is finished. You can count on this: as the air grows damper, the cord will tighten and the little weight will rise; the drier the air, the slacker the cord, and the lower the weight will sink. This hygrometer is the more to be valued for being easy to build and faithful in marking the degrees of dryness and humidity. You can also use lute or viol strings, strips of parchment or chamois, for this hygrometer or any other you can devise; the changes in the air will show in the same way, by the various movements these materials make.

BAROMETER. The barometer or baroscope follows from the suspension of mercury. It's an instrument of mechanics and physics for measuring the heaviness or lightness of the air. At first it was just a glass tube about 4 feet long, three lines across inside its bore. The top end was sealed shut, and through the bottom it was filled with quicksilver. Then the open end was plunged into a pool of mercury exposed to the air. The mercury in the tube, tending to fall, nevertheless stayed suspended at a height of about 28 inches, more or less, as the air is lighter or heavier. Since then the double barometer has been invented, which is much better and less bulky. It is the one seen on the right side of the following figure. Here, roughly, is how Father Lamy, priest of the Oratory, describes it. It's a glass channel, sealed at one end and open at the other. In this channel, picture two cylindrical chambers, set 27 inches apart. Their capacity, relative to the rest of the channel, is here as 14 to 1. Quicksilver is poured in through the opening, just enough to fill the space from the middle of the lower chamber up toward the middle of the upper one. After that the rest of the channel is filled with some other liquid that won't freeze in winter and can't dissolve the quicksilver — aqua fortis mixed with six times as much plain water serves. When the weight of the air pushes the quicksilver down an inch in the lower chamber, it consequently pushes the quicksilver in the upper chamber up an inch; the water in the rest of the channel then falls toward the lower chamber; and since the chamber's capacity is to the channel's as 14 to 1, the water in the channel drops 14 inches.

[The original here contains a full-page engraving of the double barometer and the anemoscope figure.]

The degrees of this movement are read off a wooden plate that carries the barometer. The curious have used this instrument to make observations I want to record here for the benefit of the many people who own barometers and, not knowing these notes, treat the machines as mere cabinet ornaments. 1. When the weather is calm and rain seems on the way, the mercury usually falls. 2. In fair weather, with the air clear, the mercury usually stands quite high. 3. In high winds, even without rain, the mercury falls more than at any other time, depending on the wind that blows. 4. All else equal, the mercury stands higher with an east or northeast wind. 5. In a calm frost, it is most often high. 6. After violent winds, when the mercury has been very low, it climbs forcefully the moment the first storm gives way. 7. The barometer swings far more in northern countries than in southern. 8. Between the tropics, and near the equinoctial line — as M. Halley testifies, in the English Journal of May 1686, to have found at the island of St. Helena — the mercury changes little in any season.

None of this is a digression. These phenomena the barometer displays — showing the action of the particles of air and vapor on a heavy, insensible matter like mercury — should convince us they act no less on all bodies, and far more on the bodies of certain people, more sensitive and delicate, who never fail to notice it when they pay a little attention.

[There follows here a discussion of the Anemoscope, or "Physical Prophet," a barometer-figure invented by Otto Guericke, Burgomaster of Magdeburg: a little wooden man enclosed in a glass tube who rises as the air becomes heavier and descends — even before the rain begins — as the air discharges. Guericke kept the construction a great secret all his life; in 1660 the air-pressure at Magdeburg dropped so sharply that the little man sank entirely into his tube for two or three hours, and Guericke declared in the assembly that a very great storm must be raging somewhere — which proved true, the wind reaching Magdeburg two hours later. M. de Monconys describes seeing it in October 1663; and M. Comiers at last showed, in a discourse inserted in the Mercure Galant of March 1683, that the Anemoscope is nothing but an application of the Barometer, with the water added above the mercury, as in the double Barometer, to make the rising and falling of the little man more perceptible. The author cites Job 28:25, "Qui fecit ventis pondus, & aquas appendit in mensura" — He who made a weight for the winds, and weighed the waters by measure — as scriptural authority for the heaviness of the air.]

THERMOMETER. Some credit this invention to Robert Fludd, others to Drebbel. The instrument serves to read the degrees of cold and heat in the air. It can also go into a bath, to judge its temperature and regulate the heat you want to give it. It wasn't made as well at first as it is today, though it's now simpler than ever. The thermometer is just a single glass vial with a very long, slender neck. At the lower end is a bulb, much like the one at the bottom of the barometer. Through the top, the bulb and part of the neck are filled with spirit of wine, after which the vial is sealed shut at the enameler's lamp. The tube is mounted, like the barometer, on a wooden plate marked in degrees, to show how far the spirit of wine climbs and expands with heat in the neck of the vial — squeezing the air into a smaller volume, which the air can easily bear, having been greatly expanded by the flame used to melt the glass and seal the top when it was enclosed. Conversely, when the weather turns cold, the spirit of wine contracts, takes up less room, sinks lower, and lets the air spread back out. M. Rohault had a thermometer that marked the degrees of cold and heat very plainly: he affirms that the difference between the greatest and the least height of the spirit of wine was more than three feet.

This section should close with a fine experiment, joined to a piece of reasoning, from the celebrated M. Boyle. I want to show you, he says, by a striking experiment, how much power the parts of the air, and the invisible particles mixed in it, have to act on bodies — to work very considerable changes even on the most solid ones. If an iron rod stands for some time with one end toward the earth, or toward the north, at a window or atop a house, everyone who has written on the magnet tells us that this rod, by long exposure, soaks up the magnetic particles spread in the air and acquires the magnet's power outright. So this effect can only come about because there is in the air an atmosphere of little magnetic bodies that work their way into the iron rod — bodies that have even set in motion the interior parts of the iron, hard and solid as it is, producing a change so complete that the rod becomes a perfect magnet. [Boyle de absolut. quiete in corporib. sect. 5, p. 8.]

That's more than enough to prove that the particles of vapors and exhalations can move and tip the rod, and shake Jaques Aymar to the degree we've described. Basil Valentine himself recognized this force: he affirms that the hazel rod "moves and trembles by the movement of the aerial fumes that introduce themselves into the extremity of the Rod." He adds that the substance of the staff naturally sucks in the vapors of the air so strongly that the rod must sink and tip toward the earth the vapors rise from — unless the rod is too strong and stiff to bend. [Testament, bk. 1, ch. 25 and 26.]

II. There are endless arguments proving the action and force of the vapors, exhalations, and particles of unnoticed perspiration — so many that I'm almost buried under the proofs on offer, from which I'll pick the ones that strike hardest.

1. We've seen that the particles, however fine, have great force when they act united, per modum unius — number making up for whatever they lack in size. Two or three little grains of gunpowder produce no great effect; but when there are many, nothing can resist their force. Fortifications must yield, walls crumble, stones split, rocks crack open and fall in pieces. This happens because, when gunpowder ignites, the acid salts of the sulphur, set loose, penetrate, open, separate, and force apart the volatile, long, stiff parts of the saltpeter, previously tangled in the mixture with the sulphur and charcoal: the sulphur's acid salts, driving in like little wedges between the hard, dense parts of the saltpeter, force them far apart and impress on them a motion so rapid that it overturns everything in its way.

FULMINATING GOLD. But few things in nature show the surprising force of the corpuscles more plainly than fulminating gold — nothing but a gold powder charged with certain spirits, 20 grains of which make more noise and act more violently than half a pound of gunpowder; two grains on the point of a knife, lit at a candle, detonate louder than a musket-shot. It's made from gold filings put in a vial with three times their weight of aqua regia. When the gold has dissolved, the liquid is poured into a glass; six times as much plain water is added; then oil of tartar, or volatile spirit of sal ammoniac, is thrown onto the mixture. The powder that settles to the bottom, dried on its own, is what's called fulminating gold.

FULMINATING POWDER. Fulminating powder, which costs less to make, produces roughly the same effect: three parts niter, two parts salt of tartar, and one part sulphur, pounded and mixed together. Sixty grains of this powder, heated in a spoon, detonates and flies off as forcefully as a cannon. Note that this powder breaks everything beneath it: it directs its effect downward, where gunpowder directs it upward — so if you use copper spoons, you'll find them pierced after the bang.

But speaking of powder — couldn't we say that what gunpowder does to the cannonball, the underground fires do to the vapors and exhalations rising from the earth above the watercourses and the gold and silver mines? And that, just as the ball flies farther from a longer cannon, the deeper the place the particles come from, the higher they must climb into the air? The length of the cannon increases the ball's motion because it gives the powder more time to ignite, and so more time to develop its force and act on the ball before it leaves the muzzle. Isn't it likely, then, that the nearer the particles start to the center of the earth, the more they're pushed and driven by the particles of the underground fires following behind in succession — returning, so to speak, again and again to the charge — until they burst violently into the air? I offer this as a conjecture, but one that doesn't seem groundless; and it would explain a tradition common among the well-finders, who say the vapors seen over a spring rise as high into the air as the watercourse lies deep in the earth. Cassiodorus himself knew this, as appears in one of his letters, which I'll quote when discussing the best way of finding water. Add that the narrowing of the earth's pores, through which these particles must pass, also speeds them up: wind blows more fiercely in a narrow passage, and a river runs faster under a bridge's arch where it's pinched.

Yet Father Malebranche says exactly the opposite. Let the reader judge which of us is right. Here is how he puts it: "Grant whatever power you please to the water and the forked staff," he says, "it seems clear to me that water lying exposed must act more strongly on the rod than when it's hidden under the earth." [Mercure of January 1693, p. 59.] It will seem much clearer, to anyone who thinks it through, that vapors driven by the underground fires have more force and action than those drifting up off a pond — and are therefore more capable of agitating the rod. So many reasons leap to the eye that I'll leave each reader the pleasure of finding them.

III. And what may we not expect from the force of seepage? Nothing can stand against the action of these little wedges — these imperceptible particles that work their way into the pores of the peasant and of the hazel branch. Machines infinitely more solid, of far more impenetrable build, cannot resist these little agents when they operate by seeping in — or, to put it more plainly, when they push or pull one another.

1. Here is an admirable case for grasping how powerful the seepage of moisture into a body can be — what happened at Rome when Sixtus V had the great Obelisk of the Vatican raised. It's said that Fontana, the Pope's celebrated architect, not having foreseen that a mass of one million six thousand and forty-eight pounds would stretch the cables, would have had the grief of watching his enterprise risk failure, but for an unknown voice that cried out to wet the cables. This was promptly done; the cables shortened, and carried that prodigious obelisk onto its base, into the position where it is admired today. Father Kircher, who reports the event, says nothing of this detail of the stretched cables; I read it elsewhere, though I can no longer recall in what book.

2. If that case is doubtful, here is another beyond dispute, proving the force of seepage just as plainly: the way millstones are split. First a rock is cut into a cylinder; then, to divide it into several millstones, a ring of holes is cut around the rock and filled with wedges of willow-wood dried in the oven. The wedges are set in circles around the cylinder at the thickness wanted for each stone; and when the weather turns damp, the wedges swell, break the rock, and split it into as many millstones as there were rings. A prodigious effect, surely, of the force of seepage.

3. Let's consult M. Boyle. He says that one day, wanting to test how far the force of vapors goes when they act by seepage, he hung from the end of a fairly long but thin cord, run over a pulley, a lead weight of a hundred pounds; and when the weather turned to rain, the watery vapors, seeping into the cord, made it swell — which lifted the weight a quite perceptible distance. What he says about the beans called haricots is very entertaining. He once filled glass vessels and earthen vessels with them, added water, and reckoned that the particles of moisture, seeping into the beans' pores, would make them swell. Which happened just as he foresaw: he found the vessels in pieces, and the cords that held down the lids snapped.

IV. Nothing in nature demonstrates the force of the corpuscles better than the mechanics of animal movement. It must be admitted that the structure of the brain, the nerves, and the muscles — the arrangement and placement of all the parts relative to one another — in short, the whole construction and harmony of an animal's body — are things so surprising and admirable that they can only be the work of an infinitely wise and powerful artisan. And you would truly have to be more brutish than the beasts of the field to imagine that something so well contrived and so ravishing could be the product of chance, or of a blind cause without intelligence.

If the composition of animals is marvelous, their movements are nothing short of miracles. I don't mean so much the necessary movements — of the heart and the blood, which can't pause without danger to life — as the contingent ones: birds building their nests; the hen leading and raising her chicks; a dog coursing a hare, or pointing when he scents a partridge. These movements, which almost always surprise us, are produced by those little animal spirits flowing from the brain, now into one of a pair of opposing muscles, now into the other, swelling them, contracting them in turn, and causing all their varied motions — just as we see a cord swell and shorten when liquid soaks into it.

But that's not all. Go further, and consider those little spirits — so thin, so fine, so delicate — which, seeping into the pores of the muscles, not only move machines of prodigious size, like elephants, but also move other bodies of enormous weight attached to them, as when an animal carries or drags a great burden. The same goes for the mechanics of the human body, where the animal spirits sometimes let a porter lift an enormous load. Is anything, to look at it, weaker? Yet there's no weight or burden that can't be moved or carried by a machine so delicate — a machine whose whole force consists in a flow and exchange of very fine spirits, which no one would believe capable of such powerful actions if experience didn't force the point.

Let's not leave so admirable a machine without a closer look at the springs that give it movement and life. All the voluntary movements we see in man are produced by the muscles — organic, composite parts made of nerves, flesh, and fibers. These muscles have three parts: the head, the belly, and the tail. By the head and the tail they attach to the bones they move. When the belly fills with the animal spirits the brain sends flowing into it, seeping through the fibers, the muscle swells, consequently shortens, and moves the bone it's attached to. These little atoms filling the fibers — swelling the muscles whenever the brain pushes fresh ones in — are the efficient cause of those violent, prodigious movements we sometimes see in certain men. That is the whole secret and harmony of it.

But, someone will say, can so small a cause produce such great effects? I've already said that the finer these little wedges are, the more powerful their action. I've noted this holds all the more when they act in concert, per modum unius. I've pointed out that the force of seepage is surprising; I'll now add that it exceeds anything you can imagine. Tie a six-hundred-pound weight to a beam with a cord just strong enough to hold it, drawn taut, and wet the cord: you'll see those little moist particles, seeping in, either snap the cord or lift the six hundred pounds clear off the ground. Add to this a rather delightful experiment that M. Sturmius says he'll perform one day, if God grants him the means and the years: "jucunditatis multo futurum procul dubio spectaculum, digniusque in quod conficiendum, & sumptus, & operam aliquando, si volet Deus, impendamus, hoc modo praestituros esse nos confidimus." [Colleg. experiment. part 2, tentam. 11, p. 191.] Here it is. This philosopher, wanting to push further the experiment first made in England — lifting a weight with a pig's bladder filled with air — has proposed to lift a millstone off the ground by breath alone; and he has no doubt of succeeding, using bladders and a wooden machine such as is shown in the following figure. It's a great solid ring carried on four legs or columns strong enough to support the millstone's weight. Iron rings are leaded into the millstone, to take a hook fixed to the bottom of each bladder. At their upper ends the bladders are fastened firmly to the great wooden ring, through which pass reed tubes fitted with valves, so that once the bladders are blown up, the air can't escape. That's the whole apparatus. In the figure of the machine, only four bladders are shown — enough for a fairly large weight, though not for a millstone; it was drawn that way on purpose, to avoid clutter.

[The original here contains an engraving of Sturmius's bladder-machine.]

This experiment isn't mere curiosity. It's admirable for explaining how the animal spirits and the muscles produce those violent movements in animals and in men — moving, lifting, dragging, and carrying enormous weights. The bladders represent well enough the muscle's own sheath, which wraps the nerves, the cords, the fibers, the veins, and the arteries of which that organ is made. The man blowing represents the brain; and the breath is the image of the animal spirits that swell the muscles. One difference, though, must be noted above all: the bladder is single, while a muscle is perhaps made of more than four thousand little bladders — which multiplies the power enormously. And if it's true, as has been established, that the human body contains 405 muscles, should anyone be astonished at the force of a machine packed with so many springs?

But if a machine as delicate as the human body has such force in health — when the blood circulates regularly and the spirits are justly distributed to the muscles and nerves — what shall we say when this whole economy goes wrong, when some foreign matter, mixing with the spirits and the blood, heightens the fermentation, swells the nerves extraordinarily, and brings on those convulsive movements one can't watch without horror, which make one man stronger than twenty together? Mustn't we recognize that nature, with very small instruments, can produce effects that could only come from an extremely strong and powerful cause? M. Chastelain explains this very well in his excellent Treatise on convulsions and convulsive movements, pp. 103–104. If a few drops of water thrown on cords, he says, swell them and thereby make them capable of lifting burdens of incredible weight, as experience teaches — why be astonished that the spirits and the blood, swelling the motor fibers, make them capable of all the great exertions seen in convulsions and in the great convulsive movements?

We sometimes see sad evidence of the force and powerful impression of the animal spirits on children in their mothers' wombs. The child is then so intimately united to its mother that it receives every sensation that strikes her. Two lute strings tuned in unison — pluck one and the other moves and sounds — are not so closely linked as mother and child. This happens by the flow of the mother's animal spirits, which communicate themselves to the child's brain and act even on its body. From this come the secret likings and aversions people have for certain things, because their mothers craved them, or couldn't bear them, during pregnancy. From this come the marks of cherries, strawberries, or roses seen on children. From this come effects far more terrible: a pregnant mother watched a criminal broken on the wheel, and every blow dealt to that wretch struck, through the mother's animal spirits, on the tender, delicate body of her child, who came into the world broken at the same places where the criminal had been; and that subtle matter poured with such vehemence from the brain of the mother, shaken by the tragic spectacle, onto the delicate fibers of the child's brain, that they were deranged by it, and he was destitute of reason all his life. All Paris saw this, during the several years the man lived.

That is how far the force of these corpuscles extends — small and weak as they look, they produce effects that demand surprising power. It should now be easier to conceive where the peasant's symptoms and the rod's movement come from — above all if you keep in mind the quantity of particles we know to rise above springs and mines, and to spread along the route of a fleeing criminal, who, in his extreme agitation of body and mind, must perspire extraordinarily, leaving a trail of his continual perspiration behind him, the way a beast leaves its trail, a stag its scent, and a boar its tracks, along the road they have taken.

[End of Chapter Eleven]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER TWELVE

The particles of vapors, exhalations, and unnoticed perspiration that move the Divining Rod do not mix easily, or quickly, in the air.

Those who maintain that Jaques Aymar's pursuit of the Lyon murderers, guided only by his Divining Rod, can't be explained by the laws of nature never fail to ask how the villains' traces could have lingered so long on a road where people pass continually, and on a river where the air is in constant motion. This objection has been put to us a hundred times, and always with an air suggesting the objectors are extremely pleased with themselves for finding a difficulty they claim has no answer.
