Just read what appears in a letter printed in the Mercure Galant of January 1693, pp. 27 and 28. There you'll see the objection handled with care, and with relish. If the author doesn't come off as a philosopher there, he at least has the satisfaction of coming off as a rhetorician. "I have read with attention," he says, "the dissertations sent us from Lyon... the subtle matter flutters about most agreeably there; the corpuscles have an agility and a suppleness suited to everything one could wish... I would gladly be content with the stations assigned to them... The ones exhaled from a villain's body are made to remain whole months all along a road of a hundred leagues." This sparkle needs to be paid for with something solid; and to form accurate ideas about the state of these particles scattered in the air, you'd first have to define what mixture properly means.

For: 1. If by mixture you mean a jumble of heterogeneous bodies thrown together, with no body losing its own nature in the process, then I agree: in that sense the particles of vapors, exhalations, and unnoticed perspiration are mixed in the air. They certainly are — the way iron filings are mixed with gold filings when goldsmiths separate them with a magnet-stone; the way the liquids representing the four Elements in a vial are mixed when you shake the vial. But in a moment they disentangle, because in this kind of mixture none of these things has lost any of its nature.

2. If by mixture you mean a fusion of homogeneous bodies — bodies of the same nature — blended together like water with water, no longer distinguishable, with only a common action left: in that sense it is not true that the particles we credit with the rod's movement are mixed and merged with the air.

See how much daylight this single distinction lets into a matter that looked obscure and unintelligible viewed in the lump. In the first kind of mixture, the atoms are not so much mixed as combined. They aren't absorbed or lost, like a drop of water falling into the sea, never to be picked out again. They're like the 24 letters of the alphabet, combined in so many ways that their arrangement alone composes all the books filling the libraries — yet through every combination they keep their full power. The same goes for the particles of vapors, exhalations, and perspiration. Gusts of wind may shift them from place to place, but don't destroy them. They're a swarm of flies a high wind carries off — which doesn't stop them holding together, close and united.

I said the agitated air may shift them locally. Actually, I don't even grant that. Bodies act on one another only by impact; and in all likelihood the air, being coarser than the particles that move the rod, gets no grip on them. They're finer than the particles of odors, which nevertheless pass through the air easily and travel so far. Nothing stops us from crediting these particles with the same fineness we recognize in the magnetic spirits, whose course the air can neither deflect nor whose cloud it can break. For I myself saw, on the first Sunday of Lent 1693, the rod turn in Jaques Aymar's hands over a magnet-stone I presented to him — to verify with my own eyes an experiment I'd been told of in several places in Paris. That granted, it's easy to convince yourself that the disruption people imagine happening so easily to particles scattered in the air can in fact happen only with extreme difficulty. Just consider that a compass needle, once well magnetized, keeps the little whirlwind of magnetic matter it received from the magnet's touch for more than 50 years. I found in the provinces a compass whose needle had been magnetized more than 60 years before; though it had been exposed almost constantly to air and wind, it still points to the poles today as if it had just received the magnetic virtue.

But I'll go much further. Having just said these particles don't mix easily in the air, I now add that it's not possible for them to merge with the air absolutely, no matter what. Here are two reasons I hold unanswerable. These particles cannot mix and turn, as the saying goes, topsy-turvy with the air, 1. neither easily, 2. nor promptly.

I. Oil and water don't mix easily, because their constituent parts are differently shaped and of different weight, volume for volume. Water is a heap of particles that are long, supple, able to bend any way, with very polished surfaces; the parts of oil, by contrast, are branching, and lighter, volume for volume, than water's. By the laws of nature — which has set what's heavier below and what's lighter above, as everyone knows who has studied any hydrostatics — the particles of vapors, exhalations, and perspiration must float like a film on the liquid of the coarser air. And if some accident upsets this layering of particles of different shape and weight, they soon return and resume their natural station. Here is an experiment that will make this perfectly clear — the one the curious use to illustrate how the four elements composing the elementary world stacked themselves one above another by their different weights.

PHIAL REPRESENTING THE ELEMENTARY WORLD. The various writers on this popular curiosity don't all give the same recipe. I've chosen this one. Take black enamel coarsely crushed: it will sink to the bottom of the glass vessel and represent the Earth. For the Water, take calcined tartar, or gravelled ashes; leave them in the damp, and collect the solution that forms — above all the clearest part; mix in a little rock azure, to give it the color of seawater. For the Air, you need the finest brandy, tinged sky-blue with a little turnsole. Finally, to represent Fire, take linseed oil, or oil of turpentine, which is made like this: distill turpentine in a bain-marie; the water and the oil will rise together, both white and transparent; the oil, however, will float on top. Separate it with a glass funnel. Then tint it fire-colored with alkanet and saffron. (If you distill it in sand in a retort, the turpentine left at the bottom of the still will yield a thick red oil, which is an excellent balm.) All these substances differ so much in weight and shape that when you jumble them by violent shaking, you see, for a little while, a true chaos — a confusion so total you'd imagine all the little bodies of these liquids tumbled together without any rank. But scarcely have you stopped shaking when each returns to its natural place, and all the particles of one order reunite to form a layer absolutely separate from the others. The figure showing this phial of the four Elements will help make sense of what I've just said.

EXPERIMENT. Here's another very pleasing experiment, which also shows nicely how the lighter particles give way to the heavier and pass reciprocally through one another's pores to reach their natural place. Take two vials with very long necks, as the preceding figure shows. Fill one with wine, the other with water. Invert the mouth of the water-filled one onto the mouth of the wine-filled one. That done, you watch with pleasure as the wine filters up through the water, climbing little by little to take its place on top, while the water descends into the vial below.

[The original here contains an engraving showing the water-and-wine phials and the phial of the four Elements.]

EXPERIMENT. Difference of shape so effectively keeps mixed bodies from merging — however inseparable they look once blended, they still manage to disentangle — that if you put water into wine, you can extract the water again easily enough. You only need a cup made from a trunk of ivy: pour in the mixed wine and water, and almost at once the water passes, filtering through the pores of the cup, leaving behind the wine, which cannot pass — because the shape of its particles doesn't match the gaps in the ivy-wood.

Father Lana observes very judiciously that since there are rivers that keep their course, and even the sweetness of their waters, for several miles after entering the sea, it's far less surprising that effluvia keep their specific nature and their volume in the air — which offers them almost no resistance, given its mobility, and which the fine matter perspiring from bodies can easily penetrate. He has no doubt that this trail of volatile atoms is like a river constantly swelled by a successive, continual emission of particles, never interrupted by the body they flow from. He adds that these particles are certain to find in the air — above all in the same region — countless others of the same kind, shape, and weight, with which they join; this not only reinforces their swarm but extends and spreads it prodigiously far. So that, by his account, a man at Beaucaire would still be pushing particles of his perspiration as far as Lyon, the latest ones driving the earlier ones forward, as one sea-wave pushes another. I refer the curious to Father Lana's own book, where this doctrine is demonstrated at length, principally in Proposition IV [de motu transpirat. bk. 2, pp. 5–6, vol. 2].

This most curious physicist proves, by a very ingenious experiment, his Proposition XIII, that particles spread in the air do not ordinarily destroy one another. Here it is. EXPERIMENT. Though I didn't doubt, he says, that fragrant, magnetic, pestilential, and electric effluvia all keep their proper nature even when combined and mixed, I wanted to confirm it by an experiment, for the benefit of those who dabble in philosophizing without ever anchoring their reasoning in certain, evident facts. So I took a strong, vigorous magnet; near it I burned some very fragrant pastilles; I added an electric body; I burned incense besides; and over the whole I placed, at a suitable distance, a round copper plate pierced in the middle, so that all the various effluvia of these bodies could rise through the one hole. That done, I saw with pleasure that all these different particles, though mingled at the mouth of the hole, each produced effects true to its own kind: the pastilles gave off an agreeable scent; the magnet moved a compass needle; the amber held a straw suspended; and around the hole I saw a charming play of various colors shining quite distinctly. But really, do we need to go that far to be persuaded that particles of one kind don't hinder the action of another kind? Don't we see every day that however the air is shaken by the sound of a bell or the roar of a cannon, perfumes still make their scent felt, and the magnet still attracts iron? The particles that let us see objects can't block the effect of the particles that produce the sensation of sound in the ear, or of smells in the olfactory nerve. Nothing here but what is certain — obvious, even. [Lana, de motu transpirat. bk. 2, proposit. 13, p. 64, vol. 2.]

We've seen how particles of different shape and weight disentangle from one another. Here is an experiment showing that fumes separate themselves from water — so that those who report seeing vapors rise from the earth up through the waters of the sea are claiming nothing that can reasonably be doubted.

EXPERIMENT. Though tobacco is one of the most stinking herbs in the world, plenty of people use it constantly. The Abbé Nieremberg, a Spaniard, says in a book entitled Politici caelorum that it was the Devil who brought this abominable herb from the Indies into Spain, and into the rest of the world. So true is it that those who accuse the Devil of turning the Divining Rod aren't the only ones who bring him on stage: "Tabaci daemonis sollicitudine ex Indiis in Hispanias, aliasque mundi superioris oras invecta videtur." [Part 1, bk. 2, ch. 7.] It suits the Spanish character to give a small effect a grand cause — to cut a giant's coat for a dwarf. Be that as it may: here is something to amuse those who take tobacco as smoke, and what amuses them will serve us to demonstrate that bodies of different weight can't merge. Take a glass vessel a foot and a half tall, shaped roughly like a vinegar-cruet, and put water in it; fit a pipe to it as the figure shows, and when you draw the air by sucking at the neck, the smoke passes through the water and arrives at the mouth of whoever performs this little game.

[The original here contains an engraving of the water-pipe.]

Those who refine the thing say that instead of plain water you can use orange-flower water, or some other fragrant liquid, in which the tobacco smoke, depositing some of its abominable odor, borrows a pleasanter one. The ladies of Persia spend the better part of the day taking tobacco this way. They recline on great cushions of rich fabric and on carpets, and amuse themselves at the exercise. M. Tavernier describes very well how the Persians smoke, and after noting that the practice is as common among the women as the men, he adds: "They take it as smoke by a very particular contrivance. It is a glass bottle with a neck three fingers thick, into which fits a channel of wood or silver. They fill the neck of the bottle, where there is a plate on the outside, on which they put their tobacco, slightly moistened, with a coal on top. Under this plate there is a hole, into which a long cane fits; and in drawing breath, the tobacco smoke is forced down along the channel and enters the water — which they tint all sorts of colors, the bottle being half full of it. The smoke, once in the water, rises again to the surface; then, with the draw, it comes to the smoker's mouth; and thus the force of the tobacco is tempered by the water — for otherwise they could never keep taking it incessantly as they do." [Voyage de Perse vol. 1, bk. 5, ch. 17, p. 580, by M. Tavernier.] The Siamese also smoke tobacco this way, as we see from what M. de la Loubère wrote of it in the second volume of his history of Siam, p. 119.

Difference of motion alone can sometimes keep even homogeneous bodies from merging. I may fairly suppose that the particles of light are all of one nature and like configuration, and that the different effects they produce on the retina come only from white bodies setting these particles moving one way, and black bodies another — since vision happens only by the reflection or emission of the little luminous or colored bodies the object sends into the eyes. Now the unshakable cohesion of these little bodies gives us a very good picture of the steadiness of the particles standing in the air, despite winds and tempests. The luminous atoms, after all, take no damage from the movements of agitated air; whatever wind blows, the rays don't break or scatter in the space between the object and the eyes. If that happened, we would see objects shake — which doesn't happen, since we see objects as fixed in a tempest as in a calm.

And that's still not all. I say that the different impulse objects stamp on these little luminous bodies traveling to the eye is what keeps them from merging. If they merged with one another, an object would always look a single color, even when it had several. We can't easily examine what happens in the natural eye of a living man; but an artificial eye can illustrate the point. In place of the artificial eye, whose construction M. Rohault teaches, I'll give here the Magic Lantern, which is admirable for demonstrating that these luminous particles — purely because each is pushed in a way particular to each color of the object — do not mix or merge, even though the rays and swarms of these atoms reunite, cut, cross, and even pass through one another at the focus of the lenses in the lantern's tube. They all preserve their proper impulse so well that they go and paint, on the white wall, the phantom of the object with all its colors.

THE MAGIC LANTERN. The Magic Lantern is an optical machine, called magic no doubt because of its prodigious effects, and the specters and frightful monsters it displays, which people who don't know its secret attribute to magic. M. Sturmius calls it megalographic, because it shows in large the very small figures put into it, making, as the saying goes, an elephant of a fly. The invention — which some would trace back to Solomon — is due to Roger Bacon, an English monk. What's most certain is that the machine has been making noise for some time, and that Swenterus was the first to teach its construction, in a book published under the title deliciae mathematicae [part 6, proposit. 41]. The body of the lantern is tinplate, square, eight and a half inches deep and a foot and a half high. At the back is a concave metal mirror, 4 inches across and 5 lines deep, with a lamp whose cotton wick must be very thick. You burn olive oil in it, or spirit of wine. The mirror and the lamp can slide forward or back on a runner at the bottom of the lantern. At the front is a round opening of three and a half inches where, when you want to make the lantern play, you fit a tinplate tube containing two lenses a little over three inches across, ground to make the rays converge and to magnify objects greatly. Between the front of the lantern and the lens tube there is a slot for passing the frames that carry the little figures you want to show enlarged. They are painted in transparent colors on glass, or on pieces of talc about three inches across. At the top of the lantern are vents to let the smoke out, so it doesn't dim the light, which must be very bright for a fine effect. Such is the one I own; and I give its figure here, shown open, so the inside can be examined.

[The original here contains an engraving of the magic lantern, opened.]

To use the Magic Lantern, you light the lamp and darken the room where the show is to be given; opposite the lantern, at 18 or 20 feet, you stretch a white sheet on the wall, on which the phantoms of the objects appear, painted in beautiful colors and of gigantic, monstrous size. It's not for lack of effort on M. Van Dale's part that people don't believe the Witch of Endor showed the prophet Samuel to King Saul by this method. I'll say something later about this fantasy, which that author published in his Treatise on Oracles. I'm well aware the trick can be pushed far, and abused at the expense of ignorant, credulous people — above all when the operation is run by a clever man. A very able mathematician used this art to show the Emperor Rudolph II all the rulers of the Roman Empire from Julius Caesar to Maurice; and it was done so vividly and naturally that everyone present believed it could only have been managed with the help of magic and necromancy.

I'm now going to demonstrate, by experiments cheaper to perform than the magic lantern's, that the rays of the sun, or of other luminous bodies, do not mix.

EXPERIMENTS. 1. Take three luminous bodies — three lit candles made of three different-colored waxes, so that the variety of flame colors makes the experiment the finer. Set the three candles in a single line, some distance apart. Opposite them place a large square of wood or pasteboard with a small hole in the middle, arranged so there's a white wall behind it. The rays of the three flames will pass in straight lines through the hole, where they will reunite, cut, and cross at the angles they make there — and will finally go paint themselves, in their different colors, on the white wall. Even if the candles aren't different colors, as long as they differ in size you can run the same experiment and draw the same conclusions. Just look at the figure, which will finish explaining whatever I may not have made clear enough.

[The original here contains an engraving of the three-candle experiment.]

Note that this experiment only works in a darkened room — as does the next one, which has even taken its name from it: Camera obscura.

THE DARK CHAMBER. 2. If this experiment is done in a room overlooking a fine garden, or a public square full of people, it has something ravishing about it, something that borders on enchantment. You make a hole in a wall with a view of a garden or a market; in the hole you set a glass lens — one lens from an old man's spectacles will do; then you darken the room; after that, if you hold a large white card near the hole where the lens sits, you see all the objects in the square come paint and arrange themselves on the card; and these little phantoms imitate every movement of the originals. You see the birds fly past, the people come and go, the flowers with all the enamel of their colors; and it's all rendered so cleanly that, if you had time to trace what appears on the card, you'd have copies from nature drawn by Nature herself. This experiment works in full daylight. But don't surrender so completely to the pleasure of the show that you forget the point: all these luminous rays, so distinct on the card, have reunited, cut, crossed, and passed through one another at the focus of the lens — without losing their colors or the impulse the objects stamped on them. Our experiments, after all, aim to instruct while they entertain.

BURNING MIRROR MADE WITH A PIECE OF ICE. 3. Sacred history tells how Nehemiah turned a muddy water into fire [2 Macc. 1:21]. And in the fable, Prometheus stole fire from heaven and brought it to earth. But here is an experiment that does something seemingly just as prodigious: drawing from the heart of ice a fire that burns — that even ignites gunpowder. M. Mariotte, of the Royal Academy of Sciences, made a trial of it that succeeded very well. He boiled clean water over the fire for about half an hour, to drive out the airy matter, so the ice would be the more transparent. He exposed the water to very cold air. It froze, and the ice had no bubbles. He put the ice in a concave spherical vessel; bringing the vessel near the fire, he melted the ice little by little until it had taken a convex spherical shape. He did the same on the other side — leaving the piece of ice fairly uniformly convex, and consequently a burning mirror of ice. He took the little mirror by its two edges with a glove, so the heat of his hand wouldn't melt it too soon; he held it to the sun; and in very little time he set fire to gunpowder placed at the focus of this marvelous mirror. That's a winter experiment; here's one for summer. 4. Expose to the sun, when it's very hot — in summer, from 9 in the morning to 3 in the afternoon — a perfectly round glass vial full of water, and it will ignite fine powder placed at the focus of this burning mirror made of water. These last two experiments show very clearly that the sun's rays lose nothing of their nature in penetrating and passing through the pores of water and of ice — and that the corpuscles do not mix easily with the air, nor even with other bodies.

Now let's show that if this mixing happens at all, it can only happen after a long time.

II. Those who object that the particles standing in the air — the way oil floats on water — are easily displaced have never really understood what they're saying. Just make them explain it clearly, and they'll see their error. By the laws of hydrostatics, there's no understanding how these little bodies could ever merge, the way one drop of water merges with another, until it's been proved that they weigh the same, volume for volume, as the particles of the air.

OBJECTION. But, people say, the hare's trail fades so easily from the ground it crossed; why not judge the same of the traces of thieves and fleeing murderers?

ANSWER. 1. The hare's trail doesn't dissipate as easily as people imagine. It's actually surprising that an animal that runs so lightly, and presses the grass and earth so briefly with its foot, leaves there an emanation that good hunting dogs can sometimes scent two days later: "Siquidem," says Father Lana, "canes illi etiam elapsa die integra a transitu ferae, ab effluviis illis afficiuntur, quae terrae, vel gramini adhaesere tam brevi tempore, quam erat illud, quo illa fera pertransibat." [De motu transpirat. bk. 2, ch. 2, Proposit. 3, p. 55.] 2. Even when the dogs can no longer make out the animal, it doesn't follow that the particles the hare perspired are completely gone. True, these effluvia are less warm, less lively, and less numerous, because the finer parts have flown off into the upper air; and the dog, who acts mechanically, is less touched and less moved by them. But a man who adds reflection to his sensations — who has set himself to follow a man by his trail — husbands whatever his senses reveal, steers by it, and does by reason, without losing heart, what the dog gives up the moment the effluvia stop pressing on him so strongly. 3. From the hare's trail nothing follows about the effluvia coming off a fleeing murderer's body. There's a great deal of difference between the size of a hare and a man's body. And beyond that disproportion, it's certain that of all animals, man's blood carries the most salts, and that beasts have few animal spirits, even in proportion to their bulk, as M. Régis has remarked very well in his Physics [bk. 7, part 2, ch. 11, p. 81]. To which I'll add that of all men, the one who perspires most — in whom the greatest wasting of animal spirits occurs — is a fleeing villain, whose brain, furiously shaken by the image of his crime and of the punishment he's straining to escape, sends a flood of animal spirits into all the muscles nature designed for the movements his flight requires. 4. And M. Boyle, who studied nature with such care and labor, has no doubt that if a wounded animal leaves in the air a matter it stays charged with for several hours, even running at speed — enough for the hounds to find it wherever it has gone — then neither should we doubt that other effluvia, though perceptible to few people, can stay in the air not just a year, but even two. [Cosmic. circa rerum qualit. p. 2.]

Say what you like, some people will believe none of it, and will dismiss as vision and fantasy what M. Boyle advanced only after the most serious examination. These skeptics must be forced to yield to the truth; and that's what I'll do with the following facts, which show that particles spread in the air preserve themselves a very long time. 1. Forestus relates that pestilential atoms preserved themselves a long while in a spider's web. [bk. 6, observat. 22.] 2. Alexander Benedictus writes that a mattress that had unhappily retained a vapor of plague, on being shaken rather lightly, infected and killed those present. 3. Sennert reports that, being in 1542 at Breslau — a city of Bohemia and capital of Silesia — at the time the plague killed six thousand people there in about six months, it happened that fourteen years later, a folded linen in which the plague had stayed sealed, carried from that city to another, set off there a plague so furious it spread even into the neighboring villages, where many people died. [bk. 4, de febrib. ch. 3.] 4. Trincavella recounts a terrible example of how stubbornly contagious particles hold out against the movements of the air. He says a plague that killed ten thousand people originated in ropes that had formerly been used to lower the bodies of plague victims into their graves. [bk. 3, cons. 17.] These examples led M. Boyle to conjecture that 20 days are too few to clear plague from the air, though the physicians customarily prescribe no more. That may sometimes be enough to purify things left in the open air; but he cites an example proving it isn't always. 5. He tells us, after Diemerbroeck, that this learned man, having touched with his foot a bit of straw in his garden — on which, more than eight months before, a servant sick with plague had rested some hours — felt at once the plague-vapors fasten on his foot and raise there a very painful pustule, which turned into a pestilential carbuncle; even though, he adds, the straw had spent those 8 months exposed to air, wind, rain, snow, and frost: "Mirum tamen est hoc contagium tantopere in praedicto stramine potuisse subsistere, utpote quod tota hyeme, ventis & pluviis, nivibus & frigori expositum fuisset." [bk. 4, de peste; reported by M. Boyle at the end of his Treatise de mira subtilit. effluv. p. 20.] 6. And M. Boyle adds that although glove-perfumers use very little fragrant matter, he kept a pair of Spanish gloves for 29 years — gloves he even used often — which perfumed everything he touched, and which at the end of that time still gave off a scent so lively that it could doubtless have lasted several years more. So true is it that once fine matter has lodged in a place, it does not detach easily.

Everything I've said about these particles swimming in the liquid of the air concerns only the thieves and fleeing murderers; no one will have trouble picturing that there are always vapors over the springs and exhalations over the mines, since these give them off incessantly. So I trust it will now be less difficult to understand how Jaques Aymar can follow a murderer or a thief long after his flight.

DIFFICULTY. How could the particles of the Lyon murderers remain on the river and on the sea, where nothing appears fit to hold them in place?

ANSWER. Don't imagine these particles swimming in the air need some anchor to cling to, lest the wind carry them off. It's by the inviolable laws of nature that they stand in the lower region of the air. They can neither rise nor sink so long as they are neither lighter nor heavier, volume for volume, than the air they roll in. They float and balance there, as the air does on the water, with nothing needed to hold them in their region: the quality of their particular nature holds them there, and they would have to change that quality before they could change their dwelling.

I end this chapter here, lest anyone complain I want to leave nothing for the minds of my readers to do. But since a learned man put this last difficulty to me on behalf of a person most illustrious for his fine mind, his virtue, and his rank, I judged it might well trip up others too, and that I ought therefore to clear it away.

[End of Chapter Twelve]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER THIRTEEN

Why the Divining Rod does not turn in everyone's hands. What use is the rod, if the power comes from the person holding it?

Those who can't believe the rod's movement is natural — who bring the demon onto the stage to play his part — imagine they've won their case right here. This is where they inflate their style and parade in triumph. It would be hard to stand up to their declamations, and not be swept along by the speed of their gestures, if you didn't know how to tell a figure of rhetoric from an argument.

Father Malebranche, who is far more measured, reasons and expresses himself in these terms: "It seems clear to me," he says, "that whoever holds the rod, and however it's held — even if it were held with tongs — it ought to bend equally; just as the magnet acts equally on iron, whoever holds it and brings it near. And if it's claimed that temperament contributes to the rod's action (for the defenders of these follies believe they have the right to say whatever they please), let them explain what they mean by the word temperament; let them make an intelligible objection, and someone will try to answer them." [Letter of Father Malebranche inserted in the Mercure Galant of January 1693, pp. 59 and 60.]

I. It's very easy to give Father Malebranche what he asks, and to explain ourselves clearly.

1. True, the magnet acts equally on iron whoever holds it — because the magnet is the total cause of that action. Not so with the rod's movement. It is produced partly by the particles rising from the springs and mines, and partly by the disposition of the person holding it. No doubt about it: if the vapors rising from the earth alone moved the rod, it would have to turn equally in anybody's hands. There's something intelligible.

2. It's equally intelligible that these earth-vapors will act on certain people, who will be very sensitive to them, while others won't be moved by them at all — because the weave of their fibers leaves no pores matched to the size and shape of the volatile atoms rising from springs, mines, and even the bodies of thieves and murderers. It's just like what we discovered after Gilbert the Englishman taught us there's an atmosphere of magnetic matter wrapping the earth, flowing north to south and south to north: this matter does not soak into every kind of body equally. Forge two rods, one of iron and one of silver, and let them cool aligned north and south, and you'll find the subtle matter has done nothing at all to the silver rod — while it has so charged the iron one that, hung on a pivot or a thread, it will always swing back to the position it cooled in.

3. There are dispositions in certain animals that make them sensitive to the impact of certain little bodies that don't touch or move other animals at all. Isn't this why the hare's particles, lingering on its trail, strike a hunting dog so forcefully and excite him, while doing nothing of the kind to a dog of another breed? There are dogs that hunt only the wolf; particular dogs for the boar, for the stag, for foxes, for small game. Likewise there are people whom the smell of musk stupefies, and others who, far from being bothered by it, sniff it with pleasure. Why does Father Malebranche insist that whatever produces a certain effect in one person's hands must do exactly the same in anyone's? And speaking of the magnet — this celebrated philosopher would be quite astonished to be shown that a magnet-stone does not do the same thing equally in everyone's hands. If that's so, what becomes of that rich comparison of magnet and rod, with which he believed he so soundly crushed the defenders of these follies? Yet it's a fact of experience that when a magnet is held in very warm hands, it has more difficulty carrying the weight it's customarily loaded with — hence those oddities in experiments that sometimes surprise and vex, and would almost persuade you the magnet has changed, since less power is found in it; though a little later, when the hands are more temperate, it performs as usual. This kind of syncope, or fainting fit, that befalls the magnet in overly warm hands comes from the dissipation of its magnetic spirits, deranged and scattered by the finest particles of the hands' unnoticed perspiration. For note that this discharge of perspired matter is delivered, says M. Boyle, with as much violence as the small shot that flies from a gun and pierces a bird.

In the same way, the cold, damp particles of the evening air, falling as the sun withdraws, register sharply on delicate and elderly people. Some can't bear the impact of these little bodies; it feels to them like little hammers striking their head and body. The young, and people of strong constitution, scarcely notice them. For, says M. Digby, the bubbling of their blood and the warmth of their complexion drive out of them an abundance of spirits which, being stronger than the particles of the evening damp, repel them and stop them acting with such effect on the young as they do on those whom age has chilled — who are not shielded by the emanation of their own spirits, because, though abundant, it is not delivered with the same strength and vigor. For the same reason there are many people who never catch contagious diseases, protected from them by a strong, abundant perspiration.

We've already covered a lot of ground, it seems to me; and in showing how some people are sensitive to the impressions of the particles spread in the air, while others are of such a fiber-weave that they are not touched or penetrated by them at all, I've at the same time given the reason why the Divining Rod doesn't turn equally in everyone's hands. It hardly needs spelling out. Those whose perspiration is gross, stiff, and abundant cannot see the rod turn in their hands, because their perspired particles — driven, in M. Boyle's phrase, with as much violence as the small shot that flies from a gun and pierces a bird — break and scatter the cloud, or column, of vapors, exhalations, and fumes rising from the springs, the mines, and the fleeing criminals. And if those vapors had already penetrated the rod, they would be repelled and driven out of it by the particles perspiring from the hands, which outmatch them in quantity and in motion.

Let me explain this effect, in my turn, by another quite similar one that strongly supports my system. If an iron rod hung by its middle from a thread touches, with its point, the pole of a good magnet, then even if it had already been magnetized in another direction, it loses its first impression and takes a new, entirely contrary one. Why? Because the great quantity of magnetic matter rushing from the stone forces the small quantity passing through the poles of the iron rod to retrace its path and flow in the direction opposite to its former one — helped by the suppleness of the iron's parts, which bend easily enough not to resist the new direction of the magnetic matter. [Rohault, Physiq. part 3, ch. 8, num. 56, p. 211.] The strong, abundant perspiration of the hand does the same thing to the hazel rod: it drives out the particles of the fumes that had worked their way between the fibers and into the pores of the wood.

M. de Saint Romain, physician, understood this well. He says, in effect, that "the difficulty concerns the hand that holds the rod, for not every hand is good for it... As for the hand, it is certain that hands being as different as people, the spirits that flow from them are as different as the hands. So no one should be astonished if there are spirits that hold the rod back and block this movement, and that flow from the hands of one man and not from the hands of another." [Science naturelle part 1, ch. 8, p. 43.]

What I've said here about the speed of these perspired particles, breaking the cloud of fumes the rod would turn on, is no fantasy. M. Boyle, after comparing these particles to grains of lead-shot fired from a gun, adds that you should picture them like the vaporous blast that shoots from an Aeolipile at full heat, driven with such force that a great log of even the most solid wood is set ablaze and consumed in no time. Apparently Father Kircher perspired in just this harsh, abundant way — he who declares that he tried the rod several times over gold and silver, and always found he had no talent for the operation: "Certe ego famosus hujus rei supra metallica corpora auri, & argenti, experimentum sumens, semper spe mea frustratus sum." [Mund. subterran. bk. 10, sect. 2, ch. 7, p. 200.] This learned man had seen people who did have the natural gift. Father Déchales the Jesuit assures us he watched the experiment performed by a gentleman who, in his presence, found silver hidden on purpose, and who with the same rod found springs of water without fail: "semel enim pecuniae in terram absconditae de industria, quae ab aliquo Nobili me praesente ramo coryli inventae sunt." [Mund. Mathematic. tract. 15, de fontib. proposit. 16, p. 100.]

All this is intelligible enough, and gives the reason why the rod doesn't turn equally in everyone's hands — which is exactly what Father Malebranche wanted explained intelligibly.

II. But it's not so easy to say precisely what temperament a person must have to be disposed like Jaques Aymar. One may well know that such-and-such a thing does not produce a certain effect; pinning down its proximate, immediate cause is very hard. In so obscure a matter, finding the intermediate causes is already a great deal. Fracastorius says outright that one shouldn't venture to demonstrate the very proximate causes and the proper, particular affinities of certain marvelous effects with their causes: "Particulares autem & proprias analogias, non prudentis est inquirere." [De contag. bk. 1, ch. 8.] I wouldn't attempt so laborious an explanation if we didn't live in a time when everyone is bent on perfecting natural science at any price — and if I didn't know that people look kindly on those who make some effort to unfold causes never known before.

I've noticed that everyone who has the faculty of using the Divining Rod is a person of fairly good constitution, neither fat nor thin, with soft skin and fairly firm flesh. Their blood is sound; its fermentation proceeds calmly; it reaches the parts by a just distribution and a circulation always even and entirely natural. Now, since the role of the volatile sulphurs is to envelop the acrid and acid salts of the blood — which would otherwise sour it, set it fermenting, and make it circulate too violently — I conclude that the blood of such people must contain more volatile sulphurs than acrid and acid salts.

That's not all. If sound blood is the proximate, immediate cause of a regular pulse, the same blood must be the cause of the derangement seen in the pulse during the symptoms that shake Jaques Aymar when his rod dips over something. His pulse is known to climb then as in a high fever; and I hear every day, from a hundred quarters, that people sensitive to the impact of the particles forming the vapors and the metallic exhalations are suddenly seized with a kind of fever the moment they enter places where there are metals. These spring-vapors, metal-exhalations, and perspiration-fumes must therefore be kinds of acrid, acid salts which, mixing with the blood through respiration, make it ferment excessively and circulate violently — whence Jaques Aymar falls into those faintings and that heart-sickness he suffers from even long afterward. This effervescence, breaking out so suddenly in the blood, doesn't just mimic the symptoms of fever; it is a true fever. Willis says fever is nothing but an excessive fermentation and effervescence arising in the blood and the humors: "Febris est fermentatio seu effervescentia immodica sanguini & humoribus inducta." [De febrib. ch. 1, p. 61, vol. 1.] So when Jaques Aymar follows a thief or a murderer, his pulse climbs, he feels a fire in his entrails, he suffers headaches — in a word, he goes through everything that happens in a fit of fever; and afterward he goes through the exhaustion, the lassitude, and all that follows a very violent fit. This accidental fever lasts nearly the whole rest of the day — that is, until the acrid particles that slipped into his blood by unnoticed respiration are driven out by the discharge that unnoticed perspiration produces. That happens easily, because the source of this ferment is external, in the air: it's enough to leave that atmosphere and breathe purer air for the blood to unload this acid matter through the same pores that let it into the veins and arteries.

It's this sudden change, breaking out so violently inside him, that warns him he's within the atmosphere of the vapors, the exhalations, and the fumes. When the inner derangement is great and very perceptible, it's guide enough for Jaques Aymar, and then he doesn't need the rod — which serves him only when he's moved inwardly in a weak, ambiguous way. This headlong circulation of the blood is so violent in some people that once they're in this agitation, which lasts a good while, they can't go on to a second experiment: a fresh cloud of particles, adding nothing to the fermentation of their blood, wouldn't register on them. From this you can see why the Divining Rod sometimes fails in the hands of the very person who has often used it with success. Something can go wrong in his constitution; his blood may ferment more violently — whether because acrid, acid salts have crept in, through food or through the air he breathes; or perhaps because the volatile sulphurs that used to dominate, enveloping those salts and suppressing their action, have been dissipated by overwork, by sleepless nights, by study, or otherwise — so that the acid salts, set loose, sour the blood and send it racing. The result is that a gross, acrid, stiff matter then perspires through the pores, passes from the hands into the gaps of the hazel, forces the vapors and exhalations of the waters and metals to retrace their path, and stops the rod from moving. That's why the rod can stand motionless over water and metal in the hands of a man for whom it was turning five or six hours earlier. That, again, is why there are people in whose hands the rod can never turn.

So Jaques Aymar is of a good temperament. He perspires and breathes abundantly. The weave of his body's fibers must have left pores perfectly fitted for the entry of the foreign particles that mix with his blood when, sound as it is, it starts to ferment and flare. During this violent fermentation, some of the metallic vapors (or vapors of another kind) that he breathed in perspire out through his hands onto the rod; so that the rod, penetrated by this fluid matter, becomes disposed to let itself be drawn — to drink in more easily — the particles hanging in the air over the waters, over the metals, and over the criminals' traces. Just as a freshly snuffed candle draws, by the little particles of fire lingering at the wick, the flame of a lit candle brought near it.

Can anyone deny this analogy, this affinity, between certain particles and certain pores? Isn't it exactly this that makes certain diseases in the air sometimes attack only little children, fall only on certain livestock, and strike even only certain parts of the body? That's why Fracastorius says no agent can deploy its power indiscriminately on every kind of subject — it can only make itself felt on what keeps some affinity with it: "Non omnia agunt in omnia, sed certa in certa solum quae analoga dicuntur." [Bk. 1, de contag. ch. 8.] Aren't there, says M. Boyle, men of a certain particular temperament who, once struck and penetrated by the particles of the plague, contract from it an extreme readiness to be soaked by them again? So true is it that there is a temperament that makes a person far more susceptible to the impressions of the little bodies spread in the air: "Tunc tamen cum incidunt in homines peculiaris alicujus temperamenti, qui peste quondam correpti singularem quandam dispositionem nacti sint, quae pestiferorum effluviorum operationibus facit obnoxios." [Suspicion. cosmic. circa rerum qualit. p. 2.]

But why not allow that some people have organs of touch finer than the ordinary — since there are men who see farther, and hear better, than others? It must certainly be admitted that this is entirely possible, and that plenty of examples would turn up if they were sought with care. Don't hunting dogs have a much finer nose than most other dogs? Suetonius testifies that Tiberius saw at night like a cat. Aulus Gellius speaks of peoples who saw better by night than by day [bk. 9, ch. 4]. Pliny reports, after Varro and Cicero, that a certain Strabo had sight so prodigiously strong that, during the Carthaginian war, from a cape of Sicily now called il Capo Boeo, he saw and counted the ships of the Carthaginian fleet at a cape of Africa 135 miles away — 160 miles by M. Baudrand's reckoning, that is, 54 French leagues. [Pliny bk. 7, ch. 21.] It's the same with the other senses: certain people have them finer and more delicate than the rest of mankind. Levinus Lemnius tells of a peasant of Antwerp who was stupefied by the odors he breathed in a druggist's shop — to the point of fainting outright and losing all consciousness. [De occult. natur. miraculis bk. 2, ch. 9, p. 167.] And we've already seen what M. Boyle relates of a physician friend of his who, seized with fever, developed hearing so delicate that he heard with perfect clarity what was said quite far from him, though those present heard nothing.

So there are people with sight, hearing, and smell of extraordinary keenness; it even seems that fever helps sharpen the senses and fit them for exact sensation. Why, then, shouldn't there also be people whose sense of touch is far finer and more exact than the ordinary? Why may Jaques Aymar not have, for the sensations of the organ of touch, what so many others have for the sensations objects produce on the retina, sound on the eardrum, and odors on the olfactory nerves? There's nothing incredible in any of that.

Why shouldn't the vapors and exhalations spread in the air do to the bodies of certain people what they do to the various materials of which hygrometers, barometers, and thermometers are made — and to so many other hard, dense substances that the humid air visibly penetrates and swells? Why not allow that there are bodies of extraordinary constitution, when every age has seen men with something singular beyond the rest? Saint Augustine says: "I myself have seen a man who sweated whenever he wished: everyone knows there are people who weep when they wish, and as much as they wish. But here," adds Saint Augustine, "is something far more incredible, which happened lately, and which most of our brethren witnessed. There was a priest of the Church of Calama named Restitutus who, whenever he wished, could so withdraw his mind — when someone imitated before him the voice of a person lamenting — that he lay stretched on the ground like a dead man; and not only did he feel nothing when pinched or pricked, but not even when burned. And to show that his body lay motionless only because it was emptied of all feeling — he had no breathing left at all, any more than a dead man. He said, though, that when people spoke very loud, he heard what seemed like voices coming from far away." So true is it that some people train their bodies to extraordinary things. [De civitat. Dei bk. 14, ch. 24.]

Nothing, perhaps, is more remarkable than the caprice of those who take it on themselves to put the worst construction on the gifts of nature that experience has uncovered in the peasant of Dauphiné. They take up arms, they shout; and in accusing him of sorcery they fall into movements more convulsive than the ones he suffers at a murder scene. It's truly a marvel to see the unease and the vexation so well displayed on this subject in the Letter on the Diviner of Lyon, inserted in the Mercure Galant of January 1693, p. 275, etc. They take their stand, they say, out of wise precaution, on the side of suspicion. They prefer to accuse Aymar of magic rather than believe him an honest man. They even claim religion is at stake. They can't imagine how he could have followed and traced a fleeing villain without devilry. The more they think about it, the more they confirm themselves in their view that this is not natural. They've even declared they will never come back from it. So that's settled. And I believe they'll keep their word, whatever clarification anyone offers them; in the frame of mind they advertise, talking reason to them would be perfectly useless. Aymar's star, as the saying goes, must be very unlucky, since a crime is made of a discernment he shares with most of the Negroes — who have never been accused of an understanding with the devil over it. After all, it's nothing new that certain men should be built for sensations livelier than the ordinary. Anyone can read, in the history of the Antilles, that the Negroes have so fine a sense of smell that they can tell the tracks of a Negro, a Spaniard, or a Frenchman merely by smelling the ground they walked on. And M. de la Mothe le Vayer says the guides used for crossing the seas of sand and the deserts of Africa find the roads by sniffing the ground. [Physique du Prince ch. 26, p. 111.]

III. It remains to say what use the rod is, if the power comes entirely from the man. Certainly, if the impression of the vapors were always equally strong and perceptible, you could dispense with the rod and judge by the sense of touch alone whether you're tracking the murderer or straying off the route. I know a man who finds buried silver without any rod. The metallic effluvia alone charge him so strongly that he feels his pulse climb and his heart weaken, to the point of vomiting with terrible violence. But when the impression is weak, and you feel only faintly moved, you turn to the rod, which is steered by these invisible particles, and which makes felt, by its movement, what the sense of touch alone wouldn't reveal. Just as a microscope, by assisting nature, shows what the human eye had never seen. Just as a telescope finds in the sky stars that would never have been seen without that marvelous instrument. The rod, then, is an aid — a way of making certain what sensation indicates only confusedly.

It's like a man in open country who doesn't know which quarter the wind is from. By a confused sensation he guesses it's coming from ahead of him; but the wind is weak, so he has only a slight presumption. He fastens a little piece of paper to the end of his cane like a weathervane, holds it to the wind, and knows for certain where it's from. EXPERIMENT. You can verify it another way too. Hold your middle finger in your mouth a moment; then hold it up to the air. You'll feel, along the finger — whose warmth has opened the pores — a little column of cold air striking it on the side the wind comes from. So it is with the rod. When you have only a faint, confused feeling of the presence of the vapors and exhalations, the rod is an aid for making sure of it, and for steering yourself.

But this is explained so well in the letter written from Lyon to M. l'Abbé Bignon that the reader will surely enjoy seeing how the matter was reasoned out in the very place where it made such a stir: "The rod used is ordinarily forked, held by the two ends; but a single one will serve, held in the hands slightly bent in an arc, so that it turns more readily. Even unbent, or held in one hand only, it would still turn, but less perceptibly... In cases where the movements are lively — assassinations, for example — one can dispense with the rod for following the murderers; the inner movement and agitation are warning enough. But in cases where this inner agitation is not perceptible enough, as when one wants to find silver, the rod is necessary, to make manifest what is not sufficiently known; and properly speaking, it is the rod that strikes the hour. Note further that some people can dispense with it sooner than others — namely, those on whom the impression of the foreign spirits is stronger. Those on whom it is weaker don't feel enough movement and agitation to be decided inwardly, and they need this external sign to decide them."

So the Divining Rod is only the organ, the vehicle, and the instrument by which one verifies the presence of the particles rising from the springs, from the mines, and from the footsteps of fleeing criminals. It has no power in itself. All it contributes is this: it is capable of letting itself be penetrated and soaked by the matter the hands give off, and of drawing to itself, by analogy and affinity, the particles of the vapors and exhalations that travel toward it — as the flame of a lit candle does at the smoking wick of one newly snuffed. It does for touch what the speaking-trumpet does for the ear: receiving at one end the rays of the words spoken into it, to carry them out the other to the ear of the person it's aimed at.

One difficulty remains to be answered.

DIFFICULTY. Does Jaques Aymar have different kinds of pores for breathing in the different sorts of particles that come from the watercourses, the mines, the hidden treasures, and the bodies of the villains he hunts?

ANSWER. Joannes Matthaeus raised this same difficulty about the hazel rod, and I answered it on page 201; the same answer serves for those who've applied it to the man with the rod. I showed in Chapter VIII that the human body is riddled through with pores, gaps, and little empty spaces, through which it perspires and breathes imperceptibly; and on page 215 I showed these pores aren't all of one kind: some are visible to the eye, others appear only under the microscope, and there are doubtless smaller ones still that the eye, even armed with the microscope, could never make out. That should be enough. Nevertheless, I'll add: even if the skin looked as uniform as water seems at its surface, no one could deny it might be filled with countless pores of different size and shape — since water itself is pierced with infinite pores of very different configuration and size. I'll prove it by one of the finest experiments that can be made.

EXPERIMENT. The salts, as is known, all have different shapes. Common salt always appears in cubes with square faces. Niter-salt forms six-sided columns. Sal ammoniac is hexagonal. Alum is octagonal. Salt of urine is pentagonal. Snow is six-pointed. Yet if you put these salts, and several other quite different ones, into common water one after another, they will all dissolve up to a certain quantity — that is, until all the water's pores of pentagonal shape, say, are filled with the particles of the salts of urine. This experiment, which is perfectly beautiful, shows that a body as homogeneous as water is nevertheless pierced by a great number of pores of very different shapes. Here is how M. de Monconys performed it in February 1647, as reported in his voyage to Egypt, p. 154. 1. Two ounces of distilled water were taken in a glass vial; into this water was put, little by little and at several sittings, 9 deniers of decrepitated salt, which was all the water could dissolve. (Note that the vial had been weighed beforehand.) The water was filtered, and then, transferred to another vial that had also been weighed, came to 2 ounces, 8 deniers, and 7 grains of salt-charged water. 2. Into this water saltpeter was added, up to sixteen deniers, in stages, as each portion was seen to finish dissolving. As more was about to be added, a deposit was noticed forming of 4 deniers that had already dissolved. The water was filtered as before, and came to two ounces, 20 deniers, and six grains of liquid. 3. Into this water sal ammoniac was put, up to 12 deniers, which was all that would dissolve. The first three deniers dissolved very quickly; then the whole was filtered, and came to 3 ounces, 5 deniers, and 11 grains of liquid. 4. Next, 3 deniers of alum were added, which dissolved but would not incorporate with the water, settling as mud at the bottom of the vessel, at the same weight as when put in. And while its dissolution was awaited, vegetations were observed forming and continually destroying themselves in it — looking like little mushrooms, or headed cabbages. When it was clear the water could not take up the alum, it was filtered, and only three ounces, 4 deniers, and nine grains of liquid were found, the rest having stayed behind with the alum mud. 5. Into the same water was put a piece of vitriol of Cyprus weighing three deniers, of which the water dissolved no more than it needed to tinge itself green; and onto the piece of vitriol there precipitated 20 grains of white salt of various shapes, which the water deposited; it was then filtered, and came to three ounces, two deniers, and three grains of liquid. 6. Finally, sugar candy was set to dissolve, in stages, in this liquid. The water went on dissolving the sugar for two months, after which the operation was stopped, the liquid having grown too thick — it had dissolved three ounces, 19 deniers, and 15 grains of sugar candy. (It will melt sugar for six whole months.) The experiment was pushed no further; and in all likelihood the particles of the water, loaded and clothed with those of all these different salts, no longer had the motion, or the shape, needed for new dissolutions. This water, the points of its little bodies blunted or sheathed like a fencing foil's, could no longer work into the parts of the salts to split, scatter, and dissolve them as before, when the points of its particles were, so to speak, like the point of a sword.

Monsieur Gassendi was also curious enough to perform this experiment. He testifies that he gave it all possible attention: "id cum demirarer, & tacitus causam perpenderem." And after seeking the reason why one and the same water can take up particles of so many different shapes, he concludes that the water must necessarily have pores and gaps configured like these salts. Of necessity, he says, there are in water empty spaces, like little lodgings proportioned to the shape and size of these particles, these little guests, though the eyes can perceive nothing of them: "ut paucis dicam, ostendit quam varia, insensibilia licet, loculamenta contineret aqua." [Physic. sect. 1, bk. 2, ch. 5, p. 190.]

When a person has, besides, all the natural dispositions I've explained — the ones that make someone extremely sensitive to the impressions of the particles spread in the air — nothing on the side of the skin's pores prevents him from being soaked by atoms as different in shape as those rising from the springs, the mines, the hidden treasures, and the traces of thieves and fleeing murderers.

[End of Chapter Thirteen]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER FOURTEEN

Of all the ways of discovering underground watercourses, the Divining Rod is the best.

Water doesn't just embellish gardens, palaces, and cities; it's one of those things life absolutely cannot do without. The Romans, the greatest and wisest politicians who ever lived, never encamped their armies or founded cities except where there was water. They even examined beforehand, with great care, the entrails of the local animals; and when they found them livid and marked with certain spots, they concluded the local water was unhealthy and moved elsewhere. That's why the learned have considered it no small service to states and empires to apply themselves, now and then, to finding ways of locating springs.

1. Vitruvius — who labored for the glory of Augustus by showing, in his ten books of Architecture, the perfection the arts and sciences had reached under that Emperor's reign — doesn't forget to record the various methods then used to divine where water lay. Here is what he says: "To find the places where there is water, you must, a little before sunrise, lie face-down with your chin resting on the ground where water is sought, and look out across the country; for with the chin steadied, the sight will not rise higher than it should, but will run dependably level; and if in some place a moist vapor is seen rising in waves, dig there; for this does not happen in places without water. Moreover, when seeking water, the quality of the soil must be examined, because certain ground yields it more abundantly. Water found in chalk is never plentiful or good-tasting; in shifting sand it is scanty, and even muddy and unpleasant, if found after deep digging; in black earth it is better, where the winter rains collect in it and, having soaked through, stop at solid, non-spongy layers. Water born in sandy soil, like that along riverbanks, is also very good; but the quantity is moderate, and its veins unreliable. The veins are more reliable, and good enough, in coarse sand, in gravel, and in carbuncle-earth. In red stone they are good too, and abundant, provided the water doesn't escape through the joints of the rock. At the foot of mountains, among rocks and pebbles, the waters are more abundant, colder, and more wholesome. In the valleys they are salty, heavy, lukewarm, and unappealing — unless they come down from the mountains and travel underground to those places, or unless the shade of trees gives them that pleasant sweetness found in waters issuing from the foot of mountains.

"Beyond what has been said, there are other marks for recognizing places where water may be found — namely, where there grow small rushes, willows sprung up on their own, alders, the vitex, reeds, ivy, and all the other plants that are born and thrive only where there is water. But don't trust these plants if you see them in marshes, which, lying lower than the surrounding country, receive and collect the winter rains falling on the neighboring fields and hold them a long while. If, however, these plants appear naturally, unplanted, in places that are not marshes, you may look for water there. And if these marks fail, try this test. Dig a pit three feet wide and at least five deep, and at sunset place at the bottom a vessel of bronze or lead, or a basin — it doesn't matter which; rub the vessel with oil inside and invert it, then cover the pit with reeds and leaves, and then with earth. If the next day drops of water are found clinging to the inside of the vessel, the place has water. Likewise, if a vessel of unbaked clay is put in the pit and covered the same way, where there is water the vessel will come out damp and softened by the moisture. If wool is left in the pit, and the next day water flows from it when wrung, that's a mark the place has plenty. If a lamp full of oil, and lit, is shut in there, and the next day is found not burned out — the oil and wick not wholly consumed, or the lamp itself damp — that signifies water in the place, because gentle heat draws moisture to itself. You can make yet another test by lighting a fire on the spot: if, once the ground is well heated, a thick vapor rises, it is a place that has water.

"When these signs have been found, the place must be dug in the manner of wells; and if a source is found, several other wells must be sunk all around and joined together by underground conduits. But know that it is chiefly on the north-facing slopes of mountains that water should be sought, and that there it is found better, more wholesome, and more abundant; for those places are not exposed to the sun, being covered with very thick trees, and the mountainside casting its own shade — so that the sun's rays, striking obliquely, cannot dry the earth. It is also in the hollows at the tops of mountains that the rainwater gathers, and the trees growing there in great numbers preserve the snow a very long time; melting little by little, it seeps down through the veins of the earth; and it is this water which, reaching the foot of the mountains, produces the springs there. But springs issuing from the bottoms of valleys cannot carry much water; and even if there were plenty, it could not be good; for the sun, heating the unshaded plains, consumes and exhausts all the moisture — or at least draws off what is lightest, purest, and most healthful, which dissipates into the vast expanse of the air, leaving only the heaviest, rawest, and most disagreeable parts for the springs of the open country." [Vitruvius, Book 8, ch. 1, pp. 252–253 of the translation of M. Perrault of the Royal Academy of Sciences.]

2. Pliny knew too well how much good water matters to the convenience of life to neglect giving means of finding it in arid country. So he addresses it in his Natural History, abridging what Vitruvius, before him, had written at greater length. Here is how he puts it. There are, he says, signs that betray the watercourses hidden in the bowels of the earth: rushes, reeds, and frogs when they seem to be brooding, so closely do they press the earth to draw moisture from it. There are besides the willow, the vitex, the alder, the ivy — above all when these plants come up on their own. For if they were planted, and the place holds water only by collecting rainfall, the sign is unreliable, and you could be deceived. But the mark drawn from the moist vapors seen from afar, before sunrise, rising at certain spots, is a sign to count on far more. Yet it must be admitted this way of seeking sources is very laborious, demanding such concentration that the eyes themselves ache from it: "Certior multo nebulosa exhalatio est, ante ortum solis longius intuentibus ... sed tanta intentione oculorum opus est, ut indolescant." [Plinius, Histor. Natur. bk. 31, ch. 3.]

3. Cassiodorus, in a letter of Theodoric, King of the Ostrogoths, says it's an infallible sign of watercourses in a place when fine vapors are seen rising from it, forming a kind of column in the air; and that the well-finders judge how deep the water lies by the height this sort of smoke reaches: "Addunt etiam in columnae speciem conspici quemdam tenuissimum fumum, qui quanta fuerit altitudine porrectus ad summum, tanto in imum latices latere cognoscunt." A fine observation, worthy of the great Cassiodorus, who shows plainly in his fine, learned letters that nothing of what is strongest and finest in the arts escaped him. It's astonishing that a man so taken up with affairs of state could have such precise knowledge of all the sciences. In this letter he specifies the care with which this water-seeker — brought expressly from Africa to Rome — should be treated, noting that his art was not unknown to the ancients and should not be rejected now. He then lists most of the signs the well-finders use to confirm whether a place has water, and gives the detail with such diligence and erudition that he records some that Vitruvius, practically a man of the trade, knew nothing of. Such is the sign Cassiodorus says the well-finders hold infallible: when, in the morning after sunrise, they see what look like clouds of tiny flies hovering close to the ground, always at one particular spot, they conclude there is certainly water beneath: "Sole autem declarato intuentur etiam magistri loca solliciti, & ubi supra terram minutissimarum volitare solitudinem conspexerint omnino muscarum, tunc promittunt aquas facile quod quaeritur inveniri." Cassiodorus teaches us that the art of seeking water was cultivated among the Greeks as among the Latins, and that a certain Marcellus had written a work on springs and underground waters. Finally he closes by commending this water-seeker to the magistrate who had written to him about the man: "If you find," he says, "that this man has as much experience as is claimed, see to his keep; and assure him his secret will be well paid for, if he will confide it to someone. For though Rome has all the waters and fountains one could wish, it's otherwise in certain suburbs, where this man's science is needed; good sense requires us to keep hold of whatever is useful to us in any respect. He should also be paired with someone who knows mechanics, to raise the waters he finds. Let this water-seeker, then, be treated with the same distinction shown to those who possess the arts useful to the public — so that it may never be said that, during our reign, anything whatsoever was neglected of all that Rome could wish for her convenience and her embellishment. Habeatur ergo iste inter reliquarum artium magistros: ne quid desiderabile putetur fuisse, quod sub nobis non potuerit Romana civitas continere." [Theodoric. epist. 53. Cassiodor. variar. bk. 3, p. 58.]

4. Palladius, reporting the first method Vitruvius gives for spotting source-places, warns that you must make sure the ground where the vapor rises is not damp at the surface, so the vapor can only be attributed to spring water flowing beneath. He adds that the test should be made in August, when the pores of the earth are open and give the vapors a freer passage.

5. The Egyptians, on the 17th of June and the days following, when the dew customarily falls, take a clod of earth, weigh it very exactly, and keep it in the house. Then they weigh it again every morning; and if they find its weight steadily increasing, they conclude the air is purified, and that the Nile will overflow in a happy, abundant flood.

6. EXPERIMENT. Father Jean François the Jesuit says that to draw water from the very air, you should calcine tartar, grind it, knead it into little balls, then dry them in a brick furnace with a flame fire; that done, if these little balls are exposed to the air in a cucurbit with the head fitted on, they will attract so much moisture that, by setting fire under the cucurbit, water can be drawn from it every day. The same can be done, he says, with any very dry salt, though well-calcined tartar is incomparably the best for it. The same Father advises, for discovering sources, boring the earth with long augers, because they bring up materials of various natures, from which one can guess whether water is hidden under that ground. He adds that augers are made that can bore through the stones they meet; and that if they're not long enough, you should first dig four or five feet into the earth at the spot in question. [L'art & la conduite des eaux, p. 8.]

7. Father Kircher gives us a method for detecting underground watercourses, which he himself has tried several times with much success. It is indeed admirable — not for searching out the places where water lies, but for confirming whether there truly is any at a spot you already suspect. At least it's perfectly easy to execute. Make a needle of wood, two or three feet long, like a compass needle. Father Kircher calls this needle the Divining Rod. One of its ends must be of an added wood that soaks up moisture readily, such as alder. The needle is hung in balance, on a pivot, or on an axle, or by a thread, at a spot where you suspect water. If there is some, the vapors rising from it penetrate the alder end quickly and easily, the needle loses its balance, and that end tips toward the earth. He wants the trial made only in the morning, when the vapor is most abundant, not yet consumed by the heat of the sun: "ante meridiem dum vapor est copiosior." [Kirker bk. 3, de Magnetism. ch. 7, p. 718.]

8. Finally, note carefully: 1. that springs are found mostly on the sides of mountains and hills exposed to the moist, rainy winds — in France, the west wind; 2. that the steepest mountains have the fewest springs; 3. that mountains covered in much greenery ordinarily always carry watercourses inside them.

There, if I'm not mistaken, are the most-used and best methods of seeking water ever known. Yet it must be admitted that, fine and curious as they are, most of them are unreliable and very difficult — excepting Father Kircher's, which seems to have fewer drawbacks; but his isn't suited to pointing out where a source lies so much as to judging whether there's water at an already-marked spot. After all, you'd need two hundred of his needles to find out, in any reasonable time, whether a given stretch of country holds water. Pliny says the method of seeking sources by inspecting certain trees that grow only in damp places is unreliable, and that you can be deceived by it. He calls this mark a deceitful augury: "augurium fallax." [Histor. Natur. bk. 26, ch. 4.] Palladius, for the same reason, warns against counting too heavily on the vapors that rise in waves, because this happens in all low ground, where the waters, running down from the mountains, descend and collect.

I conclude that the way of conducting this search for water by the Divining Rod is a discovery that can't be valued too highly, being surer and quicker than every other method practiced up to now. You'd have to be a great enemy of the public good to run down a practice so fine, so natural, and from which human society can draw such great advantages. People ought to think about cultivating this gift, and cherishing those whom nature has favored with it, rather than busying themselves confusing minds and muddying a matter that nature's secret, hidden mechanism already makes difficult enough to explain.

With the hazel rod you can not only find water, but even say how deep it lies in the earth — so you can calculate, within a small margin, the expense needed to reach it. We've already seen Cassiodorus say that in his time the well-finders took it as established that the vapors rising in a column over underground waters climb visibly into the morning air as high as the sources lie deep in the earth. And today those who seek water with the rod can say, within a foot or a foot and a half, at what depth it lies. They work like this. When they find a spot where the rod dips and indicates water, they mark the point where the movement is strongest and most violent; then they walk on from there until they feel no more moving force on the rod, and mark that spot too. Finally they measure the distance between the two points, and declare that this is the measure of the depth at which the watercourse flows. Is there anything in the world more curious, or better worth examining and perfecting? This practice of our present-day water-seekers shows me that a discovery has been made in this occult physics that the well-finders Cassiodorus describes didn't have. In his day it was known that the column of vapors rose as high in the air as the source lay deep in the earth; today it's known that this column spreads, horizontally and across its diameter, twice as far as it rises. Which is very plausible: moist vapors, sharing the nature of water, should spread along the surface of the earth more than they climb into the air. People study plenty of things that are neither so important nor even so curious.

You can also judge the size and abundance of a source by the rod's more or less violent movement: the faster it dips, the more water there is. The author of the book entitled The Restitution of Pluto recognized this very well. "If you already have," he says, "some indications that there is water at a spot, then to avoid being deceived, you must apply in those places the rod of Mercury, which demonstrates the quantity of the water, and whether you should stop there or not." He adds that "if in such a case you apply the Lunar rod, or the Mercurial, over the spot, and it dips halfway toward the East, West, North, or South, it is quite certain there is water on the side toward which it leans. And if it doesn't bend halfway, that's a sign of little water." [pp. 120, 122.]

But finally we have Father Déchales the Jesuit, so renowned for his vast work Mundus Mathematicus, who declares that no method compares to the Divining Rod for the discovery of water. Of all the means tried up to now, he says, it is the easiest and the most certain. After reporting and examining several of those means, he adds: There is indeed another method of seeking sources, which would unquestionably be altogether marvelous, if everyone had the faculty of practicing it. The whole mystery consists in taking a forked branch of hazel, or of almond, and carrying it in one's hands through the places where one wants to find water; for the moment one stands over a source, the rod turns in the hands, and dips. "Est etiam alia methodus quae haud dubie, si omnibus succederet, esset mirabilis." [Mund. mathemat. vol. 2, de fontib. nat. proposit. 16, p. 190.]

[End of Chapter Fourteen]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER FIFTEEN

Of all the ways of discovering mines, the Divining Rod is the best. France has many very rich mines. Different rods for different metals. Three fine experiments in the rod's favor.

Human life would have been very innocent and very tranquil if people had been content with the riches nature displays before our eyes with so much pomp and splendor; instead they preferred to dig themselves abysses in the bowels of the earth, hunting there, at the cost of a thousand pains and a thousand hazards, metals they could have done without — metals the Creator had very wisely hidden from them. Unable to satisfy themselves with the goods all around them, they descended into the depths and invented that low, laborious art of piercing rocks and opening mountains, to scrape together a few minerals in those deep veins where they are sometimes buried alive. "Innocentius," says Pliny, "imo vero & delicata esset vita, si nihil aliud quam supra terras concupisceret, breviterque, nisi quod secum est." [Histor. nat. bk. 33, in proëmio: Life would be more innocent — nay, even delightful — if it coveted nothing but what is above the earth, in short, nothing but what is with it.]
