I. When chance takes a hand in discovering mines, it costs human industry and labor nothing. This happens in several ways. 1. When heavy rains form torrents that sweep away the earth covering the mines: then, with a pleasant surprise, the riches nature concealed there come into view — as happened long ago with the silver mines of Freiberg, a town of Germany in Upper Saxony, exposed by torrents the rain had formed. 2. Mines are sometimes discovered when violent winds tear up trees growing directly over veins of gold or silver. 3. When high winds, torrents of rainwater, an avalanche of snow, lightning, or earthquakes break rocks loose from mountaintops, very precious mines are sometimes laid bare. 4. Justin relates that Galicia is so rich in copper, lead, and above all gold, that laborers have often cut chunks of gold-ore with the share of their plow: "auro quoque ditissima, adeo ut etiam aratro frequenter glebas aureas excindant." [Histor. bk. 44, ch. 3.] Mines have sometimes turned up in the digging of wells. 5. Diodorus of Sicily says that fires shepherds set in the forests of Spain exposed mines. Athenaeus likewise relates that fire broke out by accident in the forests of Gaul, and the melted silver flowing in streams revealed very rich, very abundant silver mines: "In Gallia cum sylva caesa accensa consedisset, liquatum argentum profluxit." [bk. 6, p. 28.] 6. Some affirm that at Goslar, a town of Germany in Lower Saxony, a horse discovered a lead mine by striking with its hoof. The same has sometimes happened through hogs rooting up the earth for acorns. [Glauber oper. miner. part 2.]

These are strokes of luck not to be counted on. Such prodigies don't happen every day. Better to rely on the rules that long experience has taught, by which the search for mines is ordinarily conducted.

1. When you find pieces of ore on the ground, or pure metal breaking the surface, you can be sure there's a mine. That's how the famously rich mine of Kuttenberg in Bohemia was discovered — by a monk who, walking in a wood, came upon what looked like a little pipe of silver rising out of the earth. Very wisely, he left his frock there as a marker and ran to tell his convent. [Glauber part 2 of the operis mineralis, p. 28.]

2. When there's hoar-frost on the ground, there's none over the metal veins, because dry, warm vapors rise from them and prevent freezing. For the same reason snow doesn't last as long there either. Some mineralists put great stock in this sign.

3. It's a fairly reliable sign of mines when you notice, toward the end of spring, that the plants and trees in the area lack vigor, their leaves mottled with spots and not a very lively green.

4. Mountains whose foot faces north and whose summit faces south often contain silver mines, which customarily run from east to west.

5. The mountains are examined; and from the color of the earth and the stones, one not only guesses there are mines, but even reads what kind of metal they hold. Green earth indicates copper. Black promises gold and silver. Grey offers hope of nothing but iron and lead.

6. Arid mountains, parched and sterile, all contain some metal — because the bad exhalations rising from the mines kill the plants. Perhaps it's with this in view that Job says the birds do not dwell on the ground where gold and precious stones grow, and the vulture does not turn that way: "Semitam ignoravit avis." [Job 28.]

7. When the stones or earth of a place prove heavier than usual, one augurs metals there.

8. The springs flowing at the foot of mountains help reveal whether there are metals. Not only do the color and smell of the water help decide the question, but the bed of these waters always carries flakes and other traces of the metals above. Agricola says the inhabitants of Navarre drew from the bottom of their wells an earth loaded with gold — which convinces him that this part of France certainly holds very rich gold mines. [Agricola de re metallica bk. 2.]

9. There are plants, though few, which, having sympathy and affinity with the metals, ordinarily grow above the mines and so mark where metals lie. Such are juniper, ivy, the wild fig, the wild pine, and most plants that bear points and thorns.

10. When a mountain regularly gives off fumes and vapors, above all toward the summit, it testifies that it holds metals inside.

These are the signs the mineralists steer by in prospecting. They're drawn from Agricola, from Cardan, from Glauber, and from Father Kircher, who counts as many as 17. But after weighing them, I've found that the ones I leave out rest only on slight conjecture, or reduce to the ten I've just given.

I can't better prove the unreliability of all these signs than by what Father Roberti the Jesuit says in a book entitled Goclenius Heautontimorumenos, which he wrote with great heat against Goclenius over the magnetic cures. When Lucian has Mercury tell the Gods that the two philosophers disputing about Providence in the Portico of Athens had so far got only as far as insults, he wasn't wrong to suppose that philosophers sometimes descend to coarse words — and are quite capable of passing from words to blows, de verbis ad verbera. You're about to see it. Father Roberti, who spares the Divining Rod nothing and uses every weapon to discredit it, nevertheless concedes, in the heat of combat, something well worth marking: that the signs by which the wisest prospectors govern themselves are all unreliable, that they are deceived by them constantly, and that not one can be counted on. "What!" says this Father, "shall more knowledge and more discernment be credited to a brute, mute stick than is found in hundreds of very enlightened men? They traverse fields, mountains, and valleys, applying prodigious attention to everything before their eyes: they detect no trace of metals; and if they come to suspect there might be some at a given spot, they admit nothing is less certain than their conjecture, and they learn daily, with grief, after great labor and expense, that their signs are thoroughly deceptive. Yet Goclenius, armed with his fork, will come ranging over the same ground; and guided by that instrument more clear-sighted than the wisest men, he will stop, infallibly, over the treasures the earth hides. He will point them out. People will dig where he marks, and find them. My dear reader, shall I speak frankly? It is the devil who guides Goclenius."

There's a figure of rhetoric pushed rather hard. But setting aside its excess, let's note what it contains: that to this day, the most learned and experienced men in the search for mines have no certain marks for recognizing the metal veins nature has hidden in the bowels of the earth: "Quid tandem bruto & muto ligno tantam scientiam attribuit, quantam nec in centenis sapientibus viris reperiat? Obibunt hi agros, montes, valles, sollicite considerantes omnia. Nusquam metalla latere agnoscent. Et si quid suspicentur, fatentur ipsi incertam esse conjecturam, idemque saepe eventus ostendit, quo non raro frustrentur, labore sumptuque inaniter profusis. ...... Obibit Rudolphus Goclenius furcifer, fodiet ille, & thesauros reperiet, quod Divinitati suae virgulae ascribet. ... Si clare dicendum est, factus est miser Rhabdolatra & Daemonolatra," etc. [Theat. sympath. p. 582.]

So even through all this declamation, you can see plainly that the Divining Rod must be counted a most precious invention, and that cultivating its use matters enormously — since it's the only sure guide we have in the world to lead us to the treasuries where nature engenders the metals.

In fact, it must be conceded that the light to be had from the ten signs above is very weak, and that they yield no reasonable conjecture, at the least, for telling what mineral a mine contains; these various signs are very ambiguous, and most of them fit equally the mines of sulphur, antimony, salt, mercury, lead, iron, copper, tin, silver, gold, and azure. But with the Divining Rod you can tell which metal lies under the spot where it bends: put two pieces of gold in the two hands, and it will turn only over gold, being charged with gold's particles; put silver in them, and it will dip only over silver. So at least say those who pride themselves on knowing how to use the rod well. Beyond that, you can also learn, within a little, the depth of the mine, working as I described for finding how deep a spring lies in the earth.

It's this uncertainty that explains why, although France holds perhaps as many underground riches as the Indies, no one dares risk the great outlay necessarily involved in opening mountains and piercing rocks to reach the metal veins, which ordinarily lie very deep; the fear of finding nothing after much labor reasonably holds everyone back. France is, beyond dispute, above the other kingdoms of Europe what Europe itself is above the other parts of the earth; and if the French yield to no nation in the sciences and the arts, in great captains and wise magistrates, her fields are also as fertile and abundant in grain, in wine, and in everything else needed for the comfort of life as any country in the world. If she has mountains dry, scorched, and sterile, that comes from the immense riches shut in their depths — since it's the warm exhalations rising from the metallic matter to the surface that kill the plants. If France once set herself to working the mines she has, she would find in them the riches the Spaniards discovered in 1544 in the mines of Potosí, a town of Peru. I'll go further: France alone contains in her lands every kind of mine of which each other state boasts one or two. France has steel as good as Spain's, and iron as good as Germany's and Sweden's. France has more tin and lead mines than England. France has good gold and silver mines, like Hungary, Dalmatia, and Lower Saxony. France has marbles of every color, porphyry, jasper, and alabaster, like Italy. France has crystal like Venice; saltpeter, white, green, and blue vitriol, and orpiments, like Upper Hungary; calamine, bitumen, pitch, as well as Liège. Finally, France has azure, and even fine precious stones — amethysts, agates, emeralds, hyacinths, rubies, garnets, sapphires, turquoises, and diamonds — to say nothing of the springs and streams that carry fine pearls and flakes of gold.

To confirm what I've just said — though I have seen some of these mines myself — I want to give here a thoroughly curious catalogue of the greater part of the mines in the kingdom, discovered, with infinite care, cost, and labor, over the space of ten years, by a German whom the late Cardinal de Richelieu brought into France for the purpose. This catalogue, which cost that able mineralist more than three hundred thousand francs, is too precious to let perish. It has at least become so rare that the little book containing it — titled The Restitution of Pluto to His Eminence — can scarcely be found any longer. Those who have the public good at heart will thank me for printing this catalogue; and I'm the more bound to include it here because all these mines were discovered by means of the Divining Rod.

CATALOGUE OF SEVERAL MINES DISCOVERED IN FRANCE BY MEANS OF THE DIVINING ROD.

In the Pyrenees mountains. Near St. Béat, a good mine carrying a quantity of gold. At the mountain of Sault, a gold mine. A league from Lorde, a good silver mine. Half a league from St. Bertrand, a great mine of crystal, and two of copper, which contain much silver.

In the County of Foix. At the place called Rivière, a gold mine. At the mountain of Montroustaud, a silver mine and one of copper that holds silver. At the mountain of Cardazet, a silver mine. At the place called the Mines of l'Appic, a mine of lead charged with silver. Near the village of Pech and Château-Verdun, three lead mines and one of copper. At the place called Désastie, a silver mine. At the place of Cousou, a silver mine that holds gold.

In Languedoc. At the place called la Bastide Delpeyrac, five jet mines, at which 400 men work every day. In the same territory, a vitriol mine. Near Tournon, six mines of a varnish that contains lead and silver. In the County of Alais, six iron mines and four of coal. In the Marquisate of Portes, three iron mines and two of coal. At the place of Malbois, an antimony mine and one of zinc. At the place of Bousque near the Rhône, a quarry of flintstones of a very fine gold color. Near la Vaouste, a varnish mine that holds lead and silver. At Lodève, a copper mine that holds silver, one of crystal, and one of sulphur. In the Barony of Reguès near Narbonne, a gold mine. At the village of St. Jean near the town of les Vents, a copper mine. A league from le Vigan, a mine of azure-stone, a mine of green earth, and five coal mines.

In the Rouergue and the Quercy. At the place of St. Félix de Sorgues, a good copper mine. At the same place, Diocese of Vabres, another copper mine. Near the town of Meux de Barres, in the valley of Combellon, a silver mine. At the place of Torsac, a copper mine. Near Villeneuve d'Agenois, a copper mine. At the place of Najéat, a copper mine; above it, a mine of azure under the Parish Church of the same place. At the place of Crémeaux, eight coal mines. At Rodez near the Château de Corbières, a copper mine.

In the Condomois, in the land of Mézin, a gold mine.

In the Vellay and Gévaudan, a mine of white and blue sapphires, very good. Near le Puy, in the territory of St. Germain, at Espailly, in a stream called in the language of the country lou Rion Tégoulliou, are found garnets, rubies, hyacinths, and good and fine opals. There are also around le Puy many quarries of gypsum and talc, and a quantity of millstones, as in the territory of Blavaugy. At Aussonne, a jet mine. Near the village of Do, at the mountain of Équierre, a silver mine. At the place of Samatan, three turquoise mines. At the place of Dizau, four iron mines. Near Bigorre, a good lead mine.

In Auvergne. At the place of Pegu, a good Amethyst mine. Under the Château d'Usson, in the vineyard of Antoine du Vert, a mine of Azure. At the Abbey of Menar, marcasites, flintstones, and a sulphur mine. At the village of Rouripec, near Pontgibaud and the mountain of le Puy, a good silver mine. At Saint-Andon near St. Amand, a copper mine. Near the Town of Brioude, a marble quarry. Near Langeac and Brioude, an antimony mine. Along the River of Langeac, a quantity of millstones for sharpening lancets, razors, etc. At the place called Prunet, four mines of coarse slate, called slates of Matte, good for covering houses instead of tiles. At the place of Murat, several quarries of similar slates.

In Provence. In the territory of le Luc, Diocese of Fréjus, a silver mine; and half a league from there, one of lead. At the mountain of Mondrieu, a varnish mine. In the territory of Sisteron, a copper mine. In the territory of Verdaches near Digne, a copper mine that holds gold and silver. At the place of Barles, an iron mine. At the place of Beaujeu, a lead mine. At the place of Pierre-Fent, a silver mine. In the territory of St. Trepet, a lead mine. Under the mountain of Callas, a lead mine. In the territory of Yères, a copper mine, containing gold and silver. In the territory of la Molle, a mine of red sulphur, one of orpiment, and one of alum. Near the Carthusian monastery, a lead mine mixed with other metals. In the territory of la Roque, a jet mine, one of iron, and one of copper. In the territory of Ramaticelle, a varnish mine. In the territory of Aix, a copper mine. In the territory of Colombières, a varnish mine. In the territory of Barjols, a gold mine and one of silver.

In Dauphiné. At the mountain of Auriau, a gold mine. Near the town of Die, stones and diamonds like those of Alençon.

In Bourbonnais. At the village of Uris, a lead mine.

In Normandy. Near Pont-Audemer, a mine of Azure.

In Maine. In the forest of Talla near la Ferté-Bernard, a copper mine with a quantity of slate.

In the Forez. At Saint Julien, a varnish mine.

In Brittany. Near the town of Lavion, a mine of Amethysts and one of Silver.

In Picardy. Near Laon, a mine of yellow amber.

That's more than 150 mines discovered in the kingdom by the labors of the German I've mentioned, who adds that he found a quantity of others besides. For my part, I can affirm that I've seen more than 50 of which he makes no mention.

But I can't resist telling here of a gold mine discovered near Toulon, which is doubtless richer than all those of Potosí. The story is fine, important, and well suited to showing that France yields nothing to all the advantages the other kingdoms of the earth boast of. The account comes from the book entitled The True Chymical Anatomy of Mineral Waters, by Henry du Rochas. This author, who was on the spot, tells it thus. In Provence, near Toulon, there's a mountain called Carquairanne, where there lived a potter who, climbing down into a cavern to retrieve a kid goat that had fallen in, noticed several caves around him. In the main one he found a great heap of stones piled on one another, of a matter yellow like brass; there was even a kind of branch that had grown up in the shape of a man's arm. The potter took a piece of about five pounds and carried it next day to Toulon, where a goldsmith bought it for 50 écus. The goldsmith, having extracted from it four pounds of very good, very pure gold, went to the sieur de Scaravaque, then Governor of the place, and explained how important it was to pursue the matter. Meanwhile the potter, lured by the 30 écus, went back to the cavern with his wife; they carried off that branch which had grown to the height of a man's arm, and which weighed 80 pounds. He blocked the cavern's entrance and planted bushes to hide it. The sieur de Scaravaque had the potter brought in, but could get nothing from him about the truth of the affair — the man insisting he had picked up the yellow stone at the seashore. He was locked in a chamber, where he was found dead at daybreak. His widow was summoned; but she could never find the place again, remembering only that from inside the cavern you could hear the waves of the sea. The father of the sieur du Rochas, then general of the mines in Provence, traveled to this mountain; but an illness that came on diverted him from the important search. Finally the sieur du Rochas, having surveyed this rich mountain, noticed that the summit was almost entirely azure — a sure sign that beneath it lies a mine of gold and silver, and that with a permission from the King one could, in a short time, find at least one vein leading to the center where all those immense treasures repose. [pp. 34 to 51.]

Mathieu Paris, in his history of France, speaks of a rich gold mine found in 1602 in the Lyonnois, near the village of St. Martin la plaine, in the middle of a peasant's vineyard. And he relates how a piece of gold from this mine, formed like the branch of a tree, was presented to Henry the Great. [vol. 2, bk. 5, p. 200.]

That's what I thought I should say about the number and richness of the mines found in France, which make this kingdom the richest, as it is today the most powerful, under heaven. But Cicero said very well that nature would have formed in vain the gold, the silver, and the other minerals in the bowels of the earth, had she not also taught men sure means of finding their veins: "aurum, & argentum, aes, ferrum frustra Natura divina genuisset, nisi eadem docuisset, quemadmodum ad eorum venas perveniretur." [De Divinat. bk. 1, num. 116.] And since the usual methods are neither easy nor certain, it appears that the secret nature destined for this purpose is the use of the Divining Rod.

III. The author of the little book entitled The Restitution of Pluto to His Eminence reports four ways of prospecting a country for mines; then he adds a fifth, on which he counts heavily, and which he calls the metallic rod. "The knowledge and practice of this rod," he says, "are most necessary; and our Ancients used it, to discover from the surface of the earth the metals within, and their depth, and even to know whether the mines are poor or rich. They also employed this rod in the search for springs, before opening the earth." [pp. 12, 13.] I don't know whom he means by the Ancients who supposedly used the hazel rod for mines and springs; it seems to me that what this German says is drawn from chapters 22 through 28 of the second book of the Testament of Basil Valentine, the Benedictine monk — for in that good chymist monk we find at length what the German abridged in his Restitution of Pluto. The Divining Rod bears the same names in both authors, with this difference: they're Latin in Basil Valentine and Italian in the German. In all likelihood the Italian names come from the mines of Trent and Tyrol, where Italian is spoken.

Since there are seven kinds of metals, we're shown seven different kinds of rods, all to be cut under various aspects of the heavens. Many people will consider it pointless to cut the wood of the rod at one time rather than another. It would indeed be hard to say whether these aspects of the heavens do anything. Reason certainly can't decide the point. As for experience, it seems to me one would have to defer to the mineralists who have spent their lives using the Divining Rod as their trade. Now these people insist it should be cut in July, August, and September rather than in any other months. They doubtless have some reason for the practice. Might it not be this: in the other seasons, the trees are full of that oily moisture — those juices destined for their nourishment, if you like — and this matter, which doesn't evaporate easily, would clog the pores and channels through which the particles of vapors, exhalations, and perspiration must flow? Whereas by late summer those juices, consumed and spent on leaves, fruits, and seeds, and even dried up by the heat, no longer block the plant's gaps, and give free passage to the particles that move the rod under the benign, favorable aspects of the stars under which he teaches it should be cut.

Since this rule about when to cut the rod can be explained and defended to some extent, I'll give here the seven kinds of aspects under which he wants the seven rods for the seven metals cut, as far as may be. Each reader can give them as much weight as he likes. What's certain is that the German held firm on the necessity of observing certain aspects to succeed in the search for metals. There's no more superstition in cutting the rod for springs and minerals in July, August, and September than in cutting building timber at one season rather than another. These are observations of the ancients, founded on long experience.

1. VERGA LUCENTI. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek gold, the gold mines, their marcasites, the azure-stone, the gilded talcs, the solar stone, and the other things that are under the influence of the Sun.

2. VERGA CANDENTE, Ò FOCOSA. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek silver, the silver mines, their marcasites, rock-crystal, diamonds, the lunar stones, and the other things that are under the influence of the Moon.

3. VIRGA SALIENTE, Ò SALTANTE. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek copper, the copper mines, their marcasites, emeralds, the stones, and the other things that are under the influences of Venus.

4. VERGA BATTENTE, Ò FURCILLA. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek tin, the tin mines, zinc, the stones, the minerals, and all the things that are under the influence of Jupiter.

5. VERGA TREPIDANTE, Ò TREMANTE. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek lead, the lead mines, antimony, the stones, and the other things that are under the influence of Saturn.

6. VERGA CADENTE, Ò INFERIORE. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek iron, the iron mines, and all that is under the influence of Mars.

7. VERGA OBVIA, Ò SUPERIORE. Face of the Heaven under which the Divining Rod must be cut, to seek mercury, the mineral cinnabar, the stones, the minerals, and all that is under the influence of Mercury.

I'll note that all these Italian names hold nothing mysterious, so far as I can tell from the Testament of Basil Valentine, which I've read only in an old and rather obscure French translation. I've concluded that all these different names come simply from the various phenomena seen in the rod's movement. Sometimes you see a little trembling motion; now it dips, now it rises — above all when the metals sought lie above the person holding it. When the fumes of the mines are strong, you sometimes see at its tip a little cloud of particles in motion, recalling now fire, now light. It usually makes little leaps over mercury mines. From these various movements it has received its various names. Finally, Basil Valentine and the German both seem to prefer hazel above all other woods for making the rod.

IV. Father Kircher, who believes the rod's dip comes from the skill — or rather the knavery — of its user, substitutes another sort of Divining Rod which no one will accuse of being unnatural. He teaches its use by three experiments he describes, which are curious, and which at bottom don't differ much from the ordinary rod, as you soon see by comparing them a little carefully.

FIRST EXPERIMENT. Make a kind of little wand of some mineral salt, three or four inches long; join it to the end of a rod of any wood, and hang the whole in balance from a thread so it can move easily, or set it on a pivot like a compass needle. If this wand is balanced over a pot of salt water, or seawater, with fire beneath it, the volatile spirits of the salt will certainly rise as vapor, fasten onto the mineral-salt end of the wand, and by their weight tip it toward the earth. There's every reason to believe the same would happen over a salt mine.

SECOND EXPERIMENT. Make a rod as just described, except with a little wand of gold in place of the mineral salt; hang it likewise in balance over a pot containing quicksilver, and it's certain the fire will at once drive the mercury into vapor, which will infallibly fasten onto the gold end of the rod; that end, loaded with the weight of the quicksilver, will promptly dip. The rod would dip the same way over a mercury mine. It's likewise very probable that running the same experiment with a rod tipped with silver, over a pot of silver-ore ground fine, the silver particles driven off by the fire would fasten onto the matching metal at the rod's end, tip it by their weight, and break its balance. The same would happen over a silver mine. The experiment extends to every other metal. Plainly it's the homogeneity — the affinity — of the parts that makes these metallic particles fasten onto the rod-end of like metal. This fine experiment is also used to find underground water, with rods of alder or other light, porous wood. It succeeds wonderfully. "And I would not say so," adds Father Kircher, "if I did not know it by my own experience": "Quod ibidem dicerem, nisi experimento a me sumpto, id certius cognovissem." [p. 201.] Nature herself taught us this pleasant experiment. Anyone who has seen mines may have noticed how the branches of the surrounding trees curve toward the earth extraordinarily, weighed down by the mineral vapors that coat the leaves like a kind of very delicate little crust. And anyone has been able to observe, a thousand times, that the plants and trees growing at the edge of springs and rivers bow the tips of their branches astonishingly toward the water's surface, soaked and overloaded as they are with the watery vapors continually rising. You can scarcely take a step on the earth without finding things to admire, worthy of the attention of the vastest minds — if it were true that people often used their reason.

THIRD EXPERIMENT. There is an altogether singular sympathy between iron and vitriol, which shuns all the other metals and fastens greedily onto iron. So if you make a wooden rod tipped at one end with iron, and hang it over a vessel containing vitriol, you will see with astonishment, as soon as the fire sets the vitriol's parts in motion, the iron lose its ferrous color and take on copper's so exactly that you'd believe a metallic transmutation was happening. What's more, the iron end becomes so loaded with vitriol particles that the rod breaks its balance and plunges headlong into the vessel.

If you compared these experiments carefully with what the Divining Rod does, you might not find as much difference as many people imagine. At bottom the mechanism is the same. It's the same hands of nature at work everywhere. Don't the warm, dry vapors rising from the earth's mines easily penetrate the Divining Rod, and don't they load it with enough mineral particles to make it sink? And why shouldn't this happen in a man's hands, when it visibly happens to the plants and trees along rivers, or around mines, whose branches bow under the weight of the mineral or watery vapors? On the contrary, doesn't it seem the dip should come easier in a man's hands? The warmth of the hands, penetrating the rod and setting all its inner parts in motion, gives it a readiness to move, to twist, to drink in the particles exhaling from the mine, and to bow under their weight. And if the rod isn't forked, and is carried in balance, as some carry it, isn't it obvious the dip will come more easily still? Father Kircher seems even to agree, when he says he cannot conceive how a rod not carried in balance could receive the impression of the metallic particles so promptly: "siquidem fieri non posse puto, ut virgae non aequilibratae, sed violenter torsae latentia metallo, tantam, & tam subitaneam vim imprimant." [Mundus subterran. bk. 10, sect. 2, ch. 7, p. 200.]

I don't know how Father Kircher — who took such great and praiseworthy pains to inform himself about everything concerning the rod's use — can have been unaware that many people use it precisely by holding it balanced on the back of the hand. But once you really understand that it can be moved by the mines' exhalations when hung in balance, it takes little further attention to understand that the same can happen when it's forked, with the two branches held in the hands. After all, it's perfectly intelligible that, the inner parts of the rod being set in motion by the particles of unnoticed perspiration, the least shock of the metallic vapors against the rod must produce a very perceptible movement. So the three preceding experiments are well suited to leading us to nature's mechanism in what is most secret and most marvelous in the effects of the Divining Rod.

Since the Divining Rod is claimed to be useful for finding treasures hidden in the earth, we should say why it dips over them so perceptibly. It's known that vapors and exhalations rise from the terrestrial globe. We saw, on page 188, how M. Browne says very thick vapors and fumes are found over wells, and over pits dug straight down into the earth; here is why there are more of them there than elsewhere. Consider a well as a cylinder, its bottom the base and its rounded depth the length, and it's easy to compute how much more fine matter a well twenty feet deep and four across must exhale than a circle of ground four feet across at the surface. The four-foot circle has only 12 feet of surface; the well, counting its base and its rounded depth, has 264 feet of surface — 252 feet more than the circle at ground level. So a well twenty feet deep and four wide must give off a cloud of vapors of 252 parts, while the surface circle exhales only 12. And the deeper the pit dug to bury a treasure, the more vapors rise from it; for though the earth is put back, centuries must pass before it settles into the arrangement nature originally gave it. And I'm not even counting what may exhale from the metals themselves, which are a highly transpirable matter.

[End of Chapter Fifteen]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER SIXTEEN

The dip of the Divining Rod over water, metals, and the footsteps of criminals does not come from the demon. This divination has nothing whatever to do with Rhabdomancy.

This chapter must open with a warning of the highest importance — one that will strip our scrupulous gentlemen of a veil they hide behind with extreme self-satisfaction, believing it does them great honor. They even announce, pointedly, that they are not of the party of the Naturalists but of the scrupulous; the distinction shows in their letter in the Mercure, where they had the two words printed in different typefaces — as if there were great merit in being scrupulous and shame in being a naturalist, and as if they were doing religion a great service by crediting the demon, and criminal magic, with the dipping of the Divining Rod. They need to be taught that referring this effect to the workings of nature serves the glory of religion no less; and they'll agree, provided they take nature not as the pagan philosophers did, but as I declared on page 63 it must be taken — that is, as the general laws of motion the Creator established, and by which He governs the whole universe. In this sense nature is aptly enough called by some the daughter of God, the arm of God, the force of God, the voice of God, ΘΕΟΥ ΦΩΝΗ. Nature in this sense may well be called by Seneca God, or the reason of God sustaining the world and keeping it in the order and harmony we admire: "Quid est enim aliud Natura, quam Deus & Divina ratio toti mundo, & partibus ejus inserta?" [bk. 4, de Benef. ch. 7.] Nature, in this sense, is what M. Gassendi so finely names the general providence of God, watching and presiding over the world as a pilot in his ship, a music-master in a concert, a father in his family, a general in an army, a king in a state. [bk. 4, Physic. sect. 1, ch. 6, p. 323, vol. 1.] Finally, in this sense — though the names of party and sect are always odious — I dare promise myself that the physicists of Lyon, who have explained the phenomena of the rod by the laws of nature, will accept the name of Naturalists without much annoyance. And being wise men, they'll take care not to call those who perch the demon on the rod to make it turn — Demonists.

I. I believe a Christian philosopher, in explaining the surprising phenomena of nature, should imitate what the theologians do in explaining the obscure passages of Holy Scripture. Just as they never resort to the mystical sense as long as they can hold to the literal one, without assuming anything that violates ordinary human understanding — so I'd want the philosophers never to refer to supernatural causes anything that can be demonstrated by the laws of physics. In the case of the rod, there is no necessity whatsoever, to explain its movement over springs, mines, hidden treasures, and the traces of criminals, to bring the demon on stage. If anyone wants to call the little particles of vapors, exhalations, and perspiration spirits — following the example of several physicists, who name them so for their extreme fineness — I consent to the spirits being accused of a part in the rod's movement. If, following certain others, they want to call nature demon, we'll consent to their publishing that the demon is mixed up in it. But if by demon they mean that spirit, enemy of God and men, hurled into the abyss with the rebel angels — I don't know how they figure it; but I'll say frankly that their system is infinitely more complicated, and less intelligible, than the one that explains the rod's dip by the general laws of motion. Saying "it's the demon" is quickly done; proving it clearly, never. So they're certainly telling us things that can't be believed without doing oneself violence.

They'll perhaps plead that they cannot conceive how this dip could come about naturally. I'm persuaded they really don't understand it. I take them at their word; I advise no one to doubt it for a moment. But why do they imagine no man in the world can explain an effect they don't understand? That, truly, is admirable. A good philosopher doesn't rule so brusquely: he asserts only what he has a clear and distinct idea of; so at most, if he doesn't understand how nature could produce such an effect, he says no more than that. And since I'm quite sure he'll never have a clear and distinct idea showing him the demon shaking the rod, he'll never bring himself to maintain there's devilry in it. He won't say, as someone said just days ago: "For my part, I believe all these means diabolical — not only as regards the discovery of thieves, of stolen goods, of field boundaries, but also of waters and metals. I claim ... that the cause can be nothing but the demon." [p. 49 of the Mercure of January 1693.]

Father Malebranche — who has told us elsewhere that truth is almost never found except with evidence, and that evidence consists only "in the clear and distinct view of all the parts and all the relations of the object necessary for forming an assured judgment" — thereby forbids us to believe that the rod's dip over waters, metals, and criminals' traces is diabolical rather than natural. Nothing is finer than the excellent rule he gives for avoiding error. I'd want not only everyone to know it, but everyone to regulate all their judgments by it. Here it is, as it stands in ch. 1 of Book I of the Recherche de la vérité, p. 17: "One ought never," he says, "to give full consent except to propositions that appear so evidently true that one cannot withhold it without feeling an inward pain, and the secret reproaches of one's reason." Certainly, holding to this admirable rule, no one will believe the rod's movement diabolical rather than natural. Why? Because one would first have to know, clearly and distinctly, all the natural causes that could bear on this effect, and be assured, after examining them, that none of them contributed anything. Frankly, I grant that after that labor and study — which demands no mediocre mind — a man has earned an incontestable right to rule on whether the rod's movement is, or is not, natural. Though the darkness of our minds, and the majesty — to speak like Pliny — under which nature has veiled her mysteries, should always keep us from pronouncing so decisively on many things. We must not measure the extent of nature's power by the narrow bounds of our intelligence. It would surely be bad logic to say: I don't conceive how this can be done; therefore it isn't natural; therefore there's devilry in it. There's a great deal wrong with that reasoning, since it assumes, as a premise, that one understands everything natural — in which one is certainly much deceived; for there are, says Pliny, many things hidden in nature's bosom that we cannot possibly penetrate: "Naturae vero rerum vis atque majestas in omnibus momentis fide caret." [hist. nat. bk. 7, ch. 1.]

Have the philosophers ever properly explained the reasons for the ebb and flow of the sea? Have they untangled how a child comes to be marked with the flowers and fruits its mother craved while carrying it? Do they grasp why the magnet, and the compass needle, decline from north now toward the east, now toward the west? Do they have a very clear and distinct idea of why the magnet repels with one pole the iron it attracted with the other? Do they know why certain springs dry up in times of dearth, and others flow more than usual in times of plenty? Why, when a father or mother of a family dies, the bees die too, or abandon their hives and the house? Why winds and tempests rise when some desperate wretch serves as his own executioner? Why the flowers decorating windows and chimneys wither and die at the death of the master of the house? Why a man's wounds worsen and grow more painful at the approach of someone bitten by a dog or a serpent? Why a murdered man's wounds reopen in the presence of his killer? — supposing all these effects, and countless similar ones, are as real as Camerarius, Frommann, Gaspar Arejes, and Pliny claim. Granted, among the marvelous effects the physicists report there are some that are fables, propped up only by the foolish credulity of simple minds that never examine anything — it will still be conceded that there is a very great number of purely natural effects which those who have studied nature most have never managed to explain, and which it would nevertheless be ridiculous to attribute to the demon: "Quamplurimi," says Gaspar Arejes, "naturae adyta & abdita investigare conati sunt: quorum causas nulli quantumvis assiduo studio occupati inveniri potuerunt." [Campus Elys. Quaest. jucundar. Quaest. 53, p. 894.]

It is therefore an injustice to ascribe to magic effects whose mechanism we don't understand. Let's blame the weakness of our minds rather than turn on nature. Do we believe she never acts except openly and perceptibly? Must she always use visible, tangible agents for us to grant her the honor of a prodigy? The moment she slips away from our senses, must she stand censured by our minds? Will everything not done under our eyes always be done by the devil? Is the demon the only invisible agent? Aren't there also little particles that can travel invisibly from agent to patient, joining by physical contact two bodies that look, to the eye, separated and distant? How many things do the machine-builders accomplish by their art that look to us like enchantments, and that we don't understand? How much more, then, will nature do things that surpass us infinitely further — she who is, as Galen puts it so well, the ablest craftsman in the world: Ἀγαθὸς Δημιουργὸς Φύσις.

Nature, according to Bartholin [de natur. mirabilib. p. 72], is an abyss not to be sounded by the senses alone; they are subordinate judges, whose jurisdiction is far too limited to rule on the extent of her power. Even when we give the mind as guide to our senses, how often do we still come up short before effects that present themselves every day! And after much labor and application, we must sometimes settle for explaining by analogy effects we couldn't unfold precisely in themselves. The great Scaliger was not wrong when he cried out — against Cardan, I believe: You who play the learned man, explain to me, quite clearly, just one of those stones you find in such numbers under your feet: "Dic mihi formam lapidis, qui tamen quotidie tuis obversatur oculis, & Phyllida solus habeto."

To those who attribute the rod's movement to the Devil, I'll say what Van Helmont said on a roughly similar subject. Declaim all you like, and arm yourselves with the specious pretext of fighting superstition: you cannot make this practice suspect to people who reason. How can there be superstition in a usage that employs no words, no ceremonies, no figures, no symbols, no empty observances; that keeps no appointed hours; that profanes nothing holy; that requires, in short, no turn of imagination, no faith, no confidence, no intention, no consent, no special circumstances — nothing that could mark an appeal to the demon's help? But, comes the reply, there is a tacit pact with that enemy of the human race. There, says Van Helmont, is the last entrenchment of the ignorant. There's the anchor they clutch, believing no one can easily pry them off it. They wouldn't dare say that so many people of honor, even of piety, for whom the rod turns over water and metal, are wretched enough to renounce their baptismal vows and bind themselves to the demon by an express, formal contract — no one would believe them; the slander would be too gross. It's much smoother to insinuate an agreement of old standing, struck by some villain with the demon, who undertook that, whenever he saw fit, he would perch on the rod and make it point out the waters and the metals. Whenever he sees fit, indeed: for the rod turns for very few people — so few that plainly either the devil takes no great pleasure in this little game, or he is no longer so hungry for the ruin of men; since there's scarcely anyone who wouldn't like to have this faculty. And perhaps the very man who runs it down is one who discovered, with vexation, after several tries, that he is unhappily without it. These are, in truth, poverties that don't need serious refutation.

Still, let's say once more to these people who glory in their scruples: take care, in condemning so hastily as diabolical an effect so rare and surprising, not to do honor to the demon and injury to nature in one stroke — which would be no small straying. That spirit of pride delights to see burning on his altars an incense that a brutish, sacrilegious hand has lifted from the altars of the living God. Let us not, then, hand the demon the glory of the miracles God works through the ministry of nature — that is, through the general laws of motion He has established, and by which He governs the whole universe.

As for the good souls mentioned in the letter, whom it is hoped to intimidate, we'll tell them that God has not chosen to leave us uncertain about the course to take on such occasions. The theologians, expounding the mind of the Church, have given us rules that put us in safety, and make us unshakable against the clamor of the ignorant. Here are the marks they have left for recognizing whether a practice involves superstition, or some implicit pact. "There is a pact, and superstition," say the theologians, "whenever one is well assured that the effect which appears surprising exceeds the forces of nature, and that its mechanism cannot be unraveled in any way; above all if, to produce the effect, one has recited words unknown, barbarous, false, apocryphal, absurd, or lifted from holy Scripture and the prayers of the Church; if one has kept certain ceremonies, or superfluous, pointless observances with no relation to the expected effect; if one chooses certain feast days, or certain persons, to the exclusion of all others." [St. Thomas 2-2, quaest. 96, art. 1 and 2.] Then, no doubt, there is pact and superstition. It is therefore certain that nothing in the use of the Divining Rod smacks in the slightest of pact or superstition; far from mixing in words, ceremonies, figures, and observances that are empty, useless, and visibly without physical relation to its effects, its users hold all such things in the utmost horror, and declare that everyone who dabbles in them deserves to perish with Jannes, Mambres, and Simon the Magician.

II. But we're not out of the woods yet: our people of the scruples claim that using the rod to seek water and metals is the same thing as the Rhabdomancy condemned by Scripture and the Fathers of the Church. There's no good sense in the claim, but it must be examined and answered anyway. In chapter 21 of Ezekiel we see a superstition of the King of Babylon, who, standing at the fork of two roads — one leading to Jerusalem, capital of Judea, the other toward Rabbath, capital of the Ammonites — and not knowing which to take, wanted the lot to decide. So he mixed his arrows and threw them in the air, to see which way they would fall. St. Jerome adds that this king didn't dare undertake the siege of Jerusalem on his own account, knowing well that 185,000 Assyrians besieging it had been killed there in one night. In the end God's providence allowed the arrows to send him against Jerusalem, after he had double-checked with two other kinds of divination: "Stetit rex Babylonis in bivio, in capite duarum viarum, divinationem quaerens, commiscens sagittas. ... Ad dexteram ejus facta est divinatio super Jerusalem." [vv. 21–22.] You'd need a very lively imagination to find in that what is practiced in the search for springs and mines with the hazel rod. The same goes for these words of the prophet Hosea: "Populus meus in ligno suo interrogavit, & baculus ejus annuntiavit ei." [ch. 4, v. 12.] I know perfectly well this pagan divination is condemnable; I know St. Jerome calls it ῥαβδομαντείαν; I know Theophylact describes the method differently from St. Jerome, and that the interpreters scarcely agree on what exactly the divination consisted in; but I know just as well that it has no relation, near or far, to the rod I've been discussing. All the erudition about rods that the author of the second letter in the Mercure of January 1693 has paraded is pure decoration. What does it prove? The magicians of Egypt, and plenty of others, carried staves when they performed their diabolical mysteries; no doubt they also wore caps: so shall it no longer be permitted to carry either staves or caps? That conclusion is overstretched.

If I cared to, I could report in my turn all the different ways the interpreters say the Orientals used staves for Rhabdomancy. But what would it contribute to the present question? This Rhabdomancy was practiced by the Germans, true, as Tacitus reports: "They are much given to auguries and to the lot," he says, "and observe no great ceremony about it. They cut a branch of some fruit tree into several pieces, mark them with certain signs, then throw them at random onto a white cloth; then the priest, or the father of the family, lifts each twig three times, after praying to the gods, and interprets them by the marks on them." [Tacit. de Germania.] There's a Rhabdomancy nothing like Nebuchadnezzar's. Strabo, bk. 15, depicts differently again the one the Persians practiced. Paulus Venetus [bk. 1, 43] reports another, quite different, in use among the Tartars. The Algerians in Barbary have yet another, which matches the Babylonians' no better. But even if all these blind, superstitious peoples agreed on a single way of divining by wood, what would that have to do with searching out the places where there are springs, or metals? Rhabdomancy is properly a lot one consults to decide which of two undertakings to commit to. Nebuchadnezzar meant to attack both Jerusalem and Rabbath, but didn't know which to start with. He cast lots, which ruled that Jerusalem should be attacked first. That's even the sense of the Hebrew, לקסם קסם, which means to seek an answer by divining by lot — like people who divine by their buttons whether or not they'll do a thing. There's nothing of the kind in the use of the hazel rod: one infers from its movement that there is water in a spot under the earth, just as one judges from the movement of a hygrometer that there are watery vapors in the air, and that rain is therefore coming. Ammianus Marcellinus gives yet another twist to the Rhabdomancy of the Alans: "They divine the future in a marvelous manner," he says: "the women cut very straight rods, doing so with secret enchantments, and at certain precisely marked times. By these rods they know what is to come." [bk. 31.] As for our rod, it's already been said it can be made of any wood, cut at any time, without blessing or enchantment; and if certain wretches practice something of that other kind, we hold it in all possible horror, and heartily consent that they be made to feel every chastisement with which the ecclesiastical and secular judges customarily punish such impieties.

The author of the second letter in the Mercure proves no better a historian than a philosopher. He says the Germans knew nothing of the metallic rod before the Swedes came into Germany under Gustavus Adolphus, King of Sweden, who around 1630 crossed into Germany and conquered two-thirds of it in two and a half years, from the Vistula to the Danube and the Rhine. "It was the Swedes," he says, "who taught the Germans, toward the beginning of this century, the use of rods in divinations — or rather refreshed their memory of it; for Tacitus assures us their fathers, who knew many others, had long been practiced in this little game of rods." [p. 254.] Two things there are false. 1. It is not true that the Swedes taught the Divining Rod to the Germans around 1630, since Basil Valentine, a German Benedictine living around 1490, devoted seven chapters of his Testament to explaining the use of this admirable rod, which he discusses as something thoroughly familiar among the mineralists of Germany. 2. Nor is it true that they refreshed anyone's memory of it then, since throughout this work the reader will meet several German authors who wrote and philosophized on this rod from the beginning of the past century — a full hundred years before the great Gustavus came to ravage the Empire. I could easily cite some thirty German philosophers who discussed the rod even long before the King of Sweden was in the world. Georgius Agricola alone, so well known for his great work de re metallica, published in 1550, treats it as a practice common enough among the Germans.

III. When I say there is no superstition in the use of the Divining Rod as I have presented it, I don't thereby deny that villains and impious men may sometimes lace it with very bad trappings. The world has only too many superstitious people; and it would be easy for me to prove the rod has had corrupters, who put it to very criminal use, mixing in ceremonies and holy words. That is an impiety, from which the rod draws no power; but some people are so corrupted that they spoil everything they touch. According to M. l'Abbé Furetière, the sorcerers set great store by a hazel branch; I know nothing of that; but I know with certainty there is no sorcery in using it within the bounds of nature, within which I have confined its use. For all that, I don't doubt there are sorcerers — though I'm persuaded they're very rare, if by sorcerers one means those who have renounced their baptism to bind themselves by contract to the demon. Rare, I repeat — but they exist. And the hypothesis of those who will admit none at all, denying that the devils communicate perceptibly with men, lands one in embarrassments from which there's no graceful escape.

M. Van Dale, in his treatise on the Oracles, has gone to such an extreme that he maintains the demon never spoke through the Oracles. He accuses all the Fathers of the Church of being unwilling to take the trouble of reasoning exactly; of granting the pagans too readily that there were demons in the Oracles; and of not properly understanding the true interests of the Christian religion. That is rather cavalier — not to say rather impious. Should anyone speak this way of the greatest lights of Christendom, the learned teachers of the whole Christian world? In the same vein, M. Van Dale says: we do not grant that everything the Witch of Endor did for Saul was above nature, an operation of magic and the demon. All the Fathers of the Church, however, believed it was an effect of the black art, of necromancy. On this point there are only two parties in the Church of God: one maintains it was truly the soul of Samuel; the other says it was a demon impersonating the holy prophet. But no one has ever doubted there was devilry in the business — except a few dreaming Rabbis, among them Rabbi David Kimhi, to whom M. Van Dale owes his system, which no Christian I know of had ever followed. This Rabbi imagined the Witch staged a masquerade, dressing a man up as a prophet. But she would have had to give him the prophetic spirit too; for this costumed man prophesied, with perfect precision, the ruin of Saul and his family. Here is the prophecy: "Why do you consult me? Why address yourself to me, since the Lord has abandoned you? The Lord will deal with you as I told you on His behalf. He will rend your kingdom, He will tear it from your hands, to give it to David... He will even deliver Israel into the hands of the Philistines. Tomorrow you shall be with me, you and your sons." [1st book of Kings, ch. 28.] The Witch would have had to be a prophetess herself, to recognize Saul, who came, as the saying goes, incognito. She'd have needed prophecy indeed, to divine that he would ask precisely to see Samuel, and to keep a man standing by ready for the role. She had even foreseen that Saul, who had waged implacable war on diviners all his life, would change his mind that very night, all at once, and come suddenly to her house. There is the absurdity into which people throw themselves who refuse to admit that sorcerers exist.

But just as it is visibly an impiety to deny that there are sorcerers and magicians, it is also a stupidity to put them everywhere and imagine them so common. There is often more malice than truth in these accusations of sorcery. M. de la Mothe le Vayer says it very well: "We know that in countries like Lorraine, when the lords confiscated the bodies and goods of those condemned for sorcery, more sorcerers were seen there than in all the rest of Europe. So it is not without reason that the Parlement of Paris proceeds with great reserve whenever it takes up this crime; for besides the false accusations, one still often sees poor simpletons, and simple women, confessing things that never happened." [De l'instruct. de Monseig. le Dauphin, p. 146.]

[End of Chapter Sixteen]

========================================

THE OCCULT PHYSICS, or TREATISE ON THE DIVINING ROD

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN

Testimonies of several learned men who speak in favor of the Divining Rod.

Here is a cloud of witnesses who regarded the Divining Rod as something that could be used without superstition in the search for watercourses and metals. Some even extol its usefulness in magnificent terms, holding that nothing in nature is more marvelous, or better deserves the attention of philosophers. In reporting the testimonies of these learned men who attributed the rod's movement to nature, I'll follow neither chronological order nor the rank of the witnesses. Nothing has ever been settled about precedence among men of letters; they form a republic where people deal with one another without ceremony, and where nobody stands, as the saying goes, on the qui vive.

1. Basil Valentine, a good Benedictine monk, very learned in natural things, had no scruple about teaching how the hazel rod should be used: he devoted seven entire chapters of the second book of his Testament to it. He believed its use so natural that he even opens by saying that whoever wants to take up these rods must not follow his own whims and his own lights, or innovate anything in the way of using it for prospecting, "because," he adds, "Matter does not suffer anyone to derange her, or prescribe new laws to her." [Basil. Valent. Test. bk. 2, ch. 22.]

2. Michael Maierus, in his book Verum Inventum, hoc est, Munera Germaniae, chapter IV, p. 84 — where he claims the world owes Germany the invention of gunpowder — says the first charcoal mixed with the sulphur and saltpeter for that powder was hazel charcoal. This leads him to the hazel's sympathy with the metals, and he says it's for this reason that a Divining Rod is made of it, well suited to seeking the mines of gold and silver. He compares it to a midwife, whose skill helps the mountains bring forth the metallic matter they carry in their womb: "Praesertim Corylus, quae dat Virgulam Divinatoriam, metallorum sub terra latentium indicem: Dicunt enim in montibus metalliferis hanc plerumque in vertice crescere, atque inde vim illam occultam & per astra imprimi, qua metallis assultet, ea latentia prodat, inque lucem proferenda, velut obstetrix, moveat."

3. Philippe Melanchthon, so learned in physics and mathematics, wrote a discourse on sympathy in which he establishes six degrees in nature, and assigns to the second degree the sympathy found between plants and minerals. As his example he gives the forked hazel branch that mine workers use to seek the veins of gold and silver, and of the other metals. He attributes the rod's movement to the mineral juices the hazel feeds on in the soil. A man who files this effect among those nature produces by affinity and sympathy is clearly persuaded there's nothing in it but the purely natural. Here are his words: "Alter gradus συμπαθητικός est inter plantas & metalla, sicuti quidam ferunt de surculo bifido ex corylo deciso, quo metallorum venas auri & argenti explorant, atque adeo virgulam Divinam vocant: cujus surculi vires augent roborantque succi minerales, &c."

4. Peucer, Melanchthon's son-in-law, so famous for his great work on Divination — a man who mixes the demon into plenty of things he has scarcely any part in — never dreams of involving him here, and speaks of the Divining Rod as something wholly natural. "This rod," he says, "is just a forked hazel branch, with which the veins of gold and silver are discovered, because it dips over the places where these veins lie hidden under the earth. It's hard to explain why hazel branches alone have this power. For my part, I can say nothing except that I judge the hazel has some natural, secret sympathy with the metals." [Peucer de la Divinat. bk. 13, ch. 10.]

5. Keckermannus quotes Melanchthon's whole text, and uses his authority — which he freely acknowledges — to establish his teaching on the admirable sympathies found between certain natural bodies. And this learned man teaches at the same time how the hazel branch should be held. "The workmen," he says, "carry it on the tips of their fingers, and conclude that where it dips, there are veins of some metal. Some among the physicists deny this power is in the hazel; but many firmly affirm the contrary": "Negant quidem hanc vim in corylo Physici nonnulli, sed multi constanter asserunt." [Keckerman. systemat. physic. bk. 1, ch. 8, column 1488.]

6. Simon Maiolo, Bishop of Volturara in the Kingdom of Naples, says the divination that mine workers practice with the hazel rod is entirely natural. He files it among the predictions founded on physics, born of sympathy. Here is how he puts it: This Divining Rod is a forked branch of hazel, with which the mine workers seek the veins of gold and silver underground, because the rod turns when they pass over the spots where there are metals. I couldn't say why the hazel produces this effect rather than the other trees — unless there's a natural, secret sympathy between hazel and the metals: "Virgula divina est ex corylo decisus bifidus surculus, quo venas illi auri, argentive feraces explorant, inclinante sese eo virgula qua sub terra venae feruntur atque incedunt. Qua vi id soli corylorum praestent surculi ... obscurum est; nisi quod conjicio, συμπάθειαν habere corylos ad metalla connatam & occultam." [Dierum Canicular. part 2, colloq. 4, p. 380.]

7. Neuhusius, among the prodigies he recounts about plants, doesn't forget the hazel Divining Rod, which he discusses as a marvel we hold from nature's generous hands, urging us to employ it to draw out the metallic riches the earth hides from us. Finally, quite charmed by the power of this little instrument, he cries out: What then shall I now say of the Divine Rod, which is only a simple hazel branch, and which nevertheless has the power of divination for discovering metals — whether this comes from a natural sympathy with the metals, or from some secret influence of the stars, or from some other cause stronger still? Courage, then! Let us use this salutary rod, so that, having drawn the metals from the dwelling of the dead, we may search whether there isn't in ourselves, too, some faculty for divination, as we observe in the hazel: "Quid vero de VIRGULA DIVINA dicam: quae ex corylo secta indagandis metallis divinatricem facultatem obtinet ...... Age vero utamur tam salutaris baculi opera." [Odo Nuhus. sacror. fatidic. bk. 2, ch. 21, pp. 383 and 384.]

8. Pierre Belon du Mans calls the Divining Rod Caduceus, alluding to the one shown in Mercury's hand. He seems astonished that the Turks make no use of it in working the Grand Seigneur's mines — so he plainly doesn't take the practice for a fable or an evil thing. Here are his words: "The workmen who dig the ore from the earth and bring it up do not have the use of the Caduceus, which in Latin is named VIRGA DIVINA, which the Germans use in spying out the veins." [Observat. bk. 1, ch. 50, p. 16.]

9. Rudolph Glauber had made too many experiments with the Divining Rod not to be worth consulting on its use in the precious work of finding mines. Here is how he speaks of it. The metal mines can also be discovered by the power of a hazel rod. It's used like this — and I speak after having tried the experiment often. Melt the metals under a certain constellation and make of them a ball pierced through the middle; fix in the hole a year-old hazel shoot with no side branches. Carry this rod held straight out before you over the places where metals are believed to lie; and when the rod dips, and the ball sinks toward the earth, you may be sure there is some metal beneath. "And as this method is founded on physics, it must doubtless be preferred to all the other ways of prospecting for metal mines." Glauber didn't merely have sixty years' experience when he published the work I take this testimony from; what deserves special notice is that he used this rod to find metals; that he believes it founded on the laws of nature; and that he prefers it to every method in ordinary use: "hoc judicium ex naturali, & infallibili Philosophiae fundamento profectum, aliis omnibus de metallorum inventione artibus merito est praeferendum." [Glauber, de Oper. Mineral. part 3, p. 20.]

10. Camerarius, listing several natural phenomena that can't easily be accounted for, says the hazel rod the mineralists use to find gold and silver mines is one of those miracles of nature that aren't easy to explain: "Ideo non absque causa mirum nonnullis videtur, quod multa passim occurrant, de quibus quantumvis Naturalia habeantur, solida ratio reddi nequeat. Sic non absque ratione quaeri potest, quare a solis Corylorum surculis bifidis, & non idem ab aliis arboribus, vel fruticibus quae in iisdem proveniunt locis, venae auri argentive feraces detegantur, inclinante sese eo virgula, qua sub terra venae feruntur atque latent." [Meditat. historic. ch. 73, p. 335.]

11. Matthias Willenus wrote in German a treatise on the Divining Rod titled de vera Virgulae Mercurialis Relatione, printed at Jena, a town of Germany, around 1672. He defends the rod's honor with all his strength. He proves that its dip over the metals is purely natural. He argues the effect doesn't come precisely from the wood, since a rod hung from a thread would move toward neither gold nor silver. It comes instead, he says, from the stars that presided at the person's birth, which strengthen or weaken his temperament in this respect. And to ground this, he appeals to the wholly divine harmony the astrologers say exists between heaven and earth, binding all the parts of the universe into one perfectly organized body.

12. Sylvester Rattray, doctor of medicine, in a little work on the different sympathies and antipathies observed among natural things — both by himself and by other physicists — devotes a special section to the sympathy of minerals with vegetables. There he says that the hazel rod, mounted like a compass needle, serves to discover veins of silver; that a rod of wild pine indicates lead mines; and that the olive and the palm point out gold and silver — as those affirm who make a profession of hunting treasure: "Coryli virga eodem modo continuata quo acus nautica venas argenti ... ut testantur thesaurorum occultorum investigatores." [Theatrum sympathetic. p. 24.] A man who refers these effects to nature, and to the occult causes of sympathy, is far from believing the demon their author.

13. The author of the book entitled The Revelation of the Divine Majesty spends the eleventh chapter of his fifth book on this question: whether hazel rods can be used without sin in the search for mines. This author, apparently a Brother of the Rosy Cross, named Eriditus Guetman, appears the most Christian man in the world, and the most outspoken against any practice that could be remotely suspected of superstition; and he rules that the hazel rod may be used in a thoroughly Christian way to seek springs, and the mines of gold and silver — provided no words, ceremonies, or enchantment are mixed in, and the whole is done in the fear, and under the eyes, of God.

14. Joannes Christianus Frommann, doctor of medicine, is perhaps, of everyone who has written on the Divining Rod, the one to whose judgment I'd most willingly defer. He spent years examining, with the most particular care, all the practices attributed — rightly or wrongly — to criminal magic. He went into thoroughly curious detail. His diligence reached so far that I don't believe anything of consequence escaped him, particularly regarding charms, sorcery, and enchantment. He wrote on it that great, curious work titled De fascinatione. In book III, part V, ch. 1, he reviews the Divining Rod in its turn. He lays out the different opinions, examines and weighs them with great judgment. He laughs at the simplicity of those who believe the demon moves it over the metals. He scorns the notion of certain people who, to play the clever ones, hold that the rod's movement is just a conjuring trick by a deft cheat. Finally he declares for the party of the physicists, who are persuaded the movement is a purely natural effect; though he admits it's one of those marvels of nature the human mind can't easily penetrate and unfold. The best I can do is let him speak for himself: it's only fair that everyone judge, by his own lights, the opinion of so great a philosopher.

This author, playing first on the words Virga and Virgo, says: "This Rod — this Virgin — whose power, so fine and so renowned, has enchanted so many people, has made herself enviers and slanderers as well as admirers. Some, toward whom she shows nothing but coldness, and whose embraces she seems to hold in horror, accuse her out of spite of being an organ and tool of Satan, having made at least an implicit pact with that malign spirit. Others say she's a cheat and a hypocrite, imposing on the eyes of the world by her tricks. And there are those who, doing her more justice, proclaim that her virtue is unpainted and wholly natural. Deusingius is one of those men annoyed because the rod doesn't move in everyone's hands. After testing it with two of his friends, for whom it turned very happily, he was carried away — because it would not turn for him — into declaring the movement came from the demon, as he tries to prove in his Exam. Pulver. sympath. p. 57. I admit frankly that the prayers, and certain superstitious ceremonies, that some people practice in cutting the rod have sometimes troubled me; but never enough to fall into the fantasy of those who think there's magic in it. I have never found anything that could justify such a judgment. But, some say, no one can demonstrate why this rod dips over the metals? Pitiful reasoning! I know well it's hard to explain this movement and this dip. But in the philosophy of the Schools, do we know any better how qualities arise from substantial forms? How, from the matter of one animal, another arranged and organized matter can be produced, from which another animal will form? Scaliger said long ago: You stumble over stones in your path constantly; tell me then, if you please, in what the form of a stone consists? One must not deny an effect just because one doesn't understand it. There are physical things whose physics cannot be untangled. I wouldn't deny that there may sometimes be trickery of the demon. He loves to mingle among natural things. He enters into the passions of lost men, and exploits the bad disposition of people consumed by greed, to lead them into superstition and into vain, criminal observances. But there is no harm at all in using the hazel rod to seek metals, provided one intends no bad end, and practices none of those useless, superstitious ceremonies with which wretches have tried to corrupt this so natural and so innocent use of the Divining Rod." "At metalla virgae è corylo detractae beneficio, sepositis superstitionum & observantiarum inutilium ineptiis, scrutari nec illicitum est, nec illicito sanae rationi repugnante fit medio." [pp. 688, 689, 690, 691.]

15. Libavius — whom Frommann calls a man illustrious for his piety as for his learning, and who was in fact a declared enemy of all superstition — states that the use of the Divining Rod contains nothing bad in itself; that he tried it himself for finding metals; that it's quite true the physicists don't see very clearly into these marvelous effects, above all why it doesn't turn in everyone's hands; but that this should be taken as an occasion for admiring the sovereign power of God. Here is how he explains himself: "There is no harm at all in using the Divining Rod. It is made of a forked branch of hazel, or of oak, a year old; and some believe it must be cut before sunrise, during the waxing of the moon, toward the Annunciation of the Virgin — that is, toward the spring equinox. For my part, I observe none of that. It is carried in the hands ...... And if the man holding the rod has silver buttons on his coat, it will turn toward his stomach. And if he has no silver on him at all, and some is hidden in the earth, it will dip over it, whatever effort he makes with his hands to prevent it. The physicists don't know why this happens. It is one of those sympathies of nature that must be admired. I have sometimes tried it myself, and always found the rod turning precisely over the hidden metals. I have seen the same done by several people with the same success. That not everyone has the disposition needed for this operation — who can be surprised at that?"

16. Father Schott the Jesuit, who at first took the opposite side, nevertheless, while composing what he set down on the hazel rod in the fourth part of his Natural Magic [bk. 1, syntag. 1, ch. 1, p. 420], received a letter from a man he describes as considerable for his virtue and his learning — "cujusdam viri probi ac docti" — whom he had consulted to learn what he thought of the Divining Rod. He had asked him how it must be chosen, at what time, with what circumstances; and whether there wasn't a little trickery on the part of those who make a trade of this little game. Here is the answer this man gave him — Father Schott valued his judgment so highly that he printed the letter entire in his Sympathetic Magic, p. 430:

"Your Reverence asks me something that is not the easiest thing in the world — not that I find it much labor to explain the powers I've observed in the hazel; it's that I know there are not a few learned men who openly take the field against me. Some claim the hazel rod turns by the effect of a spoiled imagination. There are those who play the freethinkers, and rule rather brusquely that it's a conjuring trick executed by a deft cheat, who gives the metallic rod its movement. Some have even gone so far as to say there was at least an implicit pact with the demon — which is why they wouldn't let me use this rod until I had first renounced all pact, until they had fastened blessed wax to the ends of the rod, and even pronounced exorcisms while it turned in my hands. These clamorings of the ignorant not only soured me on the use I made of it, but made me resolve to speak of it to no one any more. But since Your Reverence proceeds in good faith in the questions you put to me, I will answer in the same spirit, and tell you frankly what several most certain experiments have taught me. The thickness and size of the rod don't matter at all; and as I absolutely deny that one must observe season, year, or hour of day for cutting it, I have always laughed at those who load it with superstitious ceremonies. I have noticed, though, that hazel cut at the full moon had more force. For the rest, the rod is forked, and counted better when you find one forked almost from the root — which is why the mine workers call them eingrund ruhten. This rod indicates not only every kind of metal; some even hold it serves to discover springs flowing in the earth. That I haven't had occasion to test. Now, to learn what is hidden under the earth, in walls, or elsewhere, you need only put a piece of metal at the tip of the rod; and if it dips, its movement will show that what's hidden in the earth is a like metal. And a man who wanted to push the experiment further would even come to discover the quantity and quality of the treasure. For my part, what I'd do in such a case is put gold or silver in the hands holding the rod — for it must be held with two hands. Then I'd approach the place; and if iron is hidden there, it's certain that, with only copper in my hands, the rod will not stir; but if the treasure's metal and the metal in my hands match, the rod will turn violently over the spot. By an entirely similar experiment I can learn the quantity of the treasure — even how much money a man has in his pocket. For if I hold in my hands more silver than there is in the treasure, or in the purse, the rod will never turn. And when the sum I'm trying to learn is the greater, the rod will swing to it at once. These are secrets not everyone knows, yet so certain that if I set out to recount all the experiments I've made of them, I could fill several sheets of paper. You should know, moreover, that hazel is drawn toward hazel; take two little rods of that wood, in the required way, and you'll see them move toward each other. As to the age of the hazel-wood, I've always taken care to use wood of one year only — easily told by the knots on it. As to how to hold it in the hands, the figure I'm sending teaches you. Why didn't you mention, last Lent, the difficulties that trouble you on this subject? I'd have cleared them all away with pleasure, and demonstrated to you most plainly that the rod's effect is entirely natural. That said, I wouldn't claim one can never be deceived with this simple instrument. Is it the demon carrying the treasures elsewhere? That may well be. I'd rather say the hazel's sympathy isn't yet fully known to us. In any case, Your Reverence will find many learned men who can unfold all this infinitely better than I. What I could tell you far more easily is why this rod turns in some hands and lies motionless in others. For nothing prevents referring these different effects to the diversity of temperament found in the blood and the hands of those persons. No objection can stand against that answer. Enough on the hazel for this time; if you wish to inform yourself still further about the rod, command me — I am wholly yours." "Utinam mihi in Quadragesima praeterita vobis praesenti, verbulo saltem insinuasset, difficultates tunc plerasque enodassem, & naturalem esse virgulae metallicae effectum clare ostendissem!"

This judgment carries all the more weight in that it comes from a man who is plainly a philosopher, as the letter shows, and whom Father Schott declares a man of science, of virtue, and very experienced besides in the use of the Divining Rod, as he himself affirms. I half suspect he was a Jesuit. It appears Father Schott and he had spent the previous Lent together. And what seems to support my conjecture is that Father Schott, since this letter, has changed his opinion on the rod. He had suspected its movement of being the work of the demon, of chance, or of trickery by the user: "audacter pronuntio illam conversionem contingere casu, vel fraude virgulam tractantis, vel ope diaboli."
