[Magi. sympath. bk. 2, syntag. 4, ch. 1, p. 427.] Since then he has said, in his curious Physics, that he would not dare affirm generally that the rod's movement is the devil's work, "because," he adds, "I know of certain knowledge that religious men of very great piety use it with altogether marvelous success, and maintain with all their strength that this movement is entirely natural, and proceeds not at all from the skill, or the force of imagination, of the man who holds it": "Universaliter autem asserere non ausim, daemonem illum effectum praestare, quoniam certo mihi constat, viros Religiosos ac probissimos, experimentum non semel, & infallibili cum successu tentasse: Qui quidem mordicus defendunt, naturalem esse, nec fraudem ullam, aut ullam phantasiae emphasin intervenire." [Physic. curios. bk. 12, ch. 4, annotat. ad coroll. 1, p. 1269.] Which leads me to think the letter-writer may well be one of those religious of very great piety. In any case, we shouldn't pass over what Schott says here without noting that he retracts much of what he had written on the hazel rod in his earlier works, declaring now that he would no longer dare affirm the demon has any part in it. And what should make this correction the weightier for us is that this Jesuit, at the head of this twelfth book, after asking some indulgence for what may have escaped him through the fire of youth in his first books, gives notice that the annotations he has added are the fruit of long study, which led him to correct his first opinions and adopt surer, more reasonable ones: "sunt enim," he says, "posteriores curae prioribus saniores." [p. 1276.]

17. The sieur de Saint Romain, doctor of medicine, in a system of physics he composed and titled La science naturelle dégagée des chicanes de l'École [Natural science freed from the chicaneries of the School], explains the Divining Rod's dip by the movement of the atoms rising from above the springs and the mines. He writes like a good physicist, and comes very close to the mechanism nature follows in this surprising effect. "I draw," he says, "the natural cause of the movement of the rod of Aaron from the mineral or aquatic spirits issuing from the places where mines and waters lie, which, meeting the rod, whose pores are matched to their hooks, draw it as they return — by the perpendicular motion natural to them — and make it bend, as if they were threads of silk, or little chains of silver." [part 1, ch. 8, p. 42.]

18. Father Déchales the Jesuit, having examined with some care the movement of the Divining Rod — which turned, in the hands of a gentleman of his acquaintance, over springs and over metals — speaks of it this way: "Two things astonish me in this experiment," he says. "First, why does this rod turn only for certain people? And second, how can the same rod serve to discover both the springs and the mines? For indeed, having one day hidden silver in the ground on purpose, I was surprised to see a gentleman armed with this hazel rod find it without the least hesitation. He found springs with the same ease, and with such certainty that he traced on the ground the course of the stream beneath. He had besides certain signs from which he conjectured there was water at a spot. Once he had located the stream, having very good eyesight, he watched the vapors that rise above the sources, and by them he followed all the way to the head of the watercourse, which he always marked exactly. I confess I was at first so struck by such a spectacle that I believed it could only happen by virtue of a pact with the demon. But after thinking it over — having seen that no words are used, that there is nothing of the kind I imagined, and that besides, the hazel rod shows the springs at all times — I much prefer to pass no judgment on it. There are in nature so many effects whose causes we don't know that if we chose to hold suspect everything we don't understand, we'd have to stand stock-still; we can scarcely move a foot without instantly meeting something that exceeds the reach of our minds." [Dechales Mund. Mathemat. de fontib. natural. pp. 190 and 191.]

Here are the testimonies of two illustrious Doctors of the Sorbonne, approvers of the letters of M. Garnier and M. Chauvin, physicians of Lyon, who explain in a physical manner everything Aymar did with his rod.

19. Monsieur Cohade, first Custodian of Sainte Croix of Lyon, one of the approvers, who taught philosophy at Paris for so long and with such reputation, says in his approbation at the end of M. Chauvin's letter: "I am grateful to the author for not having had recourse, in explaining so singular a fact, to the implicit pact with the demon, to the villager's star, to the occult qualities; and for having put the corpuscles to work .... which moves me to give my approbation with praise." And in the one he gave to M. Garnier's letter, he says: "There are in nature three sorts of hidden truths ...... The third are hidden by their nature, but study clears them up, as with the magnet ...... The history of the rod is of this last kind. It has its tangles, its darknesses, and its difficulties, which can be removed and dispelled ..... I must even add that the curés, and the devout, whose only aim is the correction of morals — and generally everyone who lives in civil society — will be very glad to learn that an innocent, unsuspect art has been found for preventing, or stopping, thieves and murderers: this is what obliges me to approve this letter, in my capacity as old philosopher and theologian. At Lyon, this 17 November 1692."

20. Monsieur Basset, Obedientiary of S. Just of Lyon, in his approbation of M. Garnier's letter, says: "It unfolds a question equally curious and important concerning the particular talents of Jaques Aymar — not with words about qualities in general ...... but by natural, tangible reasoning, with much fidelity and discernment. This work is most useful for completing the disabusing of those whom lack of knowledge, or the stubbornness of upholding old ruined claims, may have led to run down what they don't understand, or what gives them umbrage, without following any rules. At Lyon, this 8 November 1692."

21. Monsieur Geoffroy the son has written a most curious dissertation on everything Jaques Aymar did at Lyon with his rod for the discovery of the murderer. He shows there, by what he draws from physics and mathematics, how much he profited from the fine, learned education given him by M. Geoffroy, former Alderman of Paris, his father. His system: 1. The streams of particles issuing from bodies work easily into the fibers of the rod and begin to give it its first push toward dipping. 2. These streams, rushing out of the rod, push aside slightly the parts of air beneath it; from which it follows that the pressure the other parts of air exert on the rod's upper side must necessarily make it dip. Which must work with the more force, he says, the longer the rod.

22. Monsieur Lamy, physician of Paris and a great physicist, was consulted in 1670 by M. Fortin, doctor of medicine living at Helleville near Cherbourg, about the rod, then making some noise on account of M. de Contrepont, for whom it turned over water. M. Lamy's reply shows he was far from believing the devil had any hand in it. He teases those who consulted the theologians on a question that belongs, he says, entirely to the jurisdiction of the philosophers; and he even affirms that these effects of the rod, which he so often wished to witness, can easily be explained by the principles of his physics, to which he refers his friend.

23. Monsieur de Saint-André, physician of Coutances, in a letter to M. Fortin, professor of the Collège d'Harcourt and brother of the doctor of medicine, prompted by M. Lamy's letters, gives his view of the rod's movement and dip, and explains the Lyon affair very neatly by the corpuscles, and by comparison with what a hunting dog does: "It is not surprising," he says, "that the imperceptible parts detached from the body of a thief or a murderer, striking in a certain way the organ of smell — or, if you like, another sense — of the dog or of the man, give the nerves and the spirits a certain agitation, which, communicating itself to the brain and the sensitive soul, excites there a particular commotion that carries the dog, or the man, toward the side the malefactor has gone ...... As for the springs, the mines, and the treasures, the particles ceaselessly exhaling from them likewise act on the organs of those who seek them, and on the pores and fibers of the rod."

After reporting the views of those who favor our opinion, plain honesty requires acknowledging that there are very able men of the contrary party, to whom the Divining Rod's operation appears anything but natural. In this rank we place the celebrated Father Kircher, Father Schott (though this last has much softened his opinion, as we've seen), and several other very learned people; but in truth it should also be known that they let themselves be somewhat taken in by the declamation of Georgius Agricola. For these learned philosophers all lay down principles, and admit experiments, so closely analogous to those of the Divining Rod that it's surprising they didn't see it's all the same thing — and how they could fall into the fantasy of Agricola, who, expert as he otherwise was in everything mineral, let himself be simply persuaded that the rod turns only by the force of the magic verses recited to enchant it before use. Hence his name for it: the enchanted rod, Virgula incantata. [bk. 2, de re metallica, pp. 27 and 28.] I don't know whether the people he watched really used enchantments, as he says. If they did, it was uselessly — and doubtless to hide their secret, for fear it should be seen how easy it is to do the same thing; as often happens with those who make great mysteries of things quite simple in themselves. What's certain is that all the verses of Homer's Iliad and Odyssey together would not make the rod turn in the hands of a man who lacks the temperament for it. It's a pity such able men tumbled headlong into Agricola's tale, which is incredible.

24. M. Gassendi had no hesitation in mocking this supposed enchantment of the rod as a childish fancy, unworthy of a physicist. This chapter should draw toward its close with the words of that great philosopher, who, balance in hand, weighs each opinion with prodigious discernment. He speaks here as a man who hasn't run the trials himself, relying on what is published: "If the alder-wood rod, hung in balance, dips over the spots where hidden watercourses lie, this comes from that wood — which greatly loves the waters — having loaded itself with the weight of the vapors rising from the springs. Whether the same happens with the hazel rod, called the Divining Rod, in the hands of those who seek springs and mines — that seems reasonably doubtful. But in any case, if such an effect occurs, I find Agricola altogether amusing for attributing its cause to the enchantment of certain verses." "Qui minus est mirum, si Agricola eventum, si quis fuerit, referendum censuerit ad carminum incantamenta." [Gassend. vol. 2, Physic. sect. 3, membr. 1, bk. 3, ch. 3, p. 167, de Plantis.] Agricola says only the lowliest mine workers, people without religion, use the Divining Rod to seek metals, while those with a little more education and Christianity rely on the time-honored indications. That's an outburst of Agricola's; for it's certain — Father Schott the Jesuit himself tells us — that not only the meanest mine workers, but also many people of most irreproachable life, use this rod to find the veins of metals, and even to find treasures and hidden money of every kind; which they practice, he adds, with success enough. Armed with this rod, they range through the houses, the stables, the gardens, and elsewhere, and turn up things no one would ever have expected: "Hac porro virgula Metallici, aliique non pauci etiam inculpatae vitae homines, non solum utuntur ad metallicas venas, sed etiam ad thesauros ...... & saepe quidem non sine effectu." [Thaumaturgus Physic. bk. 4, ch. 1, p. 422.]

25. I can close this chapter no better than with the testimony of M. l'Abbé Gallet, Grand Penitentiary of the Church of Carpentras. The rank he holds in the Church, and the standing his deep knowledge of physics and mathematics has earned him among the learned, must give his opinion on the rod very great weight. But what raises the worth of his testimony higher still is that the rod turns in his own hands, as I learned from a person who had witnessed the experiment. This made me want this learned man's judgment on the present question — namely, whether the rod's dip is a conjuring trick, or something the demon could have a part in. One of his friends wrote to him about it, and he has been kind enough to send us an excellent Latin discourse, which I set down here entire, so that so curious a piece may not be lost. Monsieur l'Abbé Gallet declares in his paper that the rod turns for him over water and metals; that he has used it several times with admirable success to find watercourses and hidden money; and that he is far removed from the opinion of those who say there's knavery or the demon in it. As to the cause of the movement, he attributes it to the vapors exhaling from the waters, which the rod sucks in, as it once did in the earth for its growth. He's persuaded it's the weight of these vapors that makes it dip. He says the sanguine and the phlegmatic — to whom the stars at birth gave much humidity — are fitter for the rod's operations than men of choleric or melancholic temperament, the latter being too dry. It's on this basis that M. l'Abbé Gallet, having calculated the horoscope of Jaques Aymar Vernai, conjectures that he is of phlegmatic temperament: his Ascendant, Cancer, an aqueous sign in which the Moon sits in her own nocturnal house, is favorably regarded by a partile trine aspect of Jupiter. Moreover Pisces, another aqueous sign, occupied the midheaven at the moment of Aymar's birth. All of which must make humidity dominate in his temperament, give him soft flesh, and pores wide and open — and so a constitution fit to be highly sensitive to the impressions of the particles spread in the air. He notes further that, by the rules of the astrologers, Aymar, having the Sun in the 4th house, where the domicile and exaltation of Mercury are found, must find hidden things more easily than anyone. Finally, Monsieur l'Abbé Gallet, after submitting everything he says to the decisions of the Church, supplies the horoscopic figure of Jaques Aymar, which will be found here following his Latin discourse — so that the public can't reproach me with wanting to profit alone from this learned man's study.

ON THE ALTOGETHER ADMIRABLE EFFECT of the Divine Rod, by whose help Jacques Aymar Vernai, of Dauphiné, found a murderer at a great distance.

[The discourse, here translated from the Latin:] Though I have exceedingly admired the effect of the Divining Rod, by whose help the murderer of Lyon was hunted down and exposed by a certain man, Jacques Aymar Vernai of Saint-Véran in Dauphiné — and the more so because, from the account of my most distinguished friend Monsieur Panthot, Dean of the College of Physicians of Lyon, I have noted moral effects mixed with the physical — nevertheless I would dare ascribe that effect neither to imposture nor to incantation, as do Agricola (de re metal. bk. 2), Roberti (in Goclenii Heautontimorumenos, sect. 16, fol. 380 of the theatrum sympatheticum), and several others it would be superfluous to list; but rather to a physical cause hitherto unknown, whose elucidation seems to have been reserved to the learned of our own time. The authors named, little versed in natural things, judged this man-finding rod just as they judged the rod in metal-finding and water-finding. Yet it is absolutely certain that a forked hazel branch, as sketched here in the margin, indicates both metal veins and underground waters, with a trembling movement besides, plainly felt by those who carry it, balanced as it were in both hands. I have many times tested this branch's power in finding water, with the desired success; and once or twice, by its help, I detected hidden silver by happy chance, to the great astonishment of the bystanders. Nor have I used only hazel, but branches of any other tree at hand — elm, alder, mulberry, wild olive, and others — when seeking water; though it's true the hazel and the alder produced a more perceptible movement, their fibers running more lengthwise, and so being fitter to receive the watery vapors that impress the movement.

But to explain the cause of that movement by physical reasons, as I have conceived it, certain perfectly evident things must first be assumed. 1. From the places where underground springs are enclosed, vapors are continually driven upward — whether by the pressure of the air above, or by the underground fires, or by both together — vapors which, especially at sunrise, register on the senses, as may be seen in Vitruvius, bk. 8 of the architecture, ch. 1, on finding hidden waters; and these vapors climb straight up, following the disposition of the fibers of the terrestrial globe. 2. The forked branch, by the part A, where it stood nearer the trunk of the tree, passes the nourishment it receives to the upper parts B and C, by the pull of the sap sent up from the roots, which by native disposition transform those earth-and-water vapors, rising upward, into their proper juice. 3. The bark of the part A, standing in for the roots, by natural appetite sucks and draws to itself, for its own preservation, the vapors flowing from the earth that surround it when it is carried into the places they rise from abundantly; those drawn-in vapors enter within the bark and pour in headlong; and from that continual influx, the part A — held raised, against its natural position, in a kind of balance by the two hands gripping the prongs — is compelled, by the directive force of the vapors, to tip downward; and then the hands, plainly registering that trembling movement, feel the part A sinking toward the ground, by the addition of a certain introduced heaviness, as Kircher explains [de art. magnet. bk. 3, part 5, ch. 3, section on the magnetism of the gold-bearing rod].

The reason not all men perceive such a movement is sought in the diversity of bodily temperament, proceeding from the situation of the stars at the time of birth: the sanguine and the phlegmatic, the more they abound in humidity, the better they perceive the movement; but the choleric and the melancholic, by their excessive dryness, scatter the surrounding vapors with their own emanation — a dry one — and interrupt the movement of the vapors climbing perpendicularly; and so, the directive force of the vapors being broken, it can neither impress its movement on the forked branch, nor can the drawn vapors flow into it.

By the same reasoning that clears this water-finding rod of all suspicion of magic in the experiment of finding water, the man-finding rod in the Lyon experiment can also be wholly cleared of the same slander. For it is certain that, just as vapors and earthy exhalations rise from underground waters, so from the human body certain most tenuous corporeal effluvia continually stream out — and the more, when the body is shaken by passions or by violent movement. These effluvia, pouring most copiously from the blood of the slain man, enter the passages of the killer's body and, chained as it were with his blood and spirits, flow back, thus mingled, from the killer, returning by a reciprocal and continual movement to the place they came from, along the very road the killer took; and conversely the killer's effluvia, with which the corpse or its blood was soaked, mingled with those of the slain, return to the killer. From this is explained the working of the sympathetic powder, and why the blood of the slain flows in the killer's presence: by the nearness of slayer and slain, the movement of the intermixed effluvia grows more vehement, dissolving the blood, though clotted, and stirring it to flow. It also explains why the spirit of human blood, kept in a glass, shows the health or the diseases of the man it was drawn from, though he is far away; and at his death the glass shatters.

This laid down, it is probable that the Divining Rod which exposed the Lyon murderer, carried to the place where blood was shed by the homicide, was soaked with the corpuscles flowed from the blood, mingled with the killer's effluvia; its fibers were fitted for receiving and drawing in the matching parts; and so the man, carrying the rod before him in his hands, sucked in those effluvia along with it, felt the movement their entry produced, and followed the road along which his hands were perceptibly affected by it. And since, from Monsieur Panthot's account, it is established that this man suffered very many passions of the soul, even to fainting, above all at the scene of the homicide — that is a sign he is of a bodily temperament most apt for the copious emission and reception of these effluvia, which cannot happen without passion.

If a conjecture may be drawn about the said Vernai's temperament from his age of 30 years, and the day of his birth as reported by Monsieur Panthot — namely the 7th of September, the night verging on the 8th — and from the disposition of the heavens at his birth, fixed, as to the hour, by the closer agreement of the heavens with the native's qualities: it is probable that his temperament is altogether phlegmatic. For the ascendant is an aqueous sign, the Moon placed in it with dignity, and Jupiter, also in an aqueous sign, illuminating the horoscope by a partile trine aspect; the midheaven likewise an aqueous sign — all of which denotes that an excessive humidity prevails in the constitution of his body; and so, having looser pores, he is endowed with a marvelous aptitude for emitting and receiving the effluvia described above. To which may be added — saving submission to the decrees of the Church, which I profess to observe — that the Sun's position in the fourth house, mutually received in the domicile of Mercury, most strongly inclines him toward, and favors him in, the finding of treasures and of things more deeply hidden. More could be deduced; but this will suffice for the intelligent — had leisure permitted, I would have answered the various doubts that can be raised. I hope, meanwhile, that in our age — when the natural sciences, under the protection of our King, are cultivated to their depths and weighed in the first rank with unwearied diligence — the abstruse, hidden things hitherto ascribed to occult qualities or to sympathy will at last be proved wholly natural and physical, happening by the application of actives to passives, with no possibility of action at a distance; and that such effects are neither superstitious, nor magical, nor drawn from pacts with demons; for such evasions may be called the asylum of the ignorant. And so, saving the decisions of the Church, to which he wishes all this submitted, and saving any more probable or sounder judgment, so rules the undersigned, at Carpentras, this fifth day of January 1693. GALLET.

[The original here contains the engraved Horoscopic figure: "Nativité de Jaques Aymar."]

Whoever has read this work with some attention will, if I'm not mistaken, be convinced of five things.

1. That, though the number of people for whom the rod turns is small, there are nevertheless certainly several who must be believed to have this faculty; it would be a kind of madness to set oneself down as calling honorable men liars about what they attest — above all when they have no interest in telling us they have this gift.

2. That the movement and the dip of the rod happen as naturally as the movement and dip of the magnetized iron rod.

3. That even if my system didn't answer every difficulty — which won't prove to be the case, I hope — no one would thereby gain the right to credit this effect to the demon, any more than the countless others the philosophers cannot account for.

4. That since the use of the rod involves no symbols, no figures, no words, no ceremonies, no empty observances, there can be in it, according to all the theologians, neither superstition nor pact, explicit or implicit; though the delicate sensitivity required to be moved by the impressions of the particles spread in the air — and the extreme attention required to listen to oneself, to feel oneself, to recognize one's own agitation, and to steer by this criterion — are apology enough for those who use the rod. For it must never be forgotten that, since it turns over every place where vapors hang thick, forming a cloud and an atmosphere, one can never say whether it's turning precisely for the thing one is seeking. And this very fact proves beyond answer that there is no pact or bargain with the demon in the practice: otherwise more people would have the talent, and those who have it would be more certain than they are of not being misled.

5. Finally, that we ought to cherish those whose temperament fits them for this divination — after the example of the great Cassiodorus, who honored with his protection a water-seeker brought expressly from Africa to Rome, as I related on page 467; for it cannot be denied that such people are most useful to human society.

THE END.

Extract from the King's Privilege.

By Letters Patent of the King, given at Versailles the 20th of November 1691, signed Gamart, and sealed with the great Seal of yellow wax, it is permitted to the Sieur de Vallemont, Priest and Doctor of Theology, to have printed a Book he has composed, and which is entitled, La Physique Occulte, ou traité de la Baguette de Coudrier, and that during the time and space of six consecutive years, to count from the day when the said Book shall have been finished printing. With prohibitions, etc. The said Sieur de Vallemont has ceded the above Privilege to the Sieur Jean Anisson, Director of the Royal Printing-house.

Registered in the Book of the Community of Booksellers and Printers of Paris, the 6th of March 1693.

Finished printing for the first time, the 21st of March 1693.

[End of Chapter Seventeen — End of the Book]
